Greek (Kühn)
9.1.1 Περὶ τῶν γῶν. [α΄. Περὶ τῶν τῆς γῆς διαφορῶν.] Δυοῖν σημαινομένων πραγμάτων ἐκ τοῦ τῆς γῆς ὀνόματος ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστιν διαστεῖλαι πρότερον αὐτὰ σαφηνείας ἕνεκα τῶν λεχθησομένων. τὸ μὲν οὖν ἕτερον αὐτῶν σύνηθές ἐστιν ἅπασι τοῖς Ἕλλησιν. ὅταν τε γὰρ σπείρουσιν πυροὺς ἢ κριθὰς ἤ τι τῶν ἄλλων σπερμάτων, ἃ δημήτρια καλεῖται, μετρίως ὑγρὰν εἶναι χρῆναί φασι τὴν γῆν. ὅταν τε φυτεύουσιν ἀμπέλους ἢ συκὰς ἢ ἐλαίας ἤ τι τῶν ἄλλων δένδρων, φυλάττεσθαι κελεύουσι τήν τ’ ἄνικμον ἐσχάτως καὶ ξηρὰν τήν θ’ ὑγρὰν καὶ πηλώδη. καὶ γὰρ οὖν ὀνομάζουσι πηλὸν τὴν γῆν ὑγρῷ φυραθεῖσαν, οἵ τε τὰ γεωργικὰ γράψαντες ἐν ταῖς τῶν χωρίων διαφοραῖς τά τε μελάγγαια καὶ ἀργιλώδη καὶ ψαμμώδη διαστελλόμενα λέγουσιν. καλοῦσι δὲ καὶ λιπαρὰν γῆν, ἐξ ἧς ὁ πηλὸς γίνεται γλίσχρος. ἑτέραν τε κατὰ τοὐναντίον τῇδε διακειμένην, ἀλιπῆ καὶ ψαθυρὸν, ὡς ἂν εἴποι τις, ἐργαζομένη τὸν πηλόν. ἓν μὲν δὴ τοῦτο σημαινόμενόν ἐστι τοῦ τῆς γῆς ὀνόματος ἅπασι σύνηθες. ἕτερον δὲ μόνον τοῖς φιλοσόφοις, ἐπειδὴ λέγωσι τῶν σωμάτων στοιχεῖα γῆν καὶ ὕδωρ καὶ ἀέρα καὶ πῦρ. τὸ γὰρ ξηρὸν ἐσχάτως σῶμα καὶ ψυχρὸν ὀνομάζουσι γῆν. κατὰ τούτους οὖν οὐδὲν μὲν τῶν συνθέτων τούτων σωμάτων ἐστὶν ἡ στοιχειώδης γῆ, πλεῖστον μέντοι τῆς γῆς ἔχειν αὐτά φασιν, οἷον τόν τε ἀδάμαντα καὶ τὰς πέτρας, καὶ ὅσῳ γ’ ἂν ὦσι σκληρότερα τὸ σῶμα, τοσούτῳ μᾶλλον αὐτὰ εἶναί φασι γεωδέστερα ἔμπαλιν τοῖς γεωργοῖς. οὐ γὰρ τὴν σκληροτέραν σύστασιν τῶν σωμάτων ὀνομάζουσιν οὗτοι γεωδεστέραν, ἀλλὰ λίθους μὲν καὶ πέτρας τὰ τοιαῦτα καλοῦσιν ἀνεπιτηδειότατα πρὸς γεωργίαν. ἀκριβεστάτην δὲ γῆν εἶναί φασι τὴν ποῤῥωτάτην τῇ συ στάσει τῆς πέτρας, ὅπου γε καὶ τὴν ἀργιλώδη τε καὶ τὴν ψαμμώδη μέμφονται πρὸς τὰ πλεῖστα. κατὰ μὲν οὖν τὸ παρὰ τοῖς φιλοσόφοις σημαινόμενον αἱ τῆς γῆς διαφοραὶ τρισὶν ὁρισθήσονται γένεσιν. ἔστι γὰρ τὸ μέν τι λίθος αὐτῆς, τὸ δὲ μεταλλευτόν τι σῶμα, τὸ δὲ τρίτον ἡ γεωργουμένη γῆ, διαφωνίας γεγονυίας παρ’ αὐτοῖς περὶ τῶν χεομένων μεταλλευτῶν σωμάτων, οἷον χαλκοῦ καὶ κασσιτέρου καὶ μολύβδου. ταῦτα γὰρ ἔνιοί φασιν οὐ γῆς, ἀλλ’ ὕδατος ἔχειν τὸ πλέον. οἵ γε μὴν ἄλλοι πάντες ἄνθρωποι γῆν ὀνομάζουσιν ἐκείνην μόνην τὴν οὐσίαν, ἥτις ὑγρῷ φυραθεῖσα πηλὸς γίνεται. περὶ ἧς κᾀγὼ νῦν ἔγνωκα τὰς διαφορὰς εἰπεῖν, ἐκεῖνο μόνον ἔτι προσθεὶς, ὡς ἡ προειρημένη τομὴ τῶν γεωδῶν σωμάτων εἴς τε λίθους καὶ τὰ μεταλλευόμενα καὶ τὴν γεωργουμένην γῆν ἄνευ τῶν φυσικῶν ἰδίως ὀνομαζομένων σωμάτων εἴρηται. τούτων δὲ προσιόντων καὶ τὰ ξύλα πάντα καὶ καρπῶν μόρια πολλὰ, καθάπερ καὶ ζώων, ὀνομασθήσεται γεώδη τὴν οὐσίαν εἶναι. καρπῶν μὲν μόρια, πυρῆνες ἐλαιῶν καὶ γίγαρτα σταφυλῶν καὶ καρύων λέμματα καὶ κώνων, ἕτερά τε πολλὰ τοιαῦτα, τῶν ζώων δὲ τά τ’ ὀστέα καὶ τὰ κέρατα καὶ οἱ ὀδόντες. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τῶν ἐν τοῖς φυτοῖς μορίων ὅσα γεώδη καὶ σκληρὰ, τὸ μέν πού τι καὶ πρόσθεν εἴρηται, τὸ δ’ ἂν καὶ νῦν ῥηθείη· περὶ δὲ τῶν ἐν τοῖς ζώοις ἐφεξῆς εἰρήσεται, πρότερον διελθόντος μου τὰ κατὰ τὴν γῆν εἴδη. καλεῖν μὲν γὰρ ἔξεστί σοι καὶ διαφορὰς αὐτὰ καὶ εἴδη καὶ γένη. λέγω δὴ ταῦτα λίθους τε καὶ τὰ μεταλλευτὰ σώματα, καὶ τὴν εἰς πηλὸν λυομένην γῆν, καὶ πρῶτόν γε τὰς διαφορὰς ἐρῶ τῆς ὑπὸ πάντων Ἑλλήνων ὀνομαζομένης γῆς, ἥτις ἔχει κοινὸν ὕδατι πλησιάσασα, διαλύεσθαι παραχρῆμα καὶ πηλὸν γίνεσθαι. τὸ γὰρ τῆς παρὰ τοῖς φιλοσόφοις οὕτω καλούμενον οὐ κοινὸν ἅπασιν, ἀλλ’ ἐκείνοις μόνοις σύνηθες ἔχει τοὶ σημαινόμενον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χεομένων, τοὶ σημαινόμενον, φυσικῶν ἰδίως ὀνομαζομένων σωμάτων
9.1.1 On earths. [1. On the differences of earth.] Since two things are signified by the name 'earth', it is necessary first to distinguish them for the sake of clarity in what is to be said. One of them is familiar to all the Greeks. For both when they sow wheat or barley or any of the other seeds which are called Demetrian, they say the earth must be moderately moist; and when they plant vines or fig-trees or olives or any of the other trees, they bid one guard against both the earth that is extremely devoid of moisture and dry, and that which is moist and muddy. For indeed they call earth that has been kneaded with liquid 'mud', and those who have written on agriculture, in the differences of lands, speak of black-soiled, clayey and sandy as distinguished. They also call 'oily' the earth from which the mud becomes viscous, and another, disposed in the opposite way to this, non-oily and producing, as one might say, a crumbly mud. This, then, is one signification of the name 'earth', familiar to all. The other belongs to the philosophers alone, since they say that the elements of bodies are earth and water and air and fire. For they call the body that is extremely dry and cold 'earth'. According to them, then, none of these composite bodies is the elemental earth, yet they say that they have the greatest share of earth, such as adamant and rocks, and the harder they are in body, the more earthy they say they are — the reverse of the farmers. For the latter do not call the harder consistency of bodies more earthy, but call such things stones and rocks, most unsuitable for farming; and they say that the most exact earth is that which is furthest in consistency from rock, seeing that they even find fault with the clayey and the sandy for most purposes. According to the signification among the philosophers, then, the differences of earth will be defined by three genera: for one part of it is stone, another a mined body, and the third the cultivated earth — a disagreement having arisen among them concerning the mined bodies that are poured (fusible), such as copper and tin and lead; for some people say that these have the greater share not of earth but of water. All other men, however, call 'earth' only that substance which, when kneaded with liquid, becomes mud. It is about this that I too have now resolved to state the differences, adding only this further, that the aforesaid division of earthy bodies into stones, mined things and cultivated earth has been stated apart from the bodies that are properly called 'natural'. If these are added, all woods too, and many parts of fruits, as also of animals, will be named earthy in substance: parts of fruits — the stones of olives and the pips of grapes and the shells of nuts and of pine-cones, and many others of this kind; of animals, the bones and the horns and the teeth. But concerning the parts in plants that are earthy and hard, something has been said before, and something may be said now as well; concerning those in animals it will be said next in order, after I have first gone through the kinds of earth. For you may call them both differences and kinds and genera. I mean by these stones and the mined bodies and the earth that dissolves into mud; and first I shall state the differences of the earth so named by all the Greeks, which has in common that, on coming near water, it dissolves immediately and becomes mud. For that which is so called among the philosophers has its signification not common to all, but familiar to those alone.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ψαθυρὸν ... ἐργαζομένη (syntax awkward; taken as 'producing a friable mud'), χεομένων (taken as 'fused/fusible'), τῶν φυσικῶν ἰδίως ὀνομαζομένων σωμάτων (sense of 'natural' bodies, i.e. organic, uncertain), τὸ γὰρ τῆς παρὰ τοῖς φιλοσόφοις οὕτω καλούμενον (τῆς probably for τῆς γῆς; text likely corrupt), τοὶ (perhaps τοι or τὸ)
9.1.1 On earths. [1. On the differences of earth.] Since two things are signified by the name 'earth', it is necessary first to distinguish them, for the sake of clarity in what is to be said. One of them is familiar to all the Greeks. For when they sow wheat or barley or any other of the seeds that are called 'Demetrian', they say that the earth must be moderately moist; and when they plant vines or fig trees or olives or any other trees, they bid one guard against earth that is extremely without moisture and dry, and against earth that is moist and muddy. For indeed they call earth kneaded with liquid 'mud', and those who have written on agriculture, in treating the differences of lands, speak of black-earth, clayey and sandy lands as distinguished from one another. They also call 'oily' the earth from which the mud becomes viscous, and another, disposed in the contrary way to this, non-oily, producing a mud that is, as one might say, friable. This, then, is one meaning of the name 'earth', familiar to all. The other is familiar only to the philosophers, since they say that the elements of bodies are earth, water, air and fire. For they call the extremely dry and cold body 'earth'. According to these men, then, none of these composite bodies is elemental earth, yet they say that they contain a very great deal of earth, as for example adamant and rocks; and the harder they are in body, the more earthy they say they are, the opposite of the farmers. For the latter do not call the harder consistency of bodies more earthy; rather, they call such things stones and rocks, most unsuitable for farming, and they say that the most exact earth is that which is furthest in consistency from rock, inasmuch as they find fault even with the clayey and the sandy for most purposes. According to the meaning current among the philosophers, then, the differences of earth will be defined by three genera. For one kind of it is stone, another is a mined body, and the third is the farmed earth, there having arisen disagreement among them concerning the mined bodies that are fused, such as copper, tin and lead. For some people say that these contain more not of earth but of water. All other men, however, call 'earth' only that substance which, kneaded with liquid, becomes mud. It is of this that I too have now decided to state the differences, adding only this further point: that the aforementioned division of earthy bodies into stones, mined things and farmed earth was stated without the bodies that are properly called 'natural'. When these are added, all woods too, and many parts of fruits, as also of animals, will be said to be earthy in substance: parts of fruits, olive stones, grape pips, and the husks of nuts and of pine cones, and many others of this kind; of animals, the bones, the horns and the teeth. But concerning the parts in plants that are earthy and hard, something has been said somewhere before, and something may also be said now; concerning those in animals it will be said next in order, after I have first gone through the kinds of earth. For you are at liberty to call them differences, or kinds, or genera. By these I mean stones, mined bodies, and the earth that dissolves into mud. And first I shall state the differences of the earth so named by all the Greeks, which has this in common: on coming near water it dissolves at once and becomes mud. For what is so called among the philosophers has a meaning not common to all, but familiar to them alone.

Greek (Kühn)
9.1.2 [β΄. Περὶ τῶν διαφορῶν τῆς συνήθως ὀνομαζομένης γῆς.] Γῆν ἔφην ὀνομάζεσθαι σύνηθες ἅπασιν Ἕλλησιν, ἥτις ἂν εἰς τὸ ὑγρὸν ἐμβληθεῖσα παραχρῆμα διαλύεταί τε καὶ πηλὸς γίνεται. ταύτης οὖν ἡ μέν τίς ἐστιν ἣν γεωργοῦσιν οἱ ἄνθρωποι, διαφορὰς ἔχουσά τινας μὲν κατὰ τὸν ἴδιον λόγον ἐν τῷ λιπαρά τε εἶναι καὶ γλίσχρος, ἥτις πάντως ἐστὶ καὶ μέλαινα τὴν χρόαν. ἡ δὲ ψαθυρωτέρα τε καὶ ἀλιπὴς, ἣν καλοῦσιν ἄργιλον, οὖσα καὶ ἥδε λευκοτέρα πως. ἐναντιώταται μὲν αὗται διαφοραί· αἱ δ’ ἄλλαι μεταξὺ τούτων, ἤτοι τῇ ἑτέρᾳ πλησιάζουσαι μᾶλλον ἢ τῇ ἑτέρᾳ, τινὲς δὲ κᾀν τῷ ﻿ μέσῳ δοκεῖν ἀκριβῶς εἶναι, τὴν ἴσην ἀμφοῖν ἀφεστηκυῖαι διάστασιν. αἱ δ’ ἐξ ἐπιμιξίας ἑτερογενῶν σωμάτων διαφοραὶ τῆς γῆς εἰσι, καθὸ λιθώδεις τε καὶ ψαμμώδεις ὑπάρχουσιν, καὶ χωρίζουσί γε τῶν τοιούτων τὴν μεμιγμένην οὐσίαν, ἀναδεύσαντες καὶ ἀναφυράσαντες ὕδατι πολλῷ, μέχρι τοῦ πᾶν ὑγρὸν ἐργάσασθαι. καθισταμένου γὰρ τούτου τὸ μὲν τῆς λιθώδους τε καὶ ψαμμώδους ἐμφερόμενον ὑφιζάνει πᾶν, ἐποχεῖται δὲ ἡ ἀκριβὴς γῆ. τοιοῦτον γάρ τι καὶ κατὰ τὴν Λημνίαν γίνεται γῆν, ἣν μίλτον ὀνομάζουσιν ἔνιοι Λημνίαν καί τινες ἄλλοι σφραγῖδα Λημνίαν, διὰ τὴν ἐπιβαλλομένην αὐτῇ σφραγῖδα τῆς Ἀρτέμιδος ἱεράν. ταύτην γάρ τοι τὴν γῆν ἡ ἱέρεια λαμβάνουσα μετά τινος ἐπιχωρίου τιμῆς, οὐ ζώων θυομένων, ἀλλὰ πυρῶν καὶ κριθῶν ἀντιδιδομένων τῷ χωρίῳ, κομίζει μὲν εἰς τὴν πόλιν ἀναφυράσασα ὕδατι καὶ πηλὸν ὑγρὸν ἐργασαμένη καὶ τοῦτον ταράξασα σφοδρῶς, εἶτ’ ἐάσασα καταστῆναι, πρῶτον μὲν ἀφαιρεῖ τὸ ἐπιπολῆς ὕδωρ, εἶθ’ ὑπ’ αὐτῷ τὸ λιπαρὸν τῆς γῆς λαβοῦσα καὶ μόνον ἀπολιποῦσα τὸ ὑφιζηκὸς λιθῶδές τε καὶ ψαμμῶδες, ὅπερ καὶ ἄχρηστόν ἐστιν, ἄχρι τοσούτου ξηραίνει τὸν λιπαρὸν πηλὸν, ἄχρις ἂν εἰς σύστασιν ἀφίκηται μαλακοῦ κηροῦ, καὶ τούτου λαμβάνουσα μόρια σμικρὰ τὴν ἱερὰν τῆς Ἀρτέμιδος ἐπιβάλλει σφραγῖδα, κἄπειτα πάλιν ἐν σκιᾷ ξηραίνει, μέχρις ἂν ἀκριβῶς ἄνικμος ἀποτελεσθῇ, καὶ γένηται τοῦτο δὴ τὸ γινωσκόμενον ἰατροῖς ἅπασι φάρμακον ἡ Λημνία σφραγίς. οὕτω γὰρ αὐτὴν ὀνομάζουσιν, ὡς ἔφην, ἔνιοι διὰ τὴν ἐπιβαλλομένην αὐτῇ σφραγῖδα, καθάπερ γε καὶ διὰ τὴν χρόαν ἔνιοι Λημνίαν μίλτον. ἔχει μὲν οὖν τὴν χρόαν τὴν αὐτὴν τῇ μίλτῳ, διαφέρει δ’ αὐτῆς τῷ μὴ μολύνειν ἁπτομένην, καθάπερ ἐκείνην, καὶ κατά γε τὸν λόφον ἐν τῇ Λήμνῳ τὸν ὅλον ὄντα κιῤῥὸν τῇ χρόᾳ, καθ’ ὃν οὔτε δένδρον ἐστὶν οὔτε πέτρα οὔτε φυτὸν, μόνη δ’ ἡ τοιαύτη γῆ. τρεῖς δ’ αὐτῆς εὑρίσκονται διαφοραί· μία μὲν ἡ προειρημένη τῆς ἱερᾶς γῆς, ἧς οὐδεὶς ἄλλος ἅπτεται πλὴν τῆς ἱερείας. δευτέρα δ’ ἑτέρα τῆς ὄντως μίλτου, χρῶνται δ’ οἱ τέκτονες αὐτῇ μάλιστα. τρίτη δ’ ἡ τῆς ῥυπούσης, ᾗ χρῶνται τῶν πλυνόντων ὀθόνας τε καὶ ἐσθῆτας οἱ βουληθέντες. ἀνεγνωκὼς δὲ ἐγὼ παρά τε Διοσκορίδῃ καὶ ἄλλοις τισὶ μίγνυσθαι τράγειον αἷμα τῇ Λημνίᾳ γῇ, κᾀκ τοῦ διὰ μίξεως ταύτης γενομένου πηλοῦ τὴν ἱέρειαν ἀναπλάττειν τε καὶ σφραγίζειν ἃς ὀνομάζουσι Λημνίας σφραγῖδας, ὠρέχθην αὐτὸς ἱστορῆσαι τὴν συμμετρίαν τῆς μίξεως. ὥσπερ οὖν εἰς Κύπρον ἕνεκα τῶν ἐν αὐτῇ μετάλλων, εἴς τε τὴν κοίλην Συρίαν, μόριον οὖσαν τῆς Παλαιστίνης, ἕνεκεν ἀσφάλτου καί τινων ἄλλων κατ’ αὐτὴν ἀξίων ἱστορίας ἐπορεύθην, οὕτως καὶ εἰς Λῆμνον οὐκ ὤκνησα πλεῦσαι, θεασόμενος ὁπόσον μίγνυται τοῦ αἵματος τῇ γῇ. καὶ τό γε δεύτερον ἐξ Ἀσίας εἰς Ῥώμην ἀφικέσθαι πεζῇ πορευόμενος διὰ Θρᾴκης τε καὶ Μακεδονίας, ἔπλευσα πρότερον ἀπὸ Τρῳάδος Ἀλεξανδρείας εἰς Λῆμνον, ἐπιτυχὼν εἰς Θεσσαλονίκην ἀναγομένου πλοίου, συνθεμένῳ τῷ ναυκλήρῳ παραβάλλειν πρότερον τῇ Λήμνῳ· ὁ δὲ προσέσχε μὲν, οὐ μὴν ᾗ γ’ ἐχρῆν πόλει. τὴν ἀρχὴν γὰρ οὐδ’ ἠπιστάμην δύο πόλεις εἶναι κατὰ τὴν νῆσον, ἀλλὰ ᾤμην, ὡς Σάμον καὶ Χίον καὶ Κῶ Ἄνδρον τε καὶ Τῆνον καὶ πάσας τὰς κατὰ τὸ Αἰγαῖον, οὕτω καὶ τὴν Λῆμνον ὁμώνυμον ἔχειν πόλιν ὅλῃ τῇ νήσῳ μίαν. ὡς δὲ ἀποβὰς τῆς νεὼς, ἔγνων Μυρίναν μὲν ὀνομάζεσθαι τὴν πόλιν, εἶναι δ’ οὔτε κατὰ Φιλοκτήτην οὔτε κατὰ τὸ ἱερὸν τοῦ Ἡφαίστου λόφον ἐν τῇ χώρᾳ τῆς πόλεως ἐκείνης, ἀλλ’ ἐν ἑτέρᾳ τῇ Ἡφαιστιάδι καλουμένῃ, καὶ οὐδ’ ἐγγὺς εἶναι τὴν πόλιν ἐκείνην τῆς Μυρίνης, ὅ τε ναύκληρος οὐκ ἠδύνατό με περιμένειν, ἀνεβαλλόμην εἰσαῦθις, ὅταν ἀπὸ Ῥώμης εἰς Ἀσίαν ἀφίκωμαι, θεάσασθαι τὴν Ἡφαιστιάδα. καί μοι τοῦτ’ ἐπράχθη, καθάπερ ἤλπισά τε καὶ προὐθέμην. ὡς γὰρ ἀπὸ τῆς Ἰταλίας διαβαλὼν εἰς τὴν Μακεδονίαν καὶ σχεδὸν ὅλην αὐτὴν ὁδοιπορήσας ἐν Φιλίπποις ἐγενόμην, ἥπερ ἐστὶν ὅμορος τῇ Θρᾴκῃ πόλις, ἐντεῦθεν ἐπὶ τὴν πλησίον θάλατταν εἴκοσιν ἐπὶ τοῖς ἑκατὸν ἀπέχουσαν στάδια κατελθὼν, ἔπλευσα πρότερον μὲν εἰς Θάσον ἐγγύς που διακοσίους σταδίους, ἐκεῖθεν δὲ εἰς Λῆμνον ἑπτακοσίους, εἶτ’ αὖθις ἀπὸ Λήμνου τοὺς ἴσους ἑπτακοσίους εἰς Ἀλεξανδρείαν Τρῳάδα. καὶ διὰ τοῦτ’ ἐξεπίτηδες ἔγραψα περί τε τοῦ πλοῦ καὶ τῶν σταδίων, ὅπως εἴ τις ἐθέλῃ θεάσασθαι καὶ αὐτὸς ὁμοίως ἐμοὶ τὴν Ἡφαιστιάδα διαγινώσκων τὴν θέσιν αὐτῆς, οὕτως παρασκευάζοιτο πρὸς τὸν πλοῦν. ἐν γάρ τοι τῇ ὅλῃ νήσῳ τῇ Λήμνῳ, κατὰ μὲν τὸ πρὸς ταῖς ἀνατολαῖς μέρος αὐτῆς ἐστιν Ἡφαιστιὰς, κατὰ δὲ τὸ πρὸς ταῖς δυσμαῖς ἡ Μυρίνα. καὶ τό γε ὑπὸ τοῦ ποιητοῦ λεγόμενον ἐπὶ τοῦ Ἡφαίστου, κάππεσεν ἐν Λήμνῳ, διὰ τὴν φύσιν τοῦ λόφου δοκεῖ μοι τὸν μῦθον ἐπίστασθαι. φαίνεται γὰρ ὁμοιότατος κεκαυμένῳ κατά γε τὴν χρόαν καὶ διὰ τὸ μηδὲν ἐν αὐτῷ φύεσθαι. εἰς τοῦτον οὖν τὸν λόφον ἥ τε ἱέρεια παραγενομένη, καθ’ ὃν ἐγὼ καιρὸν ἐπέβην τῆς νήσου, καί τινα πυρῶν τε καὶ κριθῶν ἀριθμὸν ἐμβάλλουσα τῇ γῇ καὶ ἄλλα τινὰ ποιήσασα κατὰ τὸν ἐπιχώριον σεβασμὸν, ἐπλήρωσεν μὲν ὅλην ἅμαξαν τῆς γῆς. κομίσασα δ’ εἰς τὴν πόλιν, ὡς εἶπον ἀρτίως, ἐσκεύασε τὰς πολυθρυλήτους Λημνίας σφραγῖδας. ἔδοξεν οὖν μοι πυθέσθαι μή τι πρότερόν ποτε τράγειον ἢ αἴγειον αἷμα τῇ γῇ ταύτῃ μιγνύμενον ἐν ἱστορίᾳ παρειλήφασιν. ἐφ’ ᾗ ﻿ πεύσει πάντες οἱ ἀκούσαντες ἐγέλασαν, οὐχ οἱ τυχόντες ἄνδρες ὄντες, ἀλλὰ καὶ πάνυ πεπαιδευμένοι τά τ’ ἄλλα καὶ τὴν ἐπιχώριον ἱστορίαν ἅπασαν. ἀλλὰ καὶ βιβλίον ἔλαβον. παρά τινος αὐτῶν, γεγραμμένον ὑπό τινος τῶν ἐπιχωρίων ἀνδρῶν ἔμπροσθεν, ἐν ᾧ τὴν χρῆσιν ἅπασαν ἐδίδασκε τῆς Λημνίας γῆς, ὅθεν οὐκ ὤκνησα κᾀγὼ πειραθῆναι τοῦ φαρμάκου, δισμυρίας λαβὼν σφραγῖδας. ἐχρῆτο δὲ καὶ αὐτὸς ὁ δοὺς τὸ βιβλίον, ἐν τοῖς πρώτοις ἀριθμούμενος τῆς Ἡφαιστιάδος, εἰς πολλὰ τῷ φαρμάκῳ. καὶ γὰρ ἐπὶ τραυμάτων καὶ παλαιῶν καὶ δυσεπουλώτων ἐχεοδήκτων τε καὶ ὅλως θηριοδήκτων, ἐπί τε τῶν θανασίμων φαρμάκων, οὐ προδιδοὺς μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐπιδιδοὺς, ἐχρῆτο τῇ σφραγῖδι. ἔφασκε δὲ καὶ τοῦ διὰ τῶν ἀρκευθίδων φαρμάκου πεῖραν ἔχειν, εἰς ὃ καὶ τῆς Λημνίας ἐμβάλλεται κινοῦντος ἔμετον, εἴ τις ἔτι κατὰ τὴν κοιλίαν ὄντος τοῦ θανασίμου φαρμάκου πίοι τὸ ἀλεξητήριον. ἀλλὰ τούτου μὲν καὶ ἡμεῖς ἔχομεν πεῖραν ἐπί τε λαγωοῦ θαλαττίου καὶ κανθαρίδων, ὑποπτευσάντων μὲν εἰληφέναι τι τοιοῦτον τῶν ἀνθρώπων, ἐμεσάντων δ’ αὐτίκα πᾶν ἐπὶ τῷ διὰ τῆς Λημνίας σφραγῖδος φαρμάκῳ, καὶ μηδὲν μετὰ ταῦτα παθόντων σύμπτωμα τῶν ἑπομένων λαγωῷ τε καὶ κανθαρίσι, καίτοι τῆς δόσεως τῶν ὀλεθρίων φαρμάκων ἐλεγχθείσης. εἰ δὲ καὶ πρὸς τἄλλα θανάσιμα φάρμακα, ταῦτα δὴ τὰ δηλητήρια καλούμενα, τὴν αὐτὴν δύναμιν ἔχει τὸ διὰ τῶν ἀρκευθίδων καὶ τῆς Λημνίας φάρμακον, ἐμοὶ μὲν ἄδηλον. ὁ δ’ ἐκ τῆς Ἡφαιστιάδος ἐπηγγείλατο μέχρι τοῦ καὶ λυττῶντος κυνὸς ἰᾶσθαι δῆγμα φάσκειν αὐτὴν, πινομένην δι’ οἴνου κεκραμένου, κατὰ δὲ τοῦ ἕλκους ἐπιτιθεμένην δι’ ὄξους πάνυ δριμέος. ἀλλὰ δι’ ὄξους μὲν καὶ τὰ τῶν ἄλλων θηρίων ἔλεγεν αὐτὴν ἰᾶσθαι δήγματα, φύλλων βοτανῶν ἔξωθεν ἐπιτιθεμένων, ἃς ἐμάθομεν ἀντιπεπονθέναι σηπεδόσιν. μάλιστα δ’ ἐπῄνει σκόρδιον, εἶτα κενταύριον τὸ λεπτὸν, εἶτα πράσιον. ἐπί γε μὴν κακοήθων καὶ σηπεδονωδῶν ἑλκῶν εἴ ποτ’ ἐχρησάμην τῇ Λημνίᾳ, μεγάλως ὠφέλησε. ἡ δὲ χρῆσις γίνεται κατὰ τὸ μέγεθος τῆς τοῦ ἕλκους κακίας. τὸ μὲν γὰρ δυσῶδες καὶ λίαν πλαδαρόν τε καὶ ῥυπαρὸν, ἀνέχεται δι’ ὄξους δριμυτάτου τῆς Λημνίας λελυμένης εἰς πηλώδη σύστασιν ὁμοίως τοῖς τροχίσκοις, ὧν ἄλλος ἄλλῳ χρῆται, Πολυείδου λέγω καὶ Πασίωνος καὶ Ἄνδρωνος, καὶ τούτῳ τῷ νῦν εἰρημένῳ, προσαγορευομένῳ δὲ Βητινῷ. καὶ γὰρ οὗτοι πάντες ἰσχυρῶς ξηραίνοντες ὠφελοῦσι τὰ κακοήθη τῶν ἑλκῶν, ἀνιέμενοι ποτὲ μὲν οἴνῳ γλυκεῖ, ποτὲ δὲ σιραίῳ, ποτὲ δ’ οἰνομέλιτι, ποτὲ δὲ τῶν λευκῶν οἴνων ἐνίοις ἢ τῶν κιῤῥῶν ἢ ξανθῶν, ὡς ἂν ἡ χρεία κελεύῃ. διορισθήσεται γὰρ ὑπὲρ τούτων ἐν ἑτέροις. ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ δι’ ὄξους ἀνίενταί ποτε, καὶ δι’ οἴνου ἢ ὕδατος ἢ ὀξυμέλιτος, ἢ ὀξυκράτου τε καὶ μελικράτου. καὶ ἡ Λημνία δὲ γῆ δι’ ἑκάστου τῶν εἰρημένων ἀνιεμένη φάρμακον ἐπιτήδειόν ἐστιν εἴς τε τὴν τῶν προσφάτων τραυμάτων κόλλησιν εἴς τε τὴν τῶν χρονίων ἢ δυσεπουλώτων ἢ κακοήθων ἴασιν. ὡσαύτως δὲ καὶ ἡ ἄλλη πᾶσα φαρμακώδης γῆ. διελέσθαι γὰρ δεήσει, καθάπερ ἔμπροσθεν ἀπὸ τῆς στοιχειώδους γῆς, ἐν ᾗ καὶ οἱ λίθοι περιείχοντο τὴν εἰς πηλὸν λυομένην, οὕτω νῦν ἀπὸ τῆς γεωργουμένης τὴν φαρμακώδη. καλέσαι γὰρ οὐδὲν χεῖρον οὕτως ᾗ πρὸς τὰς θεραπείας χρώμεθα, καίτοι καὶ τῆς γεωργουμένης ἡ λιπαρὰ χρήσιμος εἰς θεραπείαν ἐστὶν ἁπάντων τῶν ξηρανθῆναι δεομένων μορίων, ὅθεν αὐτῇ κατὰ Ἀλεξάνδρειάν τε καὶ Αἴγυπτον χρῶνται πολλοὶ μὲν ἰδίᾳ προαιρέσει, πολλοὶ δ’ ἐξ ὀνειράτων. εἶδον γοῦν ἐπὶ τῆς Ἀλεξανδρείας ὑδερώδεις τε καὶ σπληνώδεις ἐνίους χρωμένους τῷ πηλῷ τῆς Αἰγυπτίας γῆς. πολλοὶ δὲ καὶ κνήμας καὶ μηροὺς καὶ πήχεις καὶ βραχίονας καὶ νῶτα καὶ πλευρὰς καὶ στέρνα τῷ πηλῷ τῆς γῆς ταύτης χριόμενοι σαφῶς ὠφε λοῦντο. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον τάς τε παλαιὰς φλεγμονὰς καὶ τὰ χαῦνα τῶν οἰδημάτων ὀνίνησιν ὁ πηλὸς οὗτος, ὥστ’ ἐνίους οἶδα καὶ ὅλην τὴν ἕξιν οἰδαλέους ἐξ αἱμοῤῥοΐδων ἀμέτρου κενώσεως γενομένους, ὠφεληθέντας ἐναργῶς. καί τινες ἀλγήματα χρόνια κατά τι μόριον ἐστηριγμένα τῷ πηλῷ τούτῳ τελέως ἐξιάσαντο, ξηραντικὴν γὰρ ἔχει πᾶσα γῆ δύναμιν. ἐπεὶ καὶ φύσει ξηρὸν αὐτῆς ἐστι τὸ σῶμα, καὶ ὅτε γε ἀκριβῶς ᾖ πυρώδους ἄμικτος οὐσίας, ἀδηκτότατα ξηραίνει, συντελεῖ δὲ εἰς τοῦτο αὐτῇ τὸ πεπλύσθαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῥυπούσης, ἐπίστασθαι, εἶναι δ’ οὔτε κατὰ Φιλοκτήτην οὔτε κατὰ τὸ ἱερὸν τοῦ Ἡφαίστου λόφον, καὶ κατά γε τὸν λόφον, Βητινῷ, κατὰ τὸν ἴδιον λόγον
9.1.2 [2. On the differences of the earth that is customarily so named.] I said that all Greeks customarily give the name "earth" to that which, when it is thrown into liquid, is at once dissolved and becomes mud. Of this, then, one kind is that which men cultivate, having certain differences in its own right (κατὰ τὸν ἴδιον λόγον), in being oily and viscous—a kind which is in every case also black in colour—while the other is more crumbly and non-oily, which they call argil, this too being in some degree whiter. These differences are the most opposed; the others lie between these, either approaching more nearly to the one or to the other, and some seem to be exactly in the middle, standing at an equal distance from both. There are also differences of earth that arise from admixture of bodies of a different kind, insofar as they are stony and sandy; and they separate the mixed-in substance from such earths by soaking and kneading them with much water, until they make the whole liquid. For when this settles, all the stony and sandy matter carried in it sinks to the bottom, while the exact earth floats above. For something of this sort happens also in the case of the Lemnian earth, which some call Lemnian miltos and certain others Lemnian seal, because of the seal sacred to Artemis which is impressed upon it. For the priestess, taking this earth with a certain local honour—not with animals being sacrificed, but with wheat and barley given in return to the place—conveys it to the city, and having kneaded it with water and made a liquid mud and stirred this violently, then having let it settle, she first removes the water on the surface, then, taking the oily part of the earth beneath it and leaving behind only the stony and sandy matter that has settled, which is indeed useless, she dries the oily mud to such a degree until it comes to the consistency of soft wax, and taking small portions of this she impresses upon them the sacred seal of Artemis, and then again dries them in the shade until it is rendered exactly free of moisture, and this becomes the drug known to all physicians, the Lemnian seal. For so some name it, as I said, because of the seal impressed upon it, just as some also, because of its colour, call it Lemnian miltos. It has, then, the same colour as miltos, but differs from it in not staining when it is touched, as that one does, and also in respect of the hill in Lemnos, which is wholly tawny in colour, on which there is neither tree nor rock nor plant, but only earth of this sort. Three differences of it are found: one, the aforesaid, of the sacred earth, which no one else touches except the priestess; a second, different one, of the true miltos, which the carpenters especially use; and a third, of the cleansing (ῥυπούσης) kind, which those of the washers of linens and garments who so wish use. Having read in Dioscorides and certain others that goat's blood is mixed with the Lemnian earth, and that from the mud produced by this mixture the priestess moulds and seals what they call Lemnian seals, I desired myself to investigate the proportion of the mixture. Just as, then, I travelled to Cyprus for the sake of the mines in it, and to Coele Syria, which is a part of Palestine, for the sake of asphalt and certain other things in it worth investigating, so too I did not hesitate to sail to Lemnos, intending to see how much of the blood is mixed with the earth. And on the second occasion of coming from Asia to Rome, travelling on foot through Thrace and Macedonia, I sailed first from Alexandria Troas to Lemnos, having found a ship putting out for Thessalonica, the ship-master having agreed to put in first at Lemnos; and he did put in, but not at the city where he should have. For at the start I did not even know that there were two cities on the island, but supposed that, like Samos and Chios and Cos and Andros and Tenos and all the islands in the Aegean, so too Lemnos had a single city bearing the same name as the whole island. But when I disembarked from the ship, I learned that the city was called Myrina, and that the hill was neither in connection with Philoctetes nor in connection with the temple of Hephaestus in the territory of that city, but in the other, called Hephaestias, and that that city was not even near Myrina; and since the ship-master could not wait for me, I deferred to a later time, when I should come from Rome to Asia, the viewing of Hephaestias. And this was accomplished for me, just as I hoped and proposed. For when, having crossed from Italy to Macedonia and having travelled through almost the whole of it, I arrived at Philippi, which is a city bordering on Thrace, going down from there to the nearby sea, which is a hundred and twenty stades distant, I sailed first to Thasos, somewhere near two hundred stades, from there to Lemnos seven hundred, then again from Lemnos the same seven hundred to Alexandria Troas. And for this reason I have deliberately written about the voyage and the stades, so that if anyone wishes to see Hephaestias himself just as I did, knowing its position, he may thus prepare for the voyage. For in the whole island of Lemnos, Hephaestias lies in the part toward the east and Myrina in the part toward the west. And what is said by the poet about Hephaestus, "he fell in Lemnos," seems to me to have set up (ἐπίστασθαι) the myth because of the nature of the hill. For it appears most like something burnt, both in colour and because nothing grows on it. To this hill, then, the priestess came, at the time when I set foot on the island, and casting a certain number of grains of wheat and barley onto the earth and performing certain other acts in accordance with the local reverence, she filled a whole wagon with the earth. And having conveyed it to the city, as I said just now, she prepared the much-talked-of Lemnian seals. It seemed good to me, then, to inquire whether they had ever before received in their tradition any account of goat's or she-goat's blood being mixed with this earth. At this inquiry all who heard laughed, being not ordinary men but very well educated both in other matters and in the whole local history. But I also received a book from one of them, written earlier by one of the local men, in which he taught the whole use of the Lemnian earth, whence I too did not hesitate to make trial of the drug, taking twenty thousand seals. And the man himself who gave the book, being counted among the first men of Hephaestias, used the drug for many purposes. For both on wounds, old ones and those hard to cicatrize, and on viper-bites and in general bites of venomous animals, and in cases of deadly drugs, giving it not only beforehand but also afterwards, he used the seal. He said also that he had experience of the drug made with juniper berries, into which Lemnian earth also is put, which provokes vomiting, if someone drinks the protective remedy while the deadly drug is still in the belly. But of this we too have experience in the case of the sea-hare and cantharides, when people suspected that they had taken something of this sort, and immediately vomited everything upon the drug made with the Lemnian seal, and afterwards suffered none of the symptoms that follow sea-hare and cantharides, even though the administration of the deadly drugs was proved. But whether against the other deadly drugs, those called deleterious, the drug made with juniper berries and Lemnian earth has the same power, is unclear to me. The man from Hephaestias went so far as to declare that it even cures the bite of a mad dog, when drunk in diluted wine and applied to the ulcer in very pungent vinegar. But with vinegar he said it cures also the bites of the other venomous creatures, with leaves of herbs applied externally, which we have learned to be opposed to putrefactions. He praised most of all scordium, then the slender centaury, then horehound. On malignant and putrefactive ulcers at any rate, whenever I used the Lemnian earth, it helped greatly. The use is in accordance with the magnitude of the badness of the ulcer. For the foul-smelling and very flabby and dirty ulcer admits the Lemnian earth dissolved in very pungent vinegar to a muddy consistency, in the same way as the troches, of which different people use different ones—I mean those of Polyeides and Pasion and Andron, and this one now mentioned, called Betinos. For all these, drying strongly, benefit the malignant ulcers, being diluted sometimes with sweet wine, sometimes with boiled-down must, sometimes with honeyed wine, sometimes with some of the white wines or the tawny or the yellow, as the need may command. For these matters will be distinguished elsewhere. Likewise they are also sometimes diluted with vinegar, and with wine or water or oxymel, or with vinegar-and-water and honey-and-water. And the Lemnian earth, diluted with each of the things mentioned, is a suitable drug both for the agglutination of fresh wounds and for the cure of chronic or hard-to-cicatrize or malignant ulcers. And likewise all the other medicinal earth. For it will be necessary to distinguish, just as earlier we distinguished from the elemental earth—in which stones too were included—the earth that is dissolved into mud, so now from the cultivated earth the medicinal. For it is no worse to name thus that which we use for treatments, although even of the cultivated earth the oily kind is useful for the treatment of all parts that need to be dried; whence in Alexandria and Egypt many use it by their own choice, many as a result of dreams. At any rate I saw at Alexandria some dropsical and splenetic people using the mud of Egyptian earth. And many, anointing their shins and thighs and forearms and upper arms and backs and sides and chests with the mud of this earth, were clearly benefited. In the same way this mud benefits old inflammations and the loose kinds of oedema, so that I know some who had become swollen in their whole habit as a result of immoderate evacuation from haemorrhoids who were manifestly benefited. And some completely cured chronic pains fixed in some part with this mud; for all earth has a drying power, since by nature its body is dry, and when it is exactly unmixed with fiery substance, it dries without the least biting; and its having been washed contributes to this.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ῥυπούσης, κατὰ Φιλοκτήτην, ἐπίστασθαι, Βητινῷ, ἀντιπεπονθέναι, κιῤῥὸν, σκόρδιον, πράσιον, ἀφικέσθαι, ἐπηγγείλατο ... φάσκειν
9.1.2 [2. On the differences of what is customarily called earth.] I said that it is customary among all Greeks to call earth that which, when thrown into liquid, at once dissolves and becomes mud. Of this, then, one kind is that which men cultivate, and it has certain differences according to its own proper account: one kind is oily and viscid, and this is in every case also black in colour; the other is more friable and non-oily, which they call clay, and this too is somewhat whiter. These differences are the most opposed; the others lie between them, either approaching one of the two more than the other, while some even seem to be exactly in the middle, standing at an equal distance from both. Other differences of earth arise from the admixture of bodies of another kind, in so far as earths are stony and sandy. People separate the admixed substance from such earths by soaking them and kneading them up with much water until they make the whole liquid. For when this settles, everything of the stony and sandy kind that was borne along in it sinks to the bottom, and the pure earth floats on top. Something of this sort is done with the Lemnian earth, which some call Lemnian red ochre and certain others Lemnian seal, because of the seal sacred to Artemis that is impressed upon it. The priestess takes this earth with a certain local rite, in which no animals are sacrificed but wheat and barley are given to the place in return, and brings it to the city. She kneads it up with water, makes a liquid mud, and stirs this vigorously; then, having let it settle, she first removes the water on the surface. Next she takes the oily part of the earth beneath it, leaving behind only the stony and sandy sediment, which is also useless, and dries the oily mud until it reaches the consistency of soft wax. Taking small portions of this, she impresses on them the sacred seal of Artemis, and then dries them again in the shade until they are made completely free of moisture. So is produced the drug known to all physicians, the Lemnian seal. For, as I said, some call it so because of the seal impressed upon it, just as some, because of its colour, call it Lemnian red ochre. It has the same colour as red ochre, but differs from it in not staining when touched, as that does. And [it is found] on the hill in Lemnos which is entirely tawny in colour, on which there is neither tree nor rock nor plant, but only earth of this sort. Three differences of it are found: one is the aforementioned sacred earth, which no one touches except the priestess; the second is another, that of true red ochre, which carpenters use most of all; the third is that of the cleansing earth, which those who wash linens and garments use if they wish. Having read in Dioscorides and certain others that goat's blood is mixed with the Lemnian earth, and that from the mud produced by this mixture the priestess moulds and seals what they call Lemnian seals, I myself was eager to inquire into the proportion of the mixture. So, just as I travelled to Cyprus for the sake of its mines, and to Coele Syria, which is part of Palestine, for the sake of asphalt and certain other things there worth inquiring into, so too I did not hesitate to sail to Lemnos, to see how much of the blood is mixed with the earth. On the second occasion, when I was to reach Rome from Asia travelling on foot through Thrace and Macedonia, I first sailed from Alexandria Troas to Lemnos, having found a ship putting out for Thessalonica, the ship-master having agreed to put in at Lemnos first. He did put in, but not at the city where he should have. For I did not even know at first that there were two cities on the island; I supposed that Lemnos, like Samos, Chios, Cos, Andros, Tenos and all the islands of the Aegean, had a single city bearing the same name as the whole island. When I left the ship, I learned that the city was called Myrina, and that neither the place associated with Philoctetes nor the hill sacred to Hephaestus lay in the territory of that city, but in that of the other, called Hephaestias, and that that city was not even near Myrina. As the ship-master could not wait for me, I put off seeing Hephaestias until another time, when I should come from Rome to Asia. And this was accomplished for me just as I had hoped and intended. For when I had crossed from Italy into Macedonia and, having travelled through almost the whole of it on foot, arrived at Philippi, which is a city bordering on Thrace, I went down from there to the nearby sea, a hundred and twenty stades distant, and sailed first to Thasos, about two hundred stades, and from there to Lemnos, seven hundred, and then again from Lemnos the same seven hundred to Alexandria Troas. I have written about the voyage and the stades on purpose, so that if anyone wishes, like me, to see Hephaestias for himself, he may know its position and prepare himself accordingly for the voyage. For on the whole island of Lemnos, Hephaestias lies in the part towards the east, and Myrina in the part towards the west. And as for what the poet says of Hephaestus, 'he fell in Lemnos', the myth seems to me to rest on the nature of the hill. For it appears very like something burnt, both in its colour and because nothing grows on it. To this hill, then, the priestess came at the very time when I landed on the island; she cast a certain number of wheat and barley grains onto the earth, performed certain other things according to the local worship, and filled a whole wagon with the earth. Having brought it to the city, she prepared, as I said just now, the much-celebrated Lemnian seals. It seemed good to me, then, to inquire whether they had received any account of he-goat's or goat's blood having ever formerly been mixed with this earth. At this question all who heard it laughed, and they were not ordinary men, but very well educated, both in other matters and in the whole local history. Indeed I also received a book from one of them, written formerly by one of the local men, in which he taught the entire use of the Lemnian earth; whence I too did not hesitate to make trial of the drug, taking twenty thousand seals. The man who gave me the book, who was counted among the foremost of Hephaestias, himself used the drug for many purposes. He used the seal for wounds, both old and hard to cicatrise, for viper bites and bites of venomous animals generally, and for deadly drugs, not only giving it beforehand but also afterwards. He said too that he had experience of the drug made with juniper berries, into which the Lemnian earth is also put, which provokes vomiting if someone drinks the remedy against it while the deadly drug is still in the belly. We ourselves have experience of this in the case of the sea-hare and cantharides: persons who suspected that they had taken something of the sort vomited everything at once on taking the drug made with the Lemnian seal, and afterwards suffered none of the symptoms that follow the sea-hare and cantharides, although the administration of the deadly drugs had been established. Whether the drug made with juniper berries and the Lemnian earth has the same power against the other deadly drugs too, those called poisons, is unclear to me. But the man from Hephaestias went so far as to declare that it heals even the bite of a rabid dog, when drunk in diluted wine and applied to the ulcer in very pungent vinegar. He said also that in vinegar it heals the bites of the other venomous animals, with the leaves of herbs laid on externally, herbs which we have learnt act contrary to putrefactions; he praised most of all skordion, then the slender centaury, then prasion. Moreover, whenever I used the Lemnian earth on malignant and putrefactive ulcers, it helped greatly. The use is made according to the degree of the ulcer's badness. For the foul-smelling, very flabby and dirty ulcer tolerates the Lemnian earth dissolved in the most pungent vinegar to a muddy consistency, in the same way as the troches, of which different people use different ones, I mean those of Polyeidus, Pasion and Andron, and this one now mentioned, which is called the Betinus. For all these, drying strongly, benefit malignant ulcers, being diluted sometimes with sweet wine, sometimes with siraion, sometimes with honeyed wine [oinomeli], sometimes with some of the white wines or the tawny or yellow ones, as need requires; for these matters will be determined elsewhere. Likewise they are sometimes diluted with vinegar, and with wine or water or oxymel, or oxycrate and melikraton. And the Lemnian earth too, diluted with each of the substances mentioned, is a drug suited both to the agglutination of fresh wounds and to the healing of chronic, hard-to-cicatrise or malignant ones. So likewise is all other medicinal earth. For it will be necessary to distinguish, just as earlier we separated from the elemental earth, in which the stones were also included, the earth that dissolves into mud, so now to separate from the cultivated earth the medicinal. There is no harm in so naming the earth that we use for treatments, although of the cultivated earth too the oily kind is useful for the treatment of all parts that need to be dried; whence in Alexandria and Egypt many use it, many by their own choice, many as a result of dreams. I saw, at any rate, in Alexandria, some dropsical persons and some suffering from the spleen using the mud of the Egyptian earth. And many, anointing their shins, thighs, forearms, upper arms, backs, sides and chests with the mud of this earth, were clearly benefited. In the same way this mud helps old inflammations and lax swellings, so that I know some who had become swollen in their whole habit from immoderate evacuation through haemorrhoids to have been evidently benefited. And some have completely cured chronic pains fixed in some part with this mud; for all earth has a drying power. For its body is by nature dry, and when it is entirely unmixed with fiery substance it dries with the least biting; and its having been washed contributes to this.

Περὶ πλύσεως γῆς.] Πλύνεται δὲ κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον ἅπασα γῆ τῷ προειρημένῳ περὶ τῆς Λημνίας, ὕδατι μὲν πρῶτον ἀναφυραθεῖσα μηδεμιᾶς φαρμακώδους ποιότητος ἔμφασιν ἔχοντι. καταστάντος δὲ τοῦ πηλοῦ καὶ περιαιρεθέντος ἄνωθεν τοῦ ὕδατος, εἶτα τοῦ μετ’ αὐτὸ χωρισθέντος ἀπὸ τῶν ὑφισταμένων κάτω λιθωδῶν τε καὶ ψαμμωδῶν. εἰ δὲ καὶ γεγυμνασμένην ἔχεις τὴν γευστικὴν αἴσθησιν, αὐτὸς ἔσῃ κριτὴς ἀκριβὴς ἧς τε δεῖ πλύνειν γῆς ἧς τε μή. τίνες μὲν γὰρ οὐ δέονται πλύσεως, τινὲς δὲ δὶς ἢ τρὶς δέονται πλυθῆναι. τὴν μὲν οὖν Λημνίαν ἑτοίμην λαμβάνεις ἅπαξ πεπλυμένην ὑπὸ τῆς ἱερείας, ἐκ δευτέρου δὲ πλυθῆναι μὴ δεομένην.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τοῦ μετ’ αὐτὸ χωρισθέντος
9.1.3 [3. On the washing of earth.] All earth is washed in the same manner as that previously described for the Lemnian: first it is kneaded up with water that has no indication of any medicinal quality; then, when the mud has settled and the water has been removed from above, next that which lies beneath it is separated from the stony and sandy matter settling at the bottom. And if you have a trained gustatory sense, you yourself will be an accurate judge of which earth needs to be washed and which does not. For some do not need washing, while some need to be washed twice or three times. The Lemnian, then, you receive ready, washed once by the priestess, and not needing to be washed a second time.

9.1.3 [3. On the washing of earth.] Every earth is washed in the same manner as was stated before concerning the Lemnian. First it is kneaded up with water that shows no trace of any medicinal quality. Then, when the mud has settled and the water has been removed from the top, the [layer] beneath it is next separated from the stony and sandy matter that settles to the bottom. And if you also have a trained gustatory sense, you will yourself be an exact judge of which earth must be washed and which not. For some do not need washing, while others need to be washed twice or three times. The Lemnian, then, you receive ready, washed once already by the priestess, and not needing to be washed a second time.

9.1.4 [δ΄. Περὶ Σαμίας γῆς.] Ἡ Σαμία δ’ οὐδὲ δεῖται πλυθῆναι. χρώμεθα δ’ αὐτῆς θατέρῳ τῶν εἰδῶν μᾶλλον, ὃ δὴ καὶ Σάμιον ἀστέρα καλοῦσιν, εἰς τὰς τοῦ αἵματος πτύσεις, ﻿ ὁπόθεν ἂν γιγνόμεναι τύχωσιν, ὥσπερ καὶ τῇ Λημνίᾳ σφραγῖδι. κατὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν δύναμιν ὠφελοῦσι καὶ τὰς ἐκ μήτρας αἱμοῤῥαγίας καὶ τὸν ὀνομαζόμενον ῥοῦν γυναικεῖον, καὶ μέντοι καὶ τὰς δυσεντερικὰς ἑλκώσεις πρὸ τοῦ σηπεδονώδη γενέσθαι τὰ ἕλκη,
