ἐχρησάμην ἐνίοτε καὶ σαφῶς ὤνησεν ἐνεθεῖσά τε δι’ ἕδρας καὶ ποθεῖσα, προαποκλυσθέντων τῶν ἑλκῶν, ὡς εἰώθαμεν πράττειν, μελικράτῳ μὲν ἀκρατεστέρῳ προτέρῳ, δευτέρῳ δ’ ἅλμῃ. ἐνέθη μὲν οὖν δι’ ἀρνογλώσσου χυλοῦ, δι’ ὀξυκράτου δ’ ὑδαροῦς ἐπόθη. φαίνεται δ’ οὐκ ὀλίγῳ τινὶ δραστικωτέραν δύναμιν ἔχειν ἡ Λημνία γῆ τῆς Σαμίας, ὅθεν οὐδὲ τὰ φλεγμῆναι φθάσαντα φέρει τὴν δύναμιν αὐτῆς, ἀλλὰ τραχύνεται σαφῶς καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ὁ ἄνθρωπος ᾖ μαλακόσαρκος. ὑπὸ δὲ τῆς Σαμίας οὐ μόνον οὐ παροξύνεταί τι τῶν οὕτως ἐχόντων μορίων, ἀλλὰ καὶ παρηγορεῖται, καὶ μάλιστά γε τὰ θερμότερα καὶ χαυνότερα, καθάπερ οἵ τε τιτθοὶ καὶ οἱ ὄρχεις καὶ οἱ ἀδένες. ἐπιτηδείως δ’ ἂν χρῶ τηνικαῦτα τῇ τοιαύτῃ γῇ, μετὰ τὸ λειῶσαι δι’ ὕδατος, ἐπιμιγνὺς ῥοδίνου καλοῦ τοσοῦτον, ὅσον τὸ μιχθὲν οὐκ ἐάσει ξηρανθῆναι τὸ φάρμακον. ἀγαθὸν δὲ τὸ οὕτως σκευασθέν ἐστι καὶ πρὸς τὰς ἄλλας φλεγμονὰς ὅσαι θερμαὶ καὶ βουβῶνας ἀρχομένους καὶ ποδαγρικὰ ῥεύματα καὶ ὅλως ὅπου ψῦξαι βουλόμεθα μετρίως μετὰ τοῦ παρηγορεῖν, ὥστ’ ἐναργῶς φαίνεσθαι τῆς Σαμίας τὴν δύναμιν μετρίως ψυκτικήν. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἀερωδεστέρα πως αὐτῆς ἡ οὐσία παραβαλλομένη τῇ Λημνίᾳ, δηλοῖ δ’ ἡ κουφότης. ἐκ τούτων οὖν τῶν γνωρισμάτων καὶ πᾶσαν ἄλλην φαρ μακώδη γῆν δοκίμαζε. λέγω δὲ γνωρίσματα τήν τε ἐν τῇ συστάσει κουφότητά τε καὶ βαρύτητα καὶ τὴν ἐν τῇ γεύσει τραχύτητα καὶ λειότητα καὶ προσέτι τὸ κολλῶδές τε καὶ ῥυπτικόν. ἐχέκολλος μὲν γάρ ἐστι καὶ γλίσχρος ὁ Σάμιος ἀστὴρ, ἐχούσης τι καὶ τῆς Λημνίας σφραγῖδος ὀλίγον τοιοῦτον. ῥυπτικήν τε δύναμιν ἔχει καὶ μετρίαν Σελινουσία τε γῆ καὶ Χία, διὸ καί τινες τῶν γυναικῶν ἐπὶ τὸ πρόσωπον αὐταῖς χρῶνται. δέδεικται δὲ ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου τὸ βραχέως ῥυπτικὸν ἅπαν εἰς σάρκωσιν ἑλκῶν ἐπιτήδειον, ἐὰν δὲ ξηραίνῃ, καὶ πρὸς ἐπούλωσιν ἀγαθόν· ἐπιτηδειότατα δ’ ἐξ αὐτῶν ἐστι τοῖς τε ἐπιπολῆς ἕλκεσι καὶ πυρικαύτοις ὅσα πρὸς τῷ ξηραίνειν ἀδήκτως οὔτε θερμαίνει σαφῶς οὔτε ψύχει. ὅθεν ἥ τε Σελινουσία καὶ ἡ Χία γῆ κάλλιστα φάρμακα πρὸς τὰ πυρίκαυτα τῶν ἑλκῶν ἐστι· δεῖται γὰρ ταῦτα μετριώτατα ῥυπτόντων φαρμάκων ἄνευ θάλψεως ἢ ψύξεως ἐπιφανοῦς, ὅπερ ὑπάρχει καὶ τῇ Σελινουσίᾳ καὶ τῇ Χίᾳ καὶ τῇ Σαμίᾳ γῇ. λέλεκται δὲ ὅτι καὶ ταύτης εἶδός ἐστιν ὁ ἀστὴρ ὀνομαζόμενος, ὑπερέχων τῆς ἄλλης γῆς τῷ γλίσχρον τι καὶ κολλῶδες ἔχειν, ὅθεν οὔτε πρὸς τὰ ἄλλα τῶν ἑλκῶν οὔτε πρὸς τὰ πυρίκαυτα ταῖς ἄλλαις ἐνάμιλλός ἐστιν, ὅσαι τε κολλῶδες οὐκ ἔχουσιν. ἐμπλαστικωτέραν γὰρ ἐργάζεται τὴν οὐσίαν τὸ κολλῶδες, ὡς μὴ ῥύπτειν, ὅταν γε δηλονότι μηδεμία δριμύτης ἄλλη προσῇ τῷ γλίσχρῳ τε καὶ κολλώδει σώματι, καθάπερ ἐν τοῖς ἰξοῖς ἐστιν. ἥ γε μὴν Χία καὶ ἡ Σελινουσία γῆ τῆς μὲν Σαμίας λείπονται πρὸς τὰς περὶ τιτθοὺς καὶ ὄρχεις καὶ βουβῶνας ἀρχομένας φλεγμονάς. ὅμως δ’ οὐκ ἀνάρμοστοι τυγχάνουσιν οὖσαι, μηδενὸς ἄλλου τῶν ἄκρως ποιούντων παρόντος. ἡ δὲ Κιμωλία μικτῆς οὖσα δυνάμεως, ἔχει μέν τι καὶ ψυκτικὸν, ἔχει δέ τι καὶ διαφορητικὸν βραχὺ, διό πλυθεῖσα μὲν ἀποτίθεται τοῦτο, χωρὶς δὲ τοῦ πλυθῆναι κατ’ ἀμφοτέρας ἐνεργεῖ τὰς δυνάμεις, καθάπερ καὶ ἄλλα πολλὰ τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων διαφοροῦντά τε καὶ ἀποκρουόμενα. παρὰ δὲ τὴν τῶν μιγνυμένων ὑγρῶν αὐτῇ φύσιν ἐναργῶς ἐπιδείκνυται τῶν δυνάμεων ἑκατέραν. τοῖς μὲν γὰρ ἀποκρουομένοις καὶ ψύχουσι μιχθεῖσα τὸν γενόμενον ἐξ αὐτῆς τε κᾀκείνων πηλὸν ἐργάζεται ψύχοντά τε καὶ ἀποκρουόμενον, τοῖς δὲ διαφορητικοῖς διαφοροῦντα καὶ αὐτόν. οὕτω γοῦν καὶ τοῖς πυρικαύτοις ἁρμόττει, καί τινες τῶν ἰδιωτῶν ἐπιχρίουσιν αὐτὴν, παραχρῆμα φυράσαντες ὄξει. χρὴ δ’ ἐν τῇ τοιαύτῃ χρήσει μὴ λίαν εἶναι δριμὺ τὸ ὄξος. εἰ δὲ τοιοῦτον εἴη, βέλτιον ὕδατος αὐτῷ μιγνύναι. μέμνησό γε μὴν τοῦτο κοινὸν ἐπὶ πάσης γῆς εἶναι τῆς κούφης. ἅπασαι γὰρ ὀνίνασι τὰ πυρίκαυτα παραχρῆμα παραχριόμεναι δι’ ὄξους, ἢ ὀξυκράτου, κωλύουσαι φλυκταινοῦσθαι. προσεπιβλέπειν δ’ ἐν τούτῳ καὶ τὴν φύσιν τοῦ θεραπευομένου σώματος, εἰ μαλακὸν ἢ σκληρὸν, εἰδὼς ἀεὶ καθόλου τοῦτο, τὰ μὲν μαλακὰ σώματα μὴ φέρειν τὴν δύναμιν τῶν ἰσχυρῶν φαρμάκων, τὰ δὲ σκληρὰ φέρειν. οὐ μὴν τῆς γ’ ἐνεστώσης πραγματείας ἴδια ταῦτα. ῥηθήσεται γὰρ ἐπιπλέον ἔν τε τοῖς περὶ συνθέσεως φαρμάκων κᾀν τοῖς περὶ τῶν εὐπορίστων. ἡ δὲ παροῦσα διέξοδος ἀπ’ ἀρχῆς ἐσπούδακε τὰς καθόλου δυνάμεις τῶν φαρμάκων εὑρεῖν, αἷς προσέχων τις τὸν νοῦν εἰς τὴν τῶν κατὰ μέρος χρῆσιν εὐπορίαν ἕξει παμπόλλην ἐπιμαθὼν δηλονότι τὴν μέθοδον τῆς χρήσεως αὐτῶν, ὥστ’ οὐκέτι προσήκει μηκύνειν, ἀλλ’ ὅπερ εἴρηται καὶ πρόσθεν ἀναμνῆσαι καὶ νῦν αὐτὴν μὲν τὴν ἄμικτον γῆν ἄλλῃ τινὶ τῶν ἑτερογενῶν οὐσιῶν ξηραντικῆς ἀδήκτως εἶναι δυνάμεως. ἐπεὶ δ’ ἀδύνατόν ἐστιν ἄμικτον εὑρεῖν ἀκριβῶς τι σῶμα, προσεπισκέπτεσθαι προσήκει τὴν μίξιν τῶν συμβεβηκότων ﻿ αὐτῇ κατά τε τὰς ἐν κουφότητι καὶ βαρύτητι διαφορὰς καὶ τὰς ἐν τῇ γεύσει. στύψεως μὲν γάρ τινος ἔμφασιν ἔχουσα τοσοῦτον προσείληφε ψύξεως, ὅσον καὶ στύψεως. εἰ δὲ δριμύτητος ἐμφαίνοιτό τι, τοσοῦτον ἔχει θερμότητος, ὅσον δριμύτητος. ὡσαύτως δ’ ἐπὶ τῆς κούφης καὶ βαρείας σκοπεῖσθαι. τῆς μὲν κούφης τοιαύτης γινομένης, ὅταν ἀερώδους οὐσίας μετέχῃ δαψιλοῦς ἐν τῇ δι’ ὅλης ἑαυτῆς κράσει, τῆς δὲ βαρείας, ὅσῳ περ ἂν ᾖ μᾶλλον τοιαύτη, τοσούτῳ μᾶλλον εἰλικρινεστέρας γῆς ὑπαρχούσης. ἴδιον δὲ γῆς ἐστι τὸ μὴ χεῖσθαι πυρὶ πλησιάζουσαν, ὅπερ ὅ τε μόλυβδος καὶ καττίτερος, ἄργυρός τε καὶ χρυσὸς ἔχουσιν, ὥσθ’ ὅταν ἀργυρῖτιν ἢ χρυσῖτιν ἢ σιδηρῖτιν ἀκούσῃς γῆν, ὀνομάζουσι γὰρ οὕτως ἔνιοι τὰς ἐκ τῶν μετάλλων λαμβανομένας, μὴ νόμιζε δι’ ὅλου κεκρᾶσθαι τὸν ἄργυρον ἢ τὸν χρυσὸν ἢ τὸν σίδηρον τῇ γῇ· πλησιάζειν δὲ μορίοις μικροῖς τῆς γῆς ἀναμεμιγμένα μόρια μικρὰ, κατὰ μὲν τὴν χρυσῖτιν χρυσοῦ, κατὰ δὲ τὴν ἀργυρῖτι νἀργύρου, κατὰ δὲ τὴν σιδηρῖτιν σιδήρου, καὶ ταῦτ’ ἐν ταῖς καμίνοις ὑπὸ τοῦ πυρὸς χεόμενα συνέρχεσθαι πρὸς ἄλληλα. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον ἡ τὴν ὕαλον ἔχουσα ψάμμιός ἐστιν, ἐν ψάμμῳ γὰρ μάλιστα τῆς τοιαύτης οὐσίας εὑρίσκεται ψήγματα πολλάκις μικρά. καὶ ὅσοι τούτων ἔμπειροι θεασάμενοι τὰς τοιαύτας ψάμμους γνωρίζουσιν ὁπόσον ἐξ αὐτῶν ἀθροῖσαι δύνανται τῆς ὑάλου, καὶ χρυσοῦ δὲ ψήγματα μικρὰ πολλὰ κατά τινας εὑρίσκεται ψάμμους, ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἐξ ἁπάσης ψάμμου τὸν χρυσὸν ἐξαίρουσι καὶ τὴν ὕαλον οἱ περὶ ταῦτ’ ἔχοντες, ἐκλεγόμενοι δηλονότι τὰς μετ’ ὀλίγης δαπάνης ἀθροι ζούσας πλεῖστον, ὡς τό γ’ ἐν ταῖς καμίνοις ἀναλώσαντας πολλὰ βραχύ τι σχεῖν τῆς διαθροιζομένης οὐσίας ἀκερδὲς αὐτοῖς εἶναι δοκεῖ. διὰ τοῦτο μὲν οὖν καίτοι γε πολλαῖς ψάμμοις χρυσοῦ καὶ ὑάλου ψηγμάτων περιεχομένων οὐκ ἐπὶ πάσας οἱ περὶ ταῦτα δεινοὶ παραγίνονται. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον οὐδὲ χαλκὸν ἢ ἄργυρον ἢ σίδηρον ἢ κασσίτερον ἢ μόλυβδον ἐκ πάσης γῆς ἐκλέγουσιν. οὐ μὴν οὐδ’ ὅταν χωρίσωσιν ἑκάστην τῶν εἰρημένων ἀπὸ τῆς ἀναμεμιγμένης γῆς, ἡ καταλειπομένη παραπλησία ταῖς ἄλλαις γαῖς ἐστιν, ἃς συνήθως ἔφην ὀνομάζεσθαι πᾶσιν Ἕλλησιν, ἐχούσαις γνώρισμα κοινὸν τὸ τέγγεσθαί τε καὶ διαλύεσθαι ῥᾳδίως εἰς πηλόν. τῆς γὰρ ἐν τοῖς μετάλλοις γῆς ὑπόλειμμα λιθῶδες γίγνεται, ἄτηκτόν τε καὶ ἄτεγκτον. λέγω δὲ τέγγεσθαι μὲν τὸ δι’ ὅλης τῆς οὐσίας ὑγραίνεσθαι, βρέχεσθαι δὲ τὸ κατὰ τὴν ἔξωθεν ἐπιφάνειαν μόνην, οὐ διικνουμένης εἰς τὸ βάθος αὐτῆς τῆς ὑγρότητος. οὕτως οὖν καὶ ἡ μεταλλευομένη Καδμεία γεννᾶται, λιθώδης οὖσα καὶ ἄτεγκτος. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τῶν τοιούτων σωμάτων ἐφεξῆς εἰρήσεται· νυνὶ δ’ ἐπανήξω πάλιν ἐπὶ τὴν φαρμακώδη γῆν, ἥτις, ὅτι μὲν εἰς πηλὸν λύεται ῥᾳδίως ἐν ὕδατι τεγγομένη, διὰ τοῦτ’ ὀνομάζεται γῆ, διότι δὲ χρῄζομεν αὐτῆς ὥσπερ καὶ ἄλλων φαρμάκων, διὰ τοῦτο φαρμακῖτις εἰκότως ἂν λέγοιτο. καίτοι τήν γ’ ἀμπελῖτιν ὀνομαζομένην γῆν ἔνιοι φαρμακῖτιν ὀνομάζουσιν μόνην, ἤτοι γ’ ὡς μόνην τοιαύτην οὖσαν ἢ ὡς ἐνεργεστέραν τὴν φαρμακώδη δύναμιν ἔχουσαν, ὅπερ καὶ ἀληθές ἐστιν. ὀνομάζεται δ’ ἀμπελῖτις οὐχ ὅτι φυτεύειν ἀμπέλους ἐν ταύτῃ βέλτιον, ἀλλ’ ὅτι περιχριομένη ταῖς ἀμπέλοις φθείρει τοὺς γεννωμένους ἐπ’ αὐτῶν σκώληκας, οὓς σκνῖπας ὀνομάζουσιν οἱ παρ’ ἡμῖν ἀμπελουργοί. γεννῶνται δὲ οὗτοι τοῦ ἦρος εἰσβάλλοντος, ἡνίκα βλαστάνουσιν οἱ ἄμπελοι καὶ τό γ’ ἐνοιδισκόμενον αὐτῶν μέρος, ὅθεν ὁ βλαστὸς φύεται, ὃ καλοῦσιν ὀφθαλμόν. τούτους οὖν τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς διεσθίοντες οἱ σκνῖπες οὐ μικρὰ βλάπτουσιν τὰς ἀμπέλους, διὸ καὶ τὰς ῥίζας τῶν ὀφθαλμῶν τούτων περιχρίουσιν οἱ περὶ ταῦτα δεινοί. κατὰ τοῦτο μὲν οὖν ἀμπελῖτίς τε καὶ φαρμακῖτις ἡ τοιαύτη γῆ προσαγορεύεται, δηλοῦσα κᾀκ τοῦ φθείρειν τοὺς σκνῖπας ὅσον αὐτῇ μέτεστι δυνάμεως φαρμακώδους. ἀφέστηκεν δὲ πολὺ καὶ τῶν ἄλλων εἰδῶν τῆς γῆς, οἷς χρώμεθα πρὸς τὰς ἰάσεις, ἐγγὺς ἤδη τῆς λιθώδους ἥκουσα. διὸ καὶ μιγνυμένην αὐτὴν ἐν ταῖς τῶν φαρμάκων γραφαῖς εὑρήσεις, ἔνθα ξηρᾶναί τι καὶ διαφορῆσαι προσήκει. τὸ γὰρ ἄδηκτον καὶ μέτριον καὶ παρηγορικὸν οὐκ ἔχει καθάπερ ἡ Χία τε καὶ Σαμία καὶ Σελινουσία. καὶ περὶ τῆς Κιμωλίας δὲ προείρηται βραχὺ τούτων οὔσης ἰσχυροτέρας, ἀδήκτου δ’ ἔτι καὶ αὐτῆς, καὶ μάλιστα εἰ πλυθείη. καὶ ἡ Κρητικὴ δὲ γῆ παραπλησία πώς ἐστι ταύταις, ἀλλ’ ἱκανῶς ἀσθενὴς ὑπάρχει, πολὺ τὸ ἀερῶδες ἔχουσα, τὸ μέντοι ῥυπτικὸν ἔχει, διὸ καὶ οἱ ἄνθρωποι λαμπρύνουσιν αὐτῇ τὰ ῥυπαρὰ τῶν ἀργυρωμάτων, ὥστ’ ἔχοις ἂν καὶ ταύτην ἐς ὅπερ καὶ τὰς ἄλλας τὰς ἀδήκτως ῥυπτούσας προειρήκαμεν ἐπιτηδείους εἶναι. τούτων δ’ ἁπασῶν ἡ Λημνία δύναμιν ἰσχυροτέραν ἔχει, πρόσεστι γὰρ αὐτῇ τι καὶ στύψεως, ἡ δ’ Ἐρετριὰς ἔτι καὶ ταύτης ἰσχυροτέραν, οὐ μὴν ὥστ’ ἤδη δάκνειν. ἐὰν δὲ καὶ πλυθῇ, πάνυ μετρία γίνεται παραπλησίως ταῖς προειρημέναις. ἐγχωρεῖ δὲ τὴν γῆν ταύτην οὐχ ἅπαξ, ἀλλὰ καὶ δὶς ἢ τρὶς πλυθῆναι, καθάπερ καὶ τὴν Κιμωλίαν. καὶ μέντοι καὶ καίουσιν αὐτὴν ἔνιοι ποιοῦντες δηλονότι λεπτομερεστέραν τε καὶ δριμυτέραν, ὥστ’ εἰς τὴν διαφορητικὴν μεταπίπτειν δύναμιν. εἰ δὲ πλυθείη καυθεῖσα, τὸ μὲν δριμὺ καταλιποῦσα τῷ ὕδατι, τὸ δ’ ἐκ τῆς ὀπτήσεως λεπτομερὲς ἔχουσα, ξηραντικωτέρα γίνεται. διὸ τῆς ἀκαύστου τῷ κοινῷ λόγῳ γῆς ἁπάσης ἐπὶ τῶν ἑλκῶν οὔσης χρησίμης, ἔτι μᾶλλον ἡ μετὰ τὸ καυθῆναι πλυθεῖσα, καὶ πρὸς τὰ δυσσάρκωτά τε καὶ δυσεπούλωτα χρήσιμός ἐστιν. ὄντων δ’ αὐτῆς δυοῖν εἰδῶν ἡ ﻿ τεφρώδης κατὰ τὴν χρόαν ἀμείνων ἐστὶ τῆς πάνυ λευκῆς. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἄλλη γῆ πνιγῖτις ὀνομαζομένη, κατὰ μὲν τὴν ὅλην δύναμιν ἐοικυῖα τῇ Κιμωλίᾳ, κατὰ δὲ τὴν χρόαν ἀφεστηκυῖα. μέλαινα γάρ ἐστιν ὁμοίως τῇ ἀμπελίτιδι, τὸ δὲ γλίσχρον καὶ κολλῶδες οὐχ ἧττον ἔχει Σαμίας γῆς, ἀλλ’ ἔστιν ὅτε καὶ μᾶλλον. ἐδόθη δ’ ἡμῖν ἐν τῷ μεγάλῳ τούτῳ λοιμῷ, καὶ ἄλλη τις ἐξ Ἀρμενίας τῆς ὁμόρου Καππαδοκίας γῆ ξηραντικωτέρα, τὴν χρόαν ὠχρά· λίθον δ’ αὐτὴν ὠνόμαζεν, οὐ γῆν, ὁ δοὺς, καὶ ἔστιν εὐλειοτάτη, καθάπερ καὶ ἡ τίτανος. ὀνομάζω δ’ οὕτω δηλονότι τὴν κεκαυμένην πέτραν. ἀλλὰ καὶ ὥσπερ ἐκείνης οὐδὲν ἐμφέρεται ψαμμῶδες, οὕτως οὐδὲ τῆς Ἀρμενίας. μετὰ γὰρ τὸ θραυσθῆναι τῷ δοίδυκι κατὰ τὴν θυίαν, οὕτως ἐστὶ λεία καὶ ἄλιθος ὥσπερ ἡ τίτανος καὶ ὁ Σάμιος ἀστὴρ, οὐ μὴν ὁμοίως γε κούφη τῷ ἀστέρι. διὸ καὶ πεπύκνωται μᾶλλον αὐτοῦ καὶ ἧττον ἀερώδης ἐστὶν, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο φαντασίαν ἀποφαίνει τοῖς ἀμελέστερον ὁρῶσι λίθος εἶναι. διαφέρει δ’ οὐδὲν ὡς πρὸς τὰ παρόντα λίθον ἢ γῆν αὐτὴν ὀνομάζειν, εἰδότας ἄκρως ξηραίνουσαν. ἐπί τε γὰρ δυσεντεριῶν καὶ τῶν κατὰ γαστέρα ῥευμάτων, αἵματός τε πτύσεως καὶ κατάῤῥου καὶ προσέτι τῶν κατὰ τὸ στόμα σηπεδονωδῶν ἑλκῶν ἁρμόττει μάλιστα. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τοὺς ἀπὸ κεφαλῆς εἰς θώρακα ῥευματιζομένους ὀνίνησι μεγάλως, ὥστε καὶ τοὺς διὰ τὴν τοιαύτην αἰτίαν συνεχῶς δυσπνοοῦντας ἰσχυρῶς ὠφελεῖ. καὶ μέντοι καὶ ὅσοι φθόῃ κάμνουσιν, καὶ τούτους ὀνίνησιν. ξηραίνει γὰρ αὐτῶν τὸ ἕλκος, ὡς μηδὲ βήττειν ἔτι, πλὴν εἰ κατὰ τὴν δίαιταν ἁμαρτάνοιεν ἀξιολόγως ἢ τὸ περιέχον ἐξαιφνίδιον εἰς δυσκρασίαν μεταπέσοι. καί μοι δοκεῖ, καθάπερ ἐπὶ τῶν συρίγγων ἐθεασάμεθα πολλάκις, οὐ μόνον ἐν ἄλλοις μορίοις, ἀλλὰ καὶ κατὰ τὴν ἕδραν ἄνευ τοῦ κολλύριον καθεῖναι τὸν ῥύπον ἢ τὸν τύλον ἐξαιροῦν τῆς σύριγγος, αὐτῷ μόνῳ τῷ ξηραίνοντι φαρμάκῳ προστελλομένας τε καὶ κλειομένας αὐτὰς, οὕτω κᾀπὶ τοῦ κατὰ τὸν πνεύμονα συμβαίνειν ἕλκους. φαίνεται γὰρ καὶ τοῦτο διὰ τῶν ξηραινόντων φαρμάκων ὁμοίως ὀνινάμενον, ὅταν τε μέτριον ᾖ καὶ μὴ μέγα λίαν, ὥστ’ ἔδοξαν ἔνιοι τῶν ἐχόντων αὐτὰ τελείως ἀπηλλάχθαι, καὶ τῶν γ’ εἰς τὴν Λιβύην ἀπὸ Ῥώμης διὰ τοιαύτην αἰτίαν πορευθέντων ἔνιοι τελείως ἐπείσθησαν ὑγιεῖς εἶναι, καὶ μέχρι γέ τινων ἐτῶν ἀμέμπτως διήγαγον, εἶθ’ ὕστερόν ποτε πάλιν ἀφυλακτότερον αὐτοῖς διαιτηθεῖσιν ὑποστροφὴ τοῦ νοσήματος ἐγένετο. τούτους οὖν, ὡς ἔφην, ἡ ἐκ τῆς Ἀρμενίας βῶλος ἐναργῶς ὠφέλησε καίτοι γ’ ἐν Ῥώμῃ διατρίβοντας, ἔτι τε μᾶλλον τοὺς δυσπνοοῦντας συνεχῶς. ἐν δὲ τῷ μεγάλῳ τούτῳ λοιμῷ παραπλησίῳ τὴν ἰδέαν ὄντι τῷ κατὰ Θουκυδίδην γενομένῳ πάντες οἱ πιόντες τούτου τοῦ φαρμάκου διὰ ταχέων ἐθεραπεύθησαν, ὅσους δ’ οὐδὲν ὤνησεν ἀπέ θανον πάντες, οὐδ’ ὑπ’ ἄλλου τινὸς ὠφελήθησαν, ᾧ καὶ δῆλον ὅτι μόνους τοὺς ἀνιάτως ἔχοντας οὐκ ὠφέλησε. πίνεται δὲ μετ’ οἴνου λεπτοῦ τὴν σύστασιν, κεραμένου μετρίως μὲν, εἰ ἀπύρετος εἴη παντάπασιν ὁ ἄνθρωπος ἢ βραχὺ πυρεταίνοι, πάνυ δ’ ὑδαροῦς, εἰ πυρέττοι μειζόνως. οὐ μὴν οὐδὲ σφοδροὶ κατὰ τὴν θερμασίαν εἰσὶν οἱ λοιμώδεις πυρετοί. περὶ δὲ τῶν ξηρανθῆναι δεομένων ἑλκῶν τί δεῖ καὶ λέγειν ὁπηλίκην ἔχει δύναμιν ἡ Ἀρμενικὴ βῶλος αὕτη; καλεῖν δ’ ἔξεστί σοι, καθάπερ ἔφην, καὶ λίθον αὐτὴν, ὡς ὁ δοὺς ὠνόμαζεν, καὶ γῆν, ὡς ἂν ἐγὼ φαίην, ἐπειδὴ καὶ τέγγεται τοῖς ὑγροῖς.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐχέκολλος, ἀκρατεστέρῳ προτέρῳ, ὅσαι τε κολλῶδες οὐκ ἔχουσιν, τὸ ἐν ταῖς καμίνοις ἀναλώσαντας, ὠχρά, φαντασίαν ἀποφαίνει
9.1.4 [4. On Samian earth.] Samian earth does not even need to be washed. We use rather the one of its two kinds which they also call the Samian star, for spittings of blood, from whatever source they happen to arise, just as we use the Lemnian seal. By the same power they also help haemorrhages from the womb and the so-called female flux, and indeed also dysenteric ulcerations before the ulcers become putrefactive; it is the custom of physicians to call such conditions nomai, from the putrefaction 'grazing' (nemesthai) upon the neighbouring parts, destroying them along with the part first damaged. I have sometimes used the Lemnian earth too in such cases, and it clearly did good both when injected through the seat and when drunk, the ulcers having first been washed out, as we are accustomed to do, first with a fairly undiluted melikraton, and second with brine. It was injected, then, in juice of plantain, and it was drunk in watery oxykraton (vinegar-and-water). The Lemnian earth appears to have a power not a little more active than the Samian, which is why parts that have already become inflamed do not tolerate its power, but are plainly made rough, and most of all when the person is soft-fleshed. Whereas by the Samian earth not only is none of the parts in such a state exacerbated, but it is actually soothed, and most of all the hotter and more flaccid parts, such as the breasts and the testicles and the glands. In such a case you would use this sort of earth suitably if, after grinding it fine in water, you mix in as much good rose oil as will, once mixed in, not allow the drug to dry out. What is prepared in this way is good also for the other inflammations that are hot, and for incipient buboes and gouty fluxes, and in general wherever we wish to cool moderately while soothing, so that the power of the Samian earth is manifestly shown to be moderately cooling. Its substance is also somewhat more airy when compared with the Lemnian, as its lightness shows. By these marks, then, test every other medicinal earth as well. By marks I mean the lightness and heaviness in its consistency, and the roughness and smoothness in its taste, and in addition the glutinous and the cleansing quality. For the Samian star is glue-holding and viscous, the Lemnian seal also having a little of this sort of quality. Both Selinusian and Chian earth have a cleansing power, and a moderate one, which is why some women use them on the face. It has been shown in the third book of the Method of Healing that everything that is slightly cleansing is suited to the fleshing-over of ulcers, and if it dries, it is also good for cicatrization; and the most suitable of these for superficial ulcers and burns are those which, in addition to drying without biting, neither heat nor cool perceptibly. Hence Selinusian and Chian earth are the finest drugs for burn ulcers; for these require drugs that cleanse most moderately without conspicuous warming or cooling, which is the case with Selinusian and Chian and Samian earth. It has been said that the so-called star is also a kind of the latter, surpassing the other earth in having something viscous and glutinous, which is why neither for other ulcers nor for burns is it a match for the others, those at least which do not have the glutinous quality. For the glutinous quality makes the substance more clogging, so that it does not cleanse, at least whenever no other pungency is present in addition to the viscous and glutinous body, as there is in bird-lime. Chian and Selinusian earth, however, fall short of the Samian for incipient inflammations about the breasts and testicles and groins. Nevertheless they are not unsuitable, when nothing else of those that act perfectly is at hand. Cimolian earth, being of mixed power, has something cooling, but also something slightly dispersing; hence when washed it lays this aside, while without being washed it acts by both powers, just as many other compound drugs do which both disperse and repel. And according to the nature of the liquids mixed with it, it manifestly displays each of its two powers. For mixed with repellent and cooling liquids it renders the mud produced from itself and them cooling and repellent, and with dispersing ones it renders it dispersing as well. In this way, at any rate, it also suits burns, and some laymen smear it on, having kneaded it on the spot with vinegar. In such use the vinegar should not be too pungent; and if it should be so, it is better to mix water with it. Remember, however, that this is common to every light earth: for all of them benefit burns when smeared on at once with vinegar or oxykraton, preventing them from blistering. In this one must look also to the nature of the body being treated, whether it is soft or hard, always knowing this as a general principle, that soft bodies do not tolerate the power of strong drugs, whereas hard bodies tolerate it. These matters, however, are not proper to the present treatise; for they will be spoken of at greater length both in the books on the composition of drugs and in those on readily available remedies. The present exposition has from the beginning striven to discover the general powers of drugs, attending to which a person will have very ample resources for their particular use, provided of course he has learnt in addition the method of using them; so that it is no longer fitting to prolong matters, but, as was said before, to recall now too that earth itself, unmixed with any other substance of a different kind, is of a drying power without biting. But since it is impossible to find any body that is exactly unmixed, it is fitting to examine in addition the admixture of what has come to be attached to it, according to the differences in lightness and heaviness and those in taste. For if it has some suggestion of astringency, it has taken on as much coldness as astringency; and if some pungency should appear, it has as much heat as pungency. One must consider in the same way in the case of light and heavy earth: the light earth becoming such whenever it partakes of abundant airy substance in the mixture throughout its whole self, while the heavy, the more it is such, by so much the more is a purer earth. It is a property of earth not to melt when it comes near fire, which lead and tin, silver and gold do have; so that when you hear of a silver-earth or gold-earth or iron-earth—for some so name those taken from the mines—do not suppose that the silver or the gold or the iron is blended through and through with the earth, but that small particles lie near small particles of the earth, mixed in with them, of gold in the gold-earth, of silver in the silver-earth, of iron in the iron-earth, and that these, being melted by the fire in the furnaces, come together with one another. In the same way the earth that contains glass is sandy, for it is in sand especially that small grains of this kind of substance are often found. And those experienced in these matters, on seeing such sands, recognize how much glass they can gather from them; and many small grains of gold are found in certain sands, but those who occupy themselves with these things do not extract the gold and the glass from every sand, choosing, of course, those which yield the most at little expense, since to spend a great deal in the furnaces and obtain a trifle of the substance collected seems to them unprofitable. For this reason, then, although many sands contain grains of gold and glass, those skilled in these matters do not go to all of them. By the same reasoning they do not pick out copper or silver or iron or tin or lead from every earth. Nor indeed, when they separate each of the things mentioned from the earth mixed with it, is the residue like the other earths, which I said are customarily so named by all Greeks, having as a common mark that they are soaked through and easily dissolve into mud. For the residue of the earth in the mines becomes stony, unmeltable and not to be soaked through. By 'being soaked through' I mean being moistened through the whole substance, and by 'being wetted' being moistened on the outer surface alone, the moisture not penetrating to its depth. Thus too the mined cadmia is produced, being stony and not to be soaked through. But about such bodies we shall speak in due course; now I shall return again to medicinal earth, which, because it readily dissolves into mud when soaked in water, is for this reason named earth, and because we have need of it as of other drugs, for this reason might reasonably be called pharmakitis. And yet some call only the so-called ampelitis earth pharmakitis, either as being the only one of this kind or as having the medicinal power more effectively, which is indeed true. It is named ampelitis not because it is better to plant vines in it, but because, smeared around the vines, it destroys the worms that breed on them, which the vine-dressers in our country call sknipes. These are bred when spring sets in, when the vines sprout, and especially the swelling part of them from which the shoot grows, which they call the eye. By eating through these eyes, then, the sknipes do no small harm to the vines, which is why those skilled in these matters smear the roots of these eyes. On this account, then, such earth is called both ampelitis and pharmakitis, showing from its destruction of the sknipes too how much medicinal power it possesses. It stands far apart also from the other kinds of earth which we use for cures, coming already near to the stony. Hence you will find it mixed in the prescriptions of drugs where it is fitting to dry and disperse something. For it does not have the non-biting, moderate and soothing quality like the Chian and Samian and Selinusian. Of the Cimolian also it has been said before that it is slightly stronger than these, but still non-biting itself too, and especially if it has been washed. The Cretan earth too is somewhat like these, but it is quite weak, having much of the airy, yet it has the cleansing quality, which is why people brighten dirty silverware with it; so that you might have this too for the purpose for which we have said before that the others which cleanse without biting are suitable. Of all these the Lemnian has a stronger power, for there is present in it also something of astringency, and the Eretrian a still stronger one than this, though not so as actually to bite. And if it is washed too, it becomes quite moderate, like those mentioned before. It is possible for this earth to be washed not once but even two or three times, as also the Cimolian. And indeed some also burn it, making it evidently more fine-particled and more pungent, so that it passes over into the dispersing power. And if, having been burnt, it is washed, leaving the pungency behind in the water but keeping the fine-particled quality from the roasting, it becomes more drying. Hence, whereas unburnt earth of every kind is, by the common account, useful for ulcers, that which has been washed after being burnt is even more so, and is useful also for ulcers that are hard to flesh over and hard to cicatrize. There being two kinds of it, the one ash-coloured in hue is better than the very white. There is also another earth called pnigitis, resembling the Cimolian in its whole power but differing in colour, for it is black, like the ampelitis, and it has the viscous and glutinous quality no less than Samian earth, but sometimes even more. There was also given to us, in this great plague, another earth from the part of Armenia bordering on Cappadocia, more drying, yellowish-ochre in colour; the one who gave it called it a stone, not an earth, and it is extremely smooth, like lime too—I so name, obviously, burnt rock. But just as nothing sandy is carried along in that, so neither in the Armenian; for after being broken up with the pestle in the mortar it is as smooth and stone-free as lime and the Samian star, though not equally light with the star. Hence it is also more compact than it and less airy, and for this reason gives those who look at it more carelessly the impression of being a stone. It makes no difference for present purposes whether one calls it a stone or an earth, knowing that it dries in the highest degree. For in dysenteries and fluxes of the belly, and spitting of blood and catarrh, and in addition putrefactive ulcers in the mouth, it is most suitable. And indeed it greatly benefits those who suffer fluxes from the head into the chest, so that it also strongly helps those who are continually short of breath for such a cause. And indeed those who labour under consumption—these too it benefits. For it dries their ulcer, so that they no longer even cough, unless they should err considerably in their regimen or the surrounding air should suddenly fall into a bad mixture. And it seems to me that, just as we have often observed in the case of fistulas, not only in other parts but also at the seat, that without inserting a collyrium that removes the filth or the callus from the fistula, by the drying drug alone they are brought together and closed, so it happens also in the case of the ulcer in the lung. For this too appears to be benefited in like manner by drying drugs, whenever it is moderate and not very large; so that some of those who had them thought they had been completely rid of them, and of those who travelled from Rome to Libya for such a cause some were completely persuaded that they were healthy, and for some years passed their lives without complaint, and then at some later time, when they had lived a less guarded regimen, a relapse of the disease occurred. These, then, as I said, the bole from Armenia manifestly helped, even though they were living in Rome, and still more those continually short of breath. And in this great plague, which is similar in form to the one that occurred in Thucydides' time, all who drank this drug were cured quickly, while all those whom it did not benefit died, nor were they helped by anything else; from which it is clear that it failed to help only those who were in an incurable state. It is drunk with wine thin in consistency, moderately mixed if the person is entirely free of fever or feverish only slightly, but quite watery if he is more strongly feverish. Nor indeed are the pestilential fevers violent in their heat. As for ulcers that need to be dried, what need is there even to say how great a power this Armenian bole has? You may call it, as I said, both a stone, as the one who gave it named it, and an earth, as I would say, since it is also soaked through by liquids.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐχέκολλος, τῶν ἄκρως ποιούντων, αὐτὴν μὲν τὴν ἄμικτον γῆν ἄλλῃ τινὶ τῶν ἑτερογενῶν οὐσιῶν, τό γ’ ἐνοιδισκόμενον αὐτῶν μέρος, ἀργυρῖτι νἀργύρου, φθόῃ, τραχύνεται
9.1.4 [4. On Samian earth.] Samian earth does not even need to be washed. Of its two kinds we use the one which they also call the ‘Samian star’ more, for spittings of blood from whatever source they happen to arise, just as we also use the Lemnian seal. By the same power these also benefit haemorrhages from the womb and the so-called female flux, and indeed also dysenteric ulcerations before the ulcers have become putrefactive. Physicians are accustomed to call such conditions ‘nomai’ (spreading sores), from the putrefaction's spreading (nemesthai) to the adjoining parts and destroying them together with the part first damaged. I have sometimes used Lemnian earth in such cases too, and it clearly helped, both injected through the anus and drunk, the ulcers having first been washed out, as we are accustomed to do, first with rather undiluted honey-water (melikraton) and second with brine. It was injected in plantain juice and drunk in watery oxycrate. Lemnian earth appears to have a power more active than the Samian by no small amount. Hence parts that are already inflamed do not tolerate its power, but are clearly roughened, especially when the person is soft-fleshed. By Samian earth, however, not only is none of the parts in that state exacerbated, but they are even soothed, especially the warmer and looser parts, such as the breasts, the testicles and the glands. In such cases you would suitably use this earth after levigating it with water, mixing in as much good rose oil as will keep the drug from drying out once it is mixed in. What is prepared in this way is also good for the other inflammations that are hot, for incipient buboes, for gouty fluxes, and in general wherever we wish to cool moderately while also soothing. It thus plainly appears that the power of Samian earth is moderately cooling. Its substance is also somewhat airier when compared with the Lemnian, as its lightness shows. From these signs, then, test every other medicinal earth too. By signs I mean lightness and heaviness in consistency, roughness and smoothness in taste, and in addition the glutinous and the cleansing. For the Samian star is glue-like and viscid, and the Lemnian seal also has a little of this quality. Selinusian earth and Chian have a cleansing power, and a moderate one; this is why some women use them on the face. It was shown in the third book of the Therapeutic Method that everything slightly cleansing is suitable for the fleshing of ulcers, and, if it also dries, good for cicatrization as well. Most suitable of these for superficial ulcers and for burns are those which, besides drying without biting, neither clearly heat nor cool. Hence Selinusian and Chian earth are excellent drugs for burn ulcers. For these need drugs that cleanse most moderately, without evident heating or cooling, and this belongs to Selinusian, to Chian and to Samian earth. It has been said that there is also a kind of this last, called the star, which exceeds the other earth in having something viscid and glutinous. Hence, both for other ulcers and for burns, it is no match for the others, that is, for those which have nothing glutinous. For the glutinous makes the substance more clogging, so that it does not cleanse, provided, of course, that no other pungency is present in the viscid and glutinous body, as there is in birdlimes (ixoi). Chian and Selinusian earth, however, fall short of Samian for incipient inflammations around the breasts, the testicles and buboes. Nevertheless they are not unfitting when none of the other drugs that act to the highest degree is at hand. Kimolian earth, being of mixed power, has something cooling and also something slightly dispersing. Hence when washed it lays the latter aside, while without washing it acts with both its powers, just as many of the compound drugs both disperse and repel. In accordance with the nature of the liquids mixed with it, it plainly displays each of its powers. For mixed with repelling and cooling substances, it makes the mud formed from it and them cooling and repelling, and mixed with dispersing substances it makes the mud dispersing too. Thus it is also fitting for burns, and some laymen smear it on, kneading it with vinegar on the spot. In such use the vinegar must not be too pungent; if it is, it is better to mix water with it. Remember that this is common to all light earth: all of them benefit burns when smeared on immediately with vinegar or oxycrate, preventing blistering. In this matter, look also at the nature of the body being treated, whether it is soft or hard, knowing always as a universal rule that soft bodies do not bear the power of strong drugs, while hard ones do. These matters, however, do not belong to the present treatise; they will be discussed more fully in the works On the Composition of Drugs and On Readily Procured Remedies. The present exposition has from the beginning been concerned to find the universal powers of drugs. Whoever attends to these will have very great resource for their use in particular cases, having of course also learned the method of using them. So it is no longer fitting to be lengthy, but only to recall now too what was said before: earth itself, unmixed with any other heterogeneous substance, is of a power drying without biting. Since it is impossible to find any body that is precisely unmixed, one should further consider the mixture of the attributes that accompany it, according to the differences in lightness and heaviness and those in taste. If it gives an indication of some astringency, it has taken on as much cooling as it has astringency. If something of pungency appears, it has as much heat as it has pungency. Likewise one should consider the light and the heavy earth. The light earth becomes such when it partakes of abundant airy substance in its mixture throughout itself. The heavy earth, the more it is such, the purer is the earth it consists of. It is proper to earth not to melt when it comes near fire, as lead and tin, silver and gold do. So when you hear of argyritis or chrysitis or sideritis earth (for some so name the earths taken from mines), do not suppose that the silver or the gold or the iron is blended with the earth throughout. Rather, small particles mixed in lie alongside small particles of the earth: of gold in chrysitis, of silver in argyritis, of iron in sideritis. In the furnaces these are melted by the fire and come together with one another. In the same way the earth containing glass is sandy, for small flakes of such substance are often found chiefly in sand. Those experienced in these matters, on seeing such sands, know how much glass they can gather from them. Many small flakes of gold are also found in certain sands. But those occupied with these things do not extract the gold and the glass from every sand. They select, of course, those that yield the most at little expense, since spending much in the furnaces to obtain little of the collected substance seems unprofitable to them. For this reason, although many sands contain flakes of gold and glass, the experts in these matters do not resort to all of them. By the same reasoning they do not extract copper or silver or iron or tin or lead from every earth either. Nor, when they have separated each of the metals mentioned from the earth mixed with it, is what remains like the other earths. These, as I said, are customarily so named by all Greeks, and they have as their common sign that they are soaked and readily dissolve into mud. For the residue of the earth in mines becomes stony, both unmeltable and unsoakable. By ‘being soaked’ (tengesthai) I mean becoming moist through the whole substance, and by ‘being wetted’ (brechesthai) becoming moist on the outer surface only, the moisture not penetrating into the depth. In this way mined cadmia too is produced, being stony and unsoakable. But about such bodies we shall speak next. Now I shall return to medicinal earth. Because it readily dissolves into mud when soaked in water, it is called earth; and because we have need of it as of other drugs, it might reasonably be called pharmakitis. Yet some call only the earth named ampelitis pharmakitis, either as being the only earth of this sort or as having the more active medicinal power, which is in fact true. It is called ampelitis not because it is better to plant vines (ampeloi) in it, but because, smeared around the vines, it destroys the worms that are generated on them, which our vine-dressers call sknipes. These are generated at the onset of spring, when the vines bud, together with that swelling part of them from which the shoot grows, which they call the ‘eye’. By gnawing through these eyes the sknipes do the vines no small harm, which is why the experts in these matters smear around the bases of these eyes. It is on this account that such earth is called ampelitis and pharmakitis, and by destroying the sknipes it shows how much medicinal power it possesses. It stands far apart from the other kinds of earth which we use for cures, coming already close to the stony. Hence you will find it mixed into the recipes of drugs where it is fitting to dry and disperse something. For it does not have the non-biting, moderate and soothing character that Chian, Samian and Selinusian earth have. It has also been said before about Kimolian earth that it is a little stronger than these, but is itself still non-biting, especially if it is washed. Cretan earth is also somewhat similar to these, but it is quite weak, having much of the airy. It does, however, have the cleansing quality, which is why people brighten dirty silverware with it. So you may use this one too for the purposes for which we said earlier that the other non-bitingly cleansing earths are suitable. Lemnian earth has a stronger power than all of these, for some astringency also belongs to it. Eretrian earth is stronger still than Lemnian, though not so strong as to bite. If it is washed, it becomes quite moderate, like those mentioned before. This earth can be washed not once only but also twice or three times, as Kimolian can. Moreover, some burn it, thereby making it more fine-particled and more pungent, so that it passes over to the dispersing power. If it is washed after being burnt, it leaves its pungency behind in the water while keeping the fine-particled quality from the roasting, and so becomes more drying. Hence, since by the general account all unburnt earth is useful for ulcers, the earth washed after burning is still more useful, and serves also for ulcers that are hard to flesh and hard to cicatrize. Of its two kinds, the one ash-coloured in hue is better than the very white one. There is also another earth called pnigitis, resembling Kimolian in its whole power but differing in colour. For it is black like ampelitis, and it has the viscid and glutinous quality no less than Samian earth, and sometimes even more. During this great plague there was also given to us another earth, from the Armenia that borders Cappadocia, more drying and pale yellow in colour. The man who gave it called it a stone, not an earth, and it is very easily levigated, like quicklime (titanos); by this name I mean, of course, burnt rock. And just as nothing sandy is carried in quicklime, so nothing sandy is carried in the Armenian earth either. After it has been crushed with the pestle in the mortar, it is as smooth and free of stone as quicklime and the Samian star, though not as light as the star. Hence it is also more condensed than the star and less airy, and for this reason it gives those who look at it carelessly the impression of being a stone. For present purposes it makes no difference whether we call it stone or earth, so long as we know that it dries to the highest degree. For it is most fitting for dysenteries and fluxes of the belly, for spitting of blood and catarrh, and in addition for the putrefactive ulcers in the mouth. It also greatly benefits those who have a flux from the head to the chest, so that it strongly helps those who are continually short of breath from such a cause. It also benefits those who suffer from phthoē, for it dries their ulcer so that they no longer cough. This holds unless they err considerably in their regimen, or the ambient air suddenly changes to a bad mixture. We have often observed fistulas, not only in other parts but also at the anus, being drawn together and closed by the drying drug alone, without inserting a collyrium to remove the filth or the callus of the fistula. It seems to me that the same thing happens with the ulcer in the lung. For this too appears to be helped in the same way by drying drugs, when it is moderate and not very large. As a result, some of those who had such ulcers seemed to be completely rid of them. Of those who travelled from Rome to Libya for this reason, some were fully convinced that they were healthy and lived without complaint for some years. Then at some later time, when they lived more carelessly, a relapse of the disease occurred. These people, as I said, the Armenian clod (bōlos) plainly helped, even though they were living in Rome, and it helped still more those continually short of breath. In this great plague, similar in form to the one that occurred in Thucydides' time, all who drank this drug were quickly cured. All whom it did not help died, nor were they helped by anything else, which also makes it clear that it failed to help only those who were incurable. It is drunk with wine of thin consistency, mixed moderately if the person is entirely without fever or only slightly feverish, but very watery if he is more feverish. Plague fevers, however, are not vehement in their heat. As for ulcers that need drying, what need is there even to say how great a power this Armenian clod has? As I said, you may call it a stone, as the man who gave it named it, or an earth, as I would say, since it is soaked by liquids.

9.2.1 Περὶ λίθων. [α΄. Περὶ ἐνεργουσῶν ποιοτήτων τῶν λίθων.] Τῶν αὐτοφυῶν σωμάτων εἰσὶ καὶ αἱ λίθοι, διαφέρουσαι τῆς συνήθως ὀνομαζομένης γῆς τῷ μὴ τέγγεσθαι. δυνάμεις δ’ ἔχουσι τὰς μὲν κατὰ τὴν ἰδιότητα τῆς ὅλης οὐσίας, τὰς δὲ κατὰ τὰς δραστικὰς ποιότητας. ὅπως δ’ ἀλλήλων διαφέρει ταῦτα πρόσθεν εἴρηται. νῦν οὖν ὁ λόγος ἡμῖν ἔστω περὶ τῶν κατὰ τὰς δραστικὰς ποιότητας ἐνεργουσῶν, ἐν αἷς ἐστι καὶ ἡ τῆς χρήσεως αὐτῆς μέθοδος. ἐδείκνυντο γὰρ αἱ κατὰ τὴν ἰδιότητα τῆς ὅλης. οὐσίας δυνάμεις ἀμέθοδοί τ’ εἶναι καὶ ἄλογοι καὶ δι’ ἐμπειρίας μόνης γινωσκόμεναι. διὰ τί γὰρ ἥδε ἡ λίθος ἁψαμένη τραύματος αἱμοῤῥαγοῦντος ἐπέχει τὴν φορὰν οὐκ ἴσμεν· διὰ τὶ μέντοι τὸν αἱματίτην καλούμενον λίθον ἐμβάλλουσιν ὀφθαλμικαῖς δυνάμεσιν ἴσμεν, ἔστι γὰρ εὕρημα λόγου τὸ τοιοῦτον. ἐὰν γοῦν ἐπ’ ἀκόνης ὀφθαλμικῆς ἀποτρίψας μεθ’ ὕδατος αὐτὸν εἰς πάχος μέλιτος ἐθελήσῃς γεύσασθαι, στυφούσης αἰσθήσῃ δυνάμεως. ἔμαθες δ’ ὅτι τὰς αὐξανομένας ἔτι φλεγμονὰς, καὶ μάλιστα ἐν νευρώδεσι μορίοις, ἀποκρούεσθαι δεῖ τοῖς στύφουσι φαρμάκοις. ὅταν δὲ μηκέτ’ ἐπιῤῥέῃ τὸ αἷμα τῷ μέρει, προσμιγνύναι τοῖς στύφουσιν τὰ διαφοροῦντα, καὶ κατὰ βραχύ γε μεταβαίνειν ἐπ’ αὐτὰ μόνα. περὶ μὲν οὖν τῶν τοιούτων δυνάμεων ἐν ταῖς λίθοις ἐροῦμεν, ἀναμνήσαντες πρότερον ὧν ἐπὶ πλεῖστον διήλθομεν ἐν τοῖς πρὸς τοὺς ἐπιτιμῶντας τοῖς σολοικίζουσιν τῇ φωνῇ. τούτων γὰρ ἔνιοι λίθον ἀῤῥενικῶς οὐκ ἐπιτρέπουσι λέγειν, ἀλλ’ ἐὰν εἴπῃς, ἔμβαλλον τὸν λίθον ἐπὶ τὸν κύνα, κεκράγασιν ὡς αὐτοὶ πεπληγμένοι λίθῳ τὴν κεφαλήν· ὥσπερ γε καὶ ἂν τὸν δρῦν εἴπῃ τις, ὡς ξύλῳ πεπληγότος ἐκβοῶσιν. ἐὰν οὖν ἐκείνοις τις πειθόμενος τὰ συνήθη τοῖς ἰατροῖς ὀνόματα μεταῤῥυθμίζῃ, τὴν λίθον λέγων τὴν αἱματῖτιν καὶ τὴν πυρῖτιν καὶ τὴν γαλακτῖτιν καὶ τὴν σχιστὴν, ὡς περίεργός τε καὶ παράσημος καταγνωσθήσεται, ﻿ πάντων ἐξ ἔθους ἤδη παλαιῶν λεγόντων ἀῤῥενικῶς τὰς διαφορὰς αὐτῶν, αἱματίτην, πυρίτην, γαλακτῖτην, μελιτίτην, γαγάτην, σχιστὸν, Φρύγιον, Ἀράβιον, Μεμφίτην. ἔμπαλιν δὲ τὴν πέτραν λέγουσιν θηλυκῶς, οὐ τὸν πέτρον ἀῤῥενικῶς. Ἀσίαν οὖν πέτραν ὀνομάζουσιν, οὐκ Ἄσιον πέτρον, τὸν λίθον Ἄσιον. οὕτως δὲ καὶ Ἀσίας πέτρας ἄνθος, ἐκ πέτρας δὲ κεκαυμένης φασὶ γίνεσθαι τὴν τίτανον. ὅ γε μὴν Ταραντῖνος Ἡρακλείδης καὶ ἄλλοι τινὲς οὐκ ἐκ πάσης λίθου φασὶ γίγνεσθαι τὴν τίτανον, ἀλλ’ ἐκ μόνης γε τῆς πέτρας, ὀνομάζοντες οὕτως κατ’ ἐξοχὴν τῶν λίθων μίαν τὴν σκληροτάτην. ἐγὼ γοῦν ἐξεπίτηδες εἴωθα, μεταβάλλων τὰ ὀνόματα, λέγειν ἑκατέρως ἅπαντα τὰ τοιαῦτα, περὶ ὧν ἀχρήστως ἐρίζουσιν ἔνιοι, δεικνὺς ἔργῳ μηδὲν βλαπτομένην τὴν σαφήνειαν τῆς ἑρμηνείας, ὁποτέρως ἄν τις εἴπῃ. θαυμαστὸν δ’ οὐδὲν τοὺς ἀγνοοῦντας ὅτι τῆς κατὰ φύσιν ἑρμηνείας ἤτοι μίαν μόνην ἀρετὴν θετέον εἶναι τὴν σαφήνειαν, ἢ πρώτην γε τῶν ἄλλων ἁπασῶν καὶ ἀρίστην, εἰς τοσούτους λήρους ἐκτρέπεσθαι. περὶ πρώτων οὖν ἐρῶ λίθων ὅσοι παρατριβόμενοι θυίαις ἢ ἀκόναις εἰς χυλὸν ἀναλύονται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πεπληγότος, παράσημος, ἀκόνης ὀφθαλμικῆς
9.2.1 On stones. [1. On the active qualities of stones.] Among the naturally occurring bodies are also the stones, which differ from what is customarily called earth in that they are not soaked through. They have powers, some in virtue of the peculiar character of their whole substance, others in virtue of their active qualities; how these differ from one another has been stated earlier. Now, then, let our account be of those which act in virtue of their active qualities, in which there also lies the method of their actual use. For the powers in virtue of the peculiar character of the whole substance were shown to be without method and without reason, and to be known by experience alone. For why this stone, on touching a haemorrhaging wound, checks the flow we do not know; but why they put the stone called haematites into ophthalmic remedies we do know, for such a thing is a discovery of reason. If, at any rate, having rubbed it down on an ophthalmic whetstone with water to the thickness of honey, you choose to taste it, you will perceive an astringent power. And you have learned that inflammations that are still increasing, and especially those in sinewy parts, must be repelled with astringent drugs; but when the blood no longer flows into the part, one should mix the discutient drugs with the astringent ones, and little by little pass over to the former alone. We shall speak, then, about such powers in stones, having first recalled what we went through at greatest length in the writings against those who censure people who commit solecisms in speech. For some of these do not permit one to say lithos in the masculine, but if you say "throw ton lithon at the dog", they shriek as though they themselves had been struck on the head with a stone; just as, if someone says ton dryn ("the oak") in the masculine, they cry out as though he had struck them with a piece of wood. If, then, someone, obeying them, remodels the names customary among physicians, saying hē lithos hē haimatitis and hē pyritis and hē galaktitis and hē schistē, he will be condemned as fussy and as using counterfeit terms, since all, from a custom already of long standing, name their varieties in the masculine: haematites, pyrites, galactites, melitites, gagates, schistos, Phrygian, Arabian, Memphite. Conversely they say hē petra ("rock") in the feminine, not ho petros in the masculine. Thus they name it Asian petra, not Asios petros, but Asian lithos (ton lithon Asion). So too "flower of Asian petra", and they say that lime comes from burnt petra. Heraclides of Tarentum, however, and certain others say that lime comes not from every stone but only from petra, naming in this way, par excellence, one of the stones, the hardest. I, at any rate, am accustomed deliberately, varying the names, to say all such things in both ways—things about which some people dispute uselessly—showing in practice that the clarity of the expression is in no way harmed, whichever way one says it. And it is no wonder that those who do not know that clarity must be posited as either the one and only virtue of natural expression, or at any rate the first and best of all the others, turn aside into such nonsense. I shall speak, then, first about those stones which, when rubbed on mortars or whetstones, dissolve into a juice.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παράσημος, οὐκ Ἄσιον πέτρον, τὸν λίθον Ἄσιον, ἐν τοῖς πρὸς τοὺς ἐπιτιμῶντας τοῖς σολοικίζουσιν τῇ φωνῇ, Περὶ ἐνεργουσῶν ποιοτήτων τῶν λίθων
9.2.1 On stones. [1. On the stones that act by their qualities.] Among naturally occurring bodies are also the stones (hai lithoi, feminine), which differ from what is customarily called earth in not being soaked through. They have powers, some according to the specific property of their whole substance, others according to their active qualities. How these differ from one another has been stated before. Now, then, let our discussion be about those that act according to their active qualities, in which the method of their use itself also lies. For it was shown that the powers according to the specific property of the whole substance are without method and without reason, and are known by experience alone. For why this particular stone, when it touches a bleeding wound, checks the flow, we do not know; but why they put the stone called haematites into ophthalmic preparations we do know, for such a thing is a discovery of reason. At any rate, if you rub it down with water on an ophthalmic whetstone to the thickness of honey and choose to taste it, you will perceive an astringent power. And you have learned that inflammations that are still increasing, especially in sinewy parts, must be repelled with astringent drugs; and that when the blood no longer flows into the part, one must mix discutient drugs with the astringent ones, and pass over gradually to these alone. About such powers in stones, then, we shall speak, having first recalled what we went through at very great length in the work against those who censure people who commit solecisms in speech. For some of these do not permit one to say lithos in the masculine; but if you say 'throw the stone (ton lithon) at the dog', they cry out as though they themselves had been struck on the head with a stone; just as, if someone says ton dryn ('the oak', in the masculine), they shout as though they had been struck with a piece of wood. If, then, someone persuaded by them should remodel the names customary among physicians, saying hē lithos haimatitis, pyritis, galaktitis and schistē, he will be condemned as over-fastidious and counterfeit, since all, from what is by now an old custom, name their varieties in the masculine: haematites, pyrites, galactites, melitites, gagates, schistos, Phrygian, Arabian, Memphite. Conversely, they say petra in the feminine, not petros in the masculine. Thus they speak of the 'Asian petra', not the 'Asian petros', while the stone (ton lithon) they call 'Asian' in the masculine. So also 'flower of Asian rock (petra)'; and they say that quicklime comes from burnt rock (petra). Heraclides of Tarentum, however, and certain others say that quicklime comes not from every stone but only from petra, using this name by pre-eminence for the single hardest of the stones. I, at any rate, am in the habit of deliberately varying the names and saying in both ways all such things about which some people quarrel uselessly, showing in practice that the clarity of the expression is in no way harmed, whichever way one says it. It is no wonder that those who do not know that clarity must be set down either as the one and only virtue of natural expression, or at least as the first and best of all the others, are diverted into such nonsense. First, then, I shall speak about those stones which, when rubbed on mortars or whetstones, dissolve into a juice.

9.2.2 [β΄. Περὶ αἱματίτου, γαλακτίτου καὶ μελιτίτου.] εἷς δέ τίς ἐστι τῶν τοιούτων καὶ ὁ καλούμενος αἱματίτης, ἀπὸ τῆς κατὰ τὴν χρόαν ὁμοιότητος οὕτως ὠνομασμένος, ὥσπερ γε καὶ ὁ γαλακτίτης, ἐπειδὴ κᾀκεῖνος εἰς χυλὸν λυθεὶς ὅμοιος φαίνεται γάλακτι. παραπλήσιον δ’ αὐτῷ κατὰ τὴν χρόαν τὸν χυλὸν ἐργαζόμενος ὁ μελιτίτης, ἀπὸ τῆς κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν ὁμοιότητος οὕτως ὠνόμασται. δέδεικται δ’ ἐν τῷ τετάρτῳ τῶνδε τῶν ὑπομνημάτων ὁ μὲν γλυκὺς ἅπας χυλὸς ὑπὸ συμμέτρου θερμασίας τοιοῦτος γινόμενος, ὁ δὲ στύφων ὑπὸ ψύξεως. ἕξει τοιγαροῦν ὁ μὲν αἱματίτης λίθος τοσοῦτον τῆς ψύξεως, ὅσον καὶ τῆς στύψεως, ὁ δὲ μελιτίτης σύμμετρον θερμότητα, μέσην δ’ ἀμφοῖν κρᾶσιν ὁ γαλακτίτης. εἰκότως οὖν ἐτόλμησαν οἱ ἰατροὶ ταῖς ὀφθαλμικαῖς δυνάμεσιν μιγνύναι τὸν αἱματίτην λίθον. καὶ μόνῳ δ’ ἂν αὐτῷ χρήσαιο τραχέων γεγονότων βλεφάρων, εἰ μὲν ἅμα φλεγμονὴ τοιαῦτ’ εἴη γεγονότα, δι’ ὠοῦ τὴν ἄνεσιν ποιούμενος, ἢ μᾶλλον ἔτι διὰ τήλεως ἀφεψήματος· εἰ δὲ χωρὶς φλεγμονῆς, δι’ ὕδατος. ἄρχου δ’ ἀεὶ μὲν ἀπὸ μετρίως ὑγροῦ διὰ τῆς μήλης ἐγχέων, ὅταν δ’ ὅτι φέρει τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ θεάσῃ, παχύτερον ἀεὶ καὶ μᾶλλον ἐργάζου. καὶ τελευτῶν οὕτω παχὺ ποιήσεις ὡς διὰ μήλης πυρῆνος ὑπαλείφεν καθ’ ὑποβολὴν ἢ ἐκτροπὴν τοῦ βλεφάρου. ὁ δ’ αὐτὸς οὗτος λίθος ὁμοίως ἐπ’ ἀκόνης ἀποτριβόμενος αἵματος πτύσεσιν ἁρμόττει καὶ πᾶσιν ἕλκεσιν. ξηρὸς δὲ λειωθεὶς ὡς χνοώδης γενέσθαι καταστέλλει τὰ ὑπερσαρκοῦντα. χρῆται μὲν οὖν οὐδεὶς αὐτῷ μόνῳ καθ’ αὑτόν. ἐγὼ δ’ ἐφ’ ὧν εἶπον ἐχρησάμην, ἐκ τῆς κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν ποιότητος εὑρὼν τὴν δύναμιν, εἶτα τῇ πείρᾳ βασανίσαι βουληθεὶς εἰ καλῶς ἐτεκμηράμην. καὶ αὐτὰ δὲ τὰ κατὰ τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς ἕλκη συνουλοῖ μόνος αὐτὸς, ἀποτριβόμενος μὲν, ὡς ὀλίγον ἔμπροσθεν εἶπον, ἤτοι δ’ ἐγχυματιζόμενος ἢ ὑπαλειφόμενος. ἐβασάνισα γὰρ καὶ τοῦτο τῇ πείρᾳ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄνεσιν, ὑπαλείφεν, καθ’ ὑποβολὴν, ὅτι φέρει τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ
9.2.2 [2. On haematites, galactites and melitites.] One among stones of this kind is also the one called haematites, so named from its resemblance in colour, just as also the galactites, since that one too, when dissolved into a juice, appears similar to milk. And the melitites, which produces a juice resembling it in colour, has been so named from its resemblance in taste. Now it has been shown in the fourth of these commentaries that every sweet juice becomes such through a moderate heat, while the astringent one does so through cooling. Accordingly the haematites stone will have as much of coldness as it has of astringency, the melitites a moderate heat, and the galactites a mixture intermediate between both. Reasonably, then, physicians have ventured to mix the haematites stone into ophthalmic medicaments. And you may use it by itself as well when the eyelids have become rough: if they have become so together with inflammation, making the dilution with egg, or better still with a decoction of fenugreek; if without inflammation, with water. Always begin from a moderately liquid form, pouring it in by means of the probe; but when you see that it tolerates its power, make it ever thicker and more so. And in the end you will make it so thick as to anoint with the tip of a probe, with the eyelid turned under or turned out. This same stone, rubbed down in the same way on a whetstone, is suited to spittings of blood and to all ulcers. Ground dry so as to become powdery, it reduces overgrowths of flesh. No one, however, uses it alone by itself. But I have used it in the cases I have mentioned, having discovered its power from its quality in respect of taste, and then wishing to test by experience whether I had inferred rightly. And the very ulcers on the eyes it alone brings to cicatrization, being rubbed down, as I said a little before, and either instilled or applied beneath the lid as a salve. For I tested this too by experience.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑπαλείφεν (read as ὑπαλείφειν), πυρῆνος, καθ’ ὑποβολὴν ἢ ἐκτροπὴν, ὅτι φέρει τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ (subject of φέρει unexpressed; taken as the affected part), χνοώδης, ἄνεσιν
9.2.2 [2. On haematites, galactites and melitites.] One of such stones is also the so-called haematites, named thus from its likeness in colour, just as galactites is too, since it also, when dissolved into a juice, appears like milk. Melitites, which produces a juice close to the latter's in colour, has been so named from its likeness in taste. It has been shown in the fourth of these commentaries that every sweet juice becomes such through moderate warmth, and the astringent juice through cooling. Accordingly the haematites stone will have as much of cooling as of astringency, melitites a moderate heat, and galactites a mixture intermediate between both. With good reason, then, the physicians have ventured to mix the haematites stone into ophthalmic preparations. You could also use it alone when the eyelids have become rough: if they have become so together with inflammation, making the dilution with egg, or better still with a decoction of fenugreek; if without inflammation, with water. Always begin by instilling it moderately fluid with the probe, and when you see that the part bears its power, make it ever thicker and thicker. And in the end you will make it so thick as to smear it on with the knob of the probe, by placing it under the eyelid or by everting the eyelid. This same stone, rubbed down in the same way on a whetstone, is suited to spittings of blood and to all ulcers. Ground dry so as to become fine as down, it represses fleshy overgrowths. Now no one uses it alone by itself; but I used it in the cases I have mentioned, having discovered its power from its quality in taste, and then wishing to test by experience whether I had inferred correctly. And by itself alone it also cicatrises the ulcers in the eyes themselves, being rubbed down, as I said a little before, and either instilled or smeared on. For I tested this too by experience.

9.2.3 [γ΄. Περὶ σχιστοῦ καὶ ἄλλων πολλῶν λίθων.] Τούτῳ δὲ τῷ λίθῳ παραπλησίαν μὲν, ἀσθενεστέραν δὲ τὴν δύναμιν ὁ σχιστὸς καλούμενος ἔχει, καὶ μετ’ αὐτὸν ὁ γαλακτίτης. ὁ δὲ μελιτίτης, ὡς ἔφην, ἤδη τι καὶ θερμότητος προσείληφεν. ἕκαστος οὖν αὐτῶν ὁ ἀποχωρῶν κατὰ βραχὺ τῆς τοῦ αἱματίτου δυνάμεως ἐπὶ μὲν τῶν ὀφθαλμῶν ὁμοίως εἰς χρῆσιν ἄγεται, πρᾳότερος δέ ἐστιν· ἀεὶ δὲ τὰ πρᾳότερα φάρμακα τοῖς μὲν ἔτι φλεγμαίνουσι μορίοις ἐστὶ προσηνέστερα, τοῖς δ’ ἀποφλεγμήνασιν μορίοις ἀσθενέστερα πρὸς τὸ τέλος ἐξυγιάσαι. κοινοῦ δὲ τοῦ ξηραίνειν ἅπασι τοῖς λίθοις ὑπάρχοντος, ὅσοι μὲν αὐτῶν ἢ εἰς χυλὸν λυθέντες ἢ ἄλλως λειωθέντες οὐδεμίαν ἐν ἀρχῇ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν ἔχουσι ποιότητα, τούτους μὲν ἡγητέον ἀσθενεστάτους τ’ εἶναι καὶ προσηνεστάτους καὶ ἀδηκτοτάτους. ὅσοι δὲ φαίνονταί τινα σαφῆ ποιό τητα κατὰ γεῦσιν ἐνδεικνύμενοι, τῆς ποιότητος ἀνάλογον ἔχουσι τὴν ἰσχύν· εἰ μὲν στύφοιεν, εἰς τὸ καταστεῖλαι καὶ σφίγξαι καὶ πιλῆσαι καὶ συναγαγεῖν, εἰ δὲ δάκνοιεν, εἰς τὸ θερμῆναί τε καὶ διαφορῆσαι καὶ τῆξαι, μέσοι δ’ αὐτῶν εἰσὶν οἱ ῥύπτοντες μόνον ἄνευ τοῦ στύφειν ἢ δάκνειν. ὁ μὲν οὖν αἱματίτης καὶ ὁ σχιστὸς, ὅ τε γαλακτίτης καὶ ὁ μελιτίτης εἰς χυλὸν ἀναλύονται, παρατριβόμενοι, καθάπερ εἶπον, ἀκόναις τε καὶ θυίαις, ὄντων καὶ ἄλλων εἰς χυλὸν λυομένων λίθων, ὥσπερ οὗτος ὁ κατ’ Αἴγυπτον γεννώμενος, ᾧ χρῶνται στιλπνοῦντες τὰς ὀθόνας. ἔστι δ’ ἀποιότερος τῶν εἰρημένων, οὔτε στύψιν οὔτε ῥύψιν οὔτε δῆξιν ἐμφαίνων. ὅθεν αὐτὸ δὴ τοῦτο μόνον ὑπάρχει τῷ λίθῳ τούτῳ τὸ ξηραίνειν, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο κηρωτῇ μιγνύντες αὐτὸν εἰς ἐπούλωσιν ἑλκῶν ἐπὶ τῶν μαλακῶν σωμάτων χρῶνται. μίγνυνται δὲ καὶ ταῖς ὀφθαλμικαῖς δυνάμεσιν ὁμοίως τοῖς προειρημένοις. ὅσῳ δ’ ἐστὶ μαλακτικώτερος αὐτῶν διὰ τὸ μηδεμίαν ἔχειν δραστικὴν ποιότητα, τοσούτῳ μετριώτερός τε καὶ ἀναισθητότερος ὑπάρχει. τοῦτον τὸν λίθον ἔνιοι μὲν μόροξον, ἔνιοι δὲ γραφίδα καλοῦσιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μόροξον, ἀναισθητότερος
9.2.3 [3. On schistos and many other stones.] The stone called schistos has a power similar to this stone but weaker, and after it the galactites. The melitites, as I said, has already taken on something of heat as well. Each of them, then, departing little by little from the power of the haematites, is brought into use for the eyes in the same way, but is milder; and milder drugs are always gentler for parts that are still inflamed, but weaker, for parts whose inflammation has subsided, with respect to healing them completely. Since drying is common to all stones, those of them which, whether dissolved into a juice or otherwise ground fine, have at the outset no quality upon tasting, these one must consider to be the weakest, the gentlest and the least biting. But those which appear to display some distinct quality upon tasting have a strength proportionate to the quality: if they are astringent, toward repressing, constricting, compacting and drawing together; if biting, toward heating, dispersing and melting; and intermediate between these are those which only cleanse, without being astringent or biting. Now the haematites and the schistos, the galactites and the melitites are dissolved into a juice when rubbed, as I said, on whetstones and in mortars, there being also other stones that are dissolved into a juice, such as this one produced in Egypt, which they use for glossing linen cloths. It is more devoid of quality than those mentioned, exhibiting neither astringency nor cleansing nor biting. Hence this alone belongs to this stone, namely drying, and for this reason, mixing it with cerate, they use it for the cicatrization of ulcers on soft bodies. It is also mixed into ophthalmic preparations in the same way as those mentioned before. But to the degree that it is more softening than they, because it has no active quality, to that degree it is more moderate and less perceptible. Some people call this stone moroxos, others graphis.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μαλακτικώτερος, ἀναισθητότερος, ἐν ἀρχῇ, μόροξον, στιλπνοῦντες, ταῖς ὀφθαλμικαῖς δυνάμεσιν, μίγνυνται
9.2.3 [3. On schistos and many other stones.] A power similar to that of this stone, but weaker, is possessed by the stone called schistos, and after it by galaktites. Melitites, as I said, has already taken on something of heat as well. Each of them, then, departing little by little from the power of haematites, is likewise brought into use for the eyes, but is milder. Milder drugs are always gentler for parts that are still inflamed, but weaker for bringing to complete health parts that have ceased to be inflamed. Drying is common to all stones. Those of them which, whether dissolved into a juice or pounded fine in some other way, have at first no quality perceptible to the taste must be considered the weakest, the gentlest and the least biting. Those which appear to display some clear quality to the taste have a strength proportionate to that quality. If they are astringent, their strength is for repressing, constricting, compacting and drawing together. If they are biting, it is for heating, dispersing and melting. Intermediate between them are those that are only cleansing, without being astringent or biting. Now haematites and schistos, galaktites and melitites dissolve into a juice when rubbed, as I said, on whetstones and in mortars. There are also other stones that dissolve into a juice, such as the one produced in Egypt, which people use to glaze linen cloths. This stone is more devoid of quality than those already mentioned, displaying neither astringency nor cleansing nor biting. Hence this very thing alone belongs to this stone, namely drying. For this reason they mix it with cerate and use it for the cicatrization of ulcers in soft bodies. Such stones are also mixed into ophthalmic preparations, in the same way as those mentioned before. By as much as it is more softening than they are, through having no active quality, by so much it is more moderate and produces less sensation. Some people call this stone moroxos, others graphis.

9.2.4 [δ΄. Περὶ τοῦ Διοσκουρίδου θυΐτου.] Ἁπάντων δὲ τῶν εἰρημένων λίθων ἰσχυροτέραν ἔχει δύναμιν ὁ ἐκ τῆς Αἰθιοπίας κομιζόμενος, ὑπόχλωρός πως, ὡς ἴασπις ὀνομαζόμενος. ἀνιέμενος οὗτος ὁ λίθος εἰς χυλὸν γαλακτώδη κατὰ τὴν χρόαν δακνώδη τοῖς γενομένοις, καὶ διὰ τοῦτ’ αὐτὴν οὐδεὶς ἔμιξεν ﻿ ὀφθαλμικαῖς δυνάμεσιν, οὔτ’ ἀποκρουστικαῖς ῥευμάτων οὔθ’ ἑλκῶν ἰατικαῖς, ἀλλὰ μόνον εἰς τὸ καθῆραί τε καὶ ῥύψαι τὰ χωρὶς φλεγμονῆς ἐπισκοτοῦντα ταῖς κόραις, ἐξ ὧν εἰσι καὶ αἱ πρόσφατοι τῶν οὐλῶν, ἃς λεπτύνειν πέφυκεν. ἀποῤῥύπτει δὲ καὶ τὰ μὴ πάνυ σκληρὰ τῶν πτερυγίων.

9.2.4 [4. On the thyites of Dioscorides.] Of all the stones that have been mentioned, the one brought from Ethiopia has a stronger power; it is somewhat greenish, being named as though it were iaspis. This stone, when dissolved into a juice milky in colour, is biting to those who taste it, and for this reason no one has mixed it into ophthalmic preparations, neither those repelling fluxes nor those curative of ulcers, but only for the purging and cleansing of the things that, without inflammation, cast a shadow over the pupils, among which are also the fresh scars, which it is of a nature to thin. It also cleanses away those of the pterygia that are not very hard.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς ἴασπις ὀνομαζόμενος (possibly corrupt for a name such as θυΐτης; cf. 9.2.4 [4. On the thyites of Dioscorides.] Stronger in power than all the stones mentioned is the one brought from Ethiopia, somewhat greenish, named as a kind of jasper. When this stone is dissolved, it yields a juice milky in colour and biting to those who taste it. For this reason no one has mixed it into ophthalmic preparations, neither those repelling fluxes nor those healing of ulcers. It is used only to purify and cleanse the things that, without inflammation, darken the pupils, among which are also the fresh scars, which it is of a nature to thin. It also cleanses away those pterygia that are not very hard.

9.2.5 [ε΄. Περὶ Ἰουδαϊκοῦ.] Ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἄλλος λίθος ἰσχυρότερος τὴν δύναμιν ἐν τῇ Παλαιστίνῃ Συρίᾳ γινόμενος, λευκὸς μὲν τὴν χρόαν, εὔρυθμος δὲ κατὰ τὸ σχῆμα καὶ γραμμὰς ἔχων ὡς ἀπὸ τόρνου γεγονυίας. ὀνομάζουσι δ’ αὐτὸν ἀπὸ τῆς χώρας ἐν ᾗ γεννᾶσθαι πέφυκεν Ἰουδαϊκὸν, καὶ χρῶνται πρὸς τοὺς ἐν τῇ κύστει λίθους, ἀνιέντες μὲν ἐπ’ ἀκόνης, ποτίζοντες δὲ δι’ ὕδατος θερμοῦ κυάθων τριῶν. ἡμεῖς δ’ ἐφ’ ὧν ἐπειράθημεν οὐδὲν ἤνυσεν ὡς πρὸς τοὺς ἐν κύστει λίθους, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν ἐν νεφροῖς συνιμένων ἐστὶ δραστήριος.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀνιέντες
9.2.5 [5. On the Judaic stone.] There is also another stone, stronger in its power, which occurs in Palestinian Syria, white in colour, well-proportioned in its shape and having lines as though made by a lathe. They name it, after the country in which it is naturally produced, Judaic, and they use it against the stones in the bladder, dissolving it on a whetstone and giving it to drink in three cyathi of hot water. For our part, in the cases in which we tried it, it accomplished nothing as regards the stones in the bladder, but against those that form in the kidneys it is active.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συνιμένων (apparently corrupt; translated as if συνισταμένων, 'forming'), εὔρυθμος, ἀνιέντες, κυάθων τριῶν (whether the three cyathi measure the water or the dose)
9.2.5 [5. On the Jewish stone.] There is also another stone, stronger in power, which occurs in Palestinian Syria. It is white in colour, well-proportioned in shape, and has lines as if made by a lathe. They name it 'Jewish' after the country in which it is naturally produced, and they use it against stones in the bladder, rubbing it down on a whetstone and giving it to drink in warm water, three cyathi. But in the cases in which we tested it, it achieved nothing as regards stones in the bladder; in the case of those forming in the kidneys, however, it is efficacious.

Greek (Kühn)
9.2.6 [στ΄. Περὶ πυρίτου.] Εἷς τῶν ἰσχυρὰν δύναμιν ἐχόντων ἐστὶν καὶ ὁ πυρίτης ὀνομαζόμενος, ᾧ χρώμεθα μιγνύντες ἐμπλάστρῳ διαφορητικῷ. προσεμβάλλεται δ’ αὐτῇ καὶ ὁ σχιστός. ὑπὸ τούτου τοῦ φαρμάκου καὶ πτύον μὲν πολλάκις, ἀλλὰ καὶ θρομβώδης σύστασις ἐν ταῖς μεταξὺ χώραις τῶν μυῶν γενομένη διεφορήθη. κεκόφθαι δὲ καὶ λελειῶσθαι πάνυ σφόδρα χρὴ τοὺς λίθους ἅπαντας ὅταν εἰς χρῆσιν ἄγωνται, παραπλησίως τοῖς εἰς τὰς ὀφθαλμικὰς δυνάμεις μιγνυμένοις. εἰ μὴ γὰρ χνοώδεις γεννηθεῖεν ὡς εἰς τὸ βάθος καταδύεσθαι τῶν σωμάτων οἷς προσφέρονται, παραπλήσιοι μενοῦσι ταύταις ταῖς θαλαττίαις τε καὶ ποταμίαις ψάμμοις, αἵτινες καὶ αὐταὶ τὴν μὲν κοινὴν ἁπάντων λίθων δύναμιν ἔχουσιν. ξηραίνουσι γοῦν τὴν ὕποιδόν τε καὶ ὑδερώδη σάρκα, κατακλινομένων ἐν αὐταῖς τεθερμασμέναις τῶν καμνόντων. οὐ μὴν εἰς ἄλλο γέ τι χρώμεθα αὐταῖς, ὥσπερ τοῖς προειρημένοις εἰς ὀφθαλμικάς τε καὶ σταλτικὰς αἵματος καὶ ῥοῦ γυναικείου δυνάμεις, ἔτι τε κολλητικὸν ἑλκῶν, ἐπουλωτικόν τε καὶ σαρκωτικόν. ὅσοι γὰρ αὐτῶν οὐκ εἰσὶ δριμεῖς, εἰς ταῦτα πάντες εἰσὶ χρήσιμοι, καθάπερ οἱ δριμεῖς, ὧν ὕστερον ἐμνημόνευσα, πρὸς τὸ ῥύψαι τε καὶ ἀποσμῆξαι καὶ ἀφελεῖν, λεπτῦναί τε καὶ διαφορῆσαι καὶ σφοδρῶς ξηρᾶναι καὶ τῆξαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πτύον, ὧν ὕστερον ἐμνημόνευσα
9.2.6 [6. On pyrites.] One of those possessing a strong power is also the stone called pyrites, which we use by mixing it into a dispersing plaster. The schistos is also added to it. By this drug pus (πτύον) has often been dispersed, but also a clotted mass that had formed in the spaces between the muscles. All stones must be pounded and ground very fine indeed whenever they are brought into use, in the same way as those mixed into ophthalmic powers. For if they are not rendered dust-fine, so as to sink into the depth of the bodies to which they are applied, they will remain similar to these sea and river sands, which themselves too possess the power common to all stones. At any rate they dry the somewhat swollen and dropsical flesh, when the sick lie down in them after they have been heated. We do not, however, use them for anything else, as we use the aforementioned stones for ophthalmic powers and for those that staunch blood and the female flux, and further as an agglutinating remedy for ulcers, and as a scarring and flesh-forming one. For those of them that are not pungent are all useful for these purposes, just as the pungent ones, which I mentioned later, are useful for cleansing and scouring and removing, for thinning and dispersing and strongly drying and melting.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πτύον (as printed means 'winnowing-fan'; probably corrupt, read as πύον 'pus'), θρομβώδης σύστασις, ὕστερον ἐμνημόνευσα ('mentioned later': sense in context unclear), ἀφελεῖν, ὁ σχιστός, δυνάμεις (here rendered 'preparations')
9.2.6 [6. On pyrites.] One of those that have a strong power is also the stone named pyrites, which we use by mixing it into a dispersing plaster. The schistos is also added to it. By this drug often pus, but also a clotted (thrombus-like) consistency formed in the spaces between the muscles, has been dispersed. All the stones, when they are brought into use, must be pounded and ground very smooth indeed, in the same way as those mixed into ophthalmic preparations. For unless they are made as fine as down, so as to sink into the depth of the bodies to which they are applied, they will remain like these sea and river sands, which themselves also have the common power of all stones. At any rate they dry flesh that is somewhat swollen and dropsical, when the patients lie down in them after they have been heated. We do not, however, use them for anything else, as we use the stones mentioned before for ophthalmic preparations and preparations that check blood and the female flux, and further for what is agglutinating of ulcers, scarring and flesh-forming. For all of them that are not pungent are useful for these purposes, just as the pungent ones, which I mentioned later, are useful for cleansing, scouring off and removing, thinning, dispersing, drying vigorously and melting.

Greek (Kühn)
9.2.7 [ζ΄. Περὶ φρυγίου.] Ταύτης τῆς δυνάμεως ἔχεται καὶ ὁ φρύγιος καλούμενος. ἀεὶ δ’ αὐτῷ κεκαυμένῳ κέχρημαι, καὶ πρὸς μὲν τὰ σηπεδονώδη τῶν ἑλκῶν ἢ αὐτὸν καθ’ ἑαυτὸν ἢ δι’ ὄξους ἢ δι’ οἰνομέλιτος ἢ δι’ ὀξυκράτου. πρὸς ὀφθαλμοὺς δὲ ξηρὸν ποιῶν φάρμακον, ὃ πολλοὶ λαβόντες ἔχουσιν ἤδη παρ’ ἐμοῦ. μίγνυμι δὲ αὐτῷ καὶ ἄλλα τινά. λεχθήσεται δὲ περὶ αὐτοῦ κατὰ τὴν περὶ συνθέσεως τῶν φαρμάκων πραγματείαν. νυνὶ δ’ ἀρκεῖ περὶ τῆς καθόλου δεδηλῶσθαι δυνάμεως αὐτοῦ. ξηραίνει γὰρ ἰσχυρῶς, ἔχων τι καὶ στύψεως ἅμα καὶ δήξεως. εἴρηται δέ μοι κᾀν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν ἄριστά τε καὶ πολύχρηστα φάρμακα εἶναι πάντα τὰ μεμιγμένα, ἐν αὑτοῖς ἔχοντα τὰς δύο δυνάμεις, τήν τε ἀποκρουστικὴν καὶ διαφορητικήν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ποιῶν (πρὸς ὀφθαλμοὺς δὲ ξηρὸν ποιῶν φάρμακον — the participle lacks a finite verb; supplied from κέχρημαι)
9.2.7 [7. On phrygios.] The stone called phrygios also partakes of this power. I have always used it burnt, and against the putrefactive sort of ulcers either by itself or with vinegar or with honeyed wine or with oxykraton (vinegar-and-water); and for the eyes, making from it a dry drug, which many have already received from me and have in their possession. I also mix certain other things with it. It will be spoken of in the treatise on the composition of drugs; for now it suffices to have set out its power in general. For it dries strongly, having something of astringency together with biting. And I have said also in what precedes that the best and most widely useful drugs are all those of mixed character, having in themselves the two powers, the repelling and the dispersing.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φρύγιος, ἔχεται, μεμιγμένα
9.2.7 [7. On Phrygian stone.] The stone called Phrygian also possesses this power. I have always used it burnt, and for putrefactive ulcers either by itself on its own or with vinegar or with honeyed wine [oinomeli] or with oxycrate. For the eyes, I make a dry drug from it, which many people have already received from me and now possess. I also mix certain other things with it. This will be discussed in the treatise On the Composition of Drugs. For now it suffices to have set out its general power. For it dries strongly, having something of astringency and at the same time of biting. And I have also said in what precedes that all mixed drugs which contain within themselves the two powers, the repelling and the dispersing, are excellent and of many uses.

9.2.8 [η΄. Περὶ ἀγηράτου.] Ἔστι δὲ καὶ ὁ ἀγήρατος καλούμενος λίθος, ᾧ τοὺς σκυτοτόμους ὁρῶμεν χρωμένους, μικτῆς δ’ ἐστὶ δυνάμεως στυπτικῆς τε καὶ διαφορητικῆς, ἀλλὰ περὶ μόνης τῆς στυπτικῆς σκεπτέον. ὠφελεῖ μὲν ἐναργῶς γαργαρεῶνας φλεγμαίνοντας, οὐ μὴν γευομένοις γε σαφῆ στύψιν ἔχων ἢ δριμύτητα φαίνεται.

9.2.8 [8. On agēratos.] There is also the stone called agēratos, which we see the leather-workers using; it is of a mixed power, both astringent and dispersing, but only the astringent one needs to be considered. It manifestly benefits inflamed uvulas, yet to those who taste it it does not appear to have any clear astringency or pungency.

9.2.8 [8. On agēratos.] There is also the stone called agēratos, which we see shoemakers using. It is of a mixed power, astringent and dispersing, but we must consider only the astringent one. It manifestly benefits inflamed uvulas, yet to those who taste it it does not appear to have a clear astringency or pungency.

Greek (Kühn)
9.2.9 [θ΄. Περὶ Ἀσσίου.] Ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἄλλος λίθος ἐν Ἀσσῷ γεννώμενος, ὃν καὶ δι’ αὐτὸ τοῦτο καλοῦσιν Ἄσσιον, οὐ σκληρὸς ὡς αἱ πέτραι· πωροειδὴς γάρ ἐστι κατά τε χρόαν καὶ σύστασιν, εὔθρυπτός τε καὶ χαῦνος, ἐπιτρέφεται δ’ αὐτῷ παραπλήσιόν τι ἀλεύρῳ λεπτοτάτῳ, τῷ προσιζάνοντι τοῖς τοίχοις τῶν ἐν τοῖς μύλωσι διαττομένων ἀλεύρων. καλοῦσι μὲν οὖν τὸ φάρμακον τοῦτο πέτρας Ἀσίας ἄνθος. ἔστι δὲ λεπτομερὲς, ὡς ἀδήκτως τὰς πλαδαρὰς σάρκας ἐκτήκειν. ὁμοίαν δ’ αὐτῷ δύναμιν ἔχουσα καὶ ἡ πέτρα καθ’ ἣν γεννᾶται τῷ σφοδρῷ τῆς ἐνεργείας ἀπολείπεται. πλεονεκτεῖ γὰρ αὐτῆς τὸ ἄνθος οὐ μόνον τῷ μᾶλλον ἐκτήκειν καὶ διαφορεῖν καὶ ταριχεύειν, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῷ χωρὶς δήξεως ἰσχυρῶς ταῦτα ποιεῖν. ἔχει δέ τι καὶ ἁλμυρὸν ἐν τῇ γεύσει τουτὶ τὸ τῆς Ἀσίας πέτρας ἄνθος, ὡς εἰκάσαι τὴν γένεσιν ἴσχειν αὐτὸ, δρόσου τινὸς ἐκ θαλάττης ἐφιζούσης τῇ πέτρᾳ, κᾄπειτα ξηραινομένης ὑπὸ τοῦ ἡλίου.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πωροειδὴς, ταριχεύειν
9.2.9 [9. On the Assian stone.] There is also another stone generated at Assos, which for this very reason they call Assian; it is not hard like rocks, for it is porous-looking (πωροειδής) in both colour and consistency, friable and loose, and there grows upon it something resembling the finest flour, the kind that settles on the walls of the mills when flour is being sifted. They call this drug, then, flower of Asian stone. It is fine-particled, so as to melt away flabby flesh without biting. The rock on which it is generated, though it has a power similar to it, falls short of it in the vehemence of its activity; for the flower surpasses it not only in melting away, dispersing and desiccating (ταριχεύειν) more, but also in doing these things strongly without biting. This flower of Asian stone also has something salty in its taste, so that one may conjecture that it gets its generation from a kind of dew from the sea settling on the rock and then being dried by the sun.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πωροειδὴς, Ἀσίας (twice; perhaps a corruption of Ἀσσίας, 'Assian'), ταριχεύειν, διαττομένων, ἐπιτρέφεται
9.2.9 [9. On the Assian stone.] There is also another stone, produced in Assos, which for this very reason they call Assian. It is not hard as rocks are, for it is like poros-stone both in colour and in consistency, and it is friable and loose. Upon it there grows something closely resembling the finest flour, namely the flour that settles on the walls of the places in mills where flour is sifted. They call this drug 'flower of the Asian rock'. It is fine-particled, so that it melts down flabby flesh without biting. The rock on which it is produced has a power similar to it but falls short in the vehemence of its activity. For the flower surpasses it not only in melting down, dispersing and curing (as by salting) to a greater degree, but also in doing these things strongly without biting. This flower of the Asian rock also has something salty in the taste, so that one may conjecture that it gets its origin from some dew from the sea settling on the rock and then being dried by the sun.
Flags (places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors): none raised.
