9.3.23 [23. On lead.] Lead is of a cooling power. For it has not only much moist substance congealed by cold, but also airy substance, while it partakes of little earthy substance. That it has a very great amount of moist substance congealed by cold, let the melting and flowing that comes about in it quickly whenever it meets fire be your proof. That it also has airy substance, this is a sign: lead alone (μόνος μόνος, sic) of the things we know increases both in bulk and in weight when it is stored in underground chambers that have turbid air, such that the things lying in them are quickly filled with mould; and the leads that bind the feet of statues to their stones have often been seen to have grown. Some have even risen to so great a bulk that they hang down from the stones like ice. These, then, are plausible indications, prior to experience, of its moistness and coldness. But what is scientific and secure is known through experience. If, then, having prepared a mortar of lead, you put into it, together with a pestle of lead, whatever liquid you wish, and grind it so that the pestle and the lead give off some juice, you will find that what is produced from both is far colder in power than the liquid was. You may put in water, thin watery wine, oil and anything at all of that kind. If you wish to make the juice still more refrigerant, let the oil be omphacine oil, rose oil, quince oil or myrtle oil. Using the juice so produced for inflammations arising in the seat with ulcers or folds, and for those in the genitals, testicles and breasts, you will have an excellent drug. Likewise also against all the other fluxes when they are beginning, such as settle into buboes, the feet or the other joints, and moreover also for malignant ulcers, so that, using it for cancerous ones too, you will marvel at the drug. If you wish to collect a great deal of the juice of lead quickly, try grinding in the sun or in air that has become warm in any way whatever. The drug will be of many uses to you if you also grind it with some of the cooling juices thrown in, such as of houseleek, navelwort, chicory, lettuce, chondrilla, psyllium, unripe grape and purslane. Those of these that do not readily give off juice, as purslane, mix with some of the other liquids, such as that of the unripe grape, which, put by itself alone into the aforementioned mortar, also produces a most excellent refrigerant drug. But lead too, flattened out, without any of the others, by itself alone, becomes an underlay for the loins of athletes in training when they have nocturnal emissions, evidently cooling them. And on one occasion a thin sheet made of lead, properly bound on over a ganglion, made it disappear completely. Anyone will bind it on properly who has learned from Hippocrates to press more on the lesion than on either side. It is therefore no wonder that burnt lead too, when it has been washed, becomes of cooling power, while before it is washed it is clearly of mixed power. And this very drug, burnt lead, is good for ulcers that are turning malignant; but when it has been washed it becomes a far better drug, an excellent one, for both their filling-up and their cicatrization. It is suitable also for the so-called Chironian ulcers and for all cancerous ones, both by itself and mixed with some of the cicatrizing drugs, such as the one made with cadmia. One must undo the dressing at the beginning every day, if the ichor is copious; if not, every third or even every fourth day. Let a sponge from cold water lie on the outside, and whenever it dries, let it be moistened. These things, then, have been said at greater length than the present treatise requires, since I was led on by the affinity of the discussion. It is now time to pass to what follows.

9.3.24 [κδ΄. Περὶ ὀστράκου.] Ὄστρακον ξηραντικῆς τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ ῥυπτικῆς δυνάμεως, μάλιστα δὲ τὸ ἐκ τῶν κλιβάνων, τῷ κατωπτῆσθαι. διὸ καὶ τῶν ἐμπλάστρων ἣν ὀνομάζουσιν ﻿ ἡφαιστιάδα, διὰ τούτου σκευαζομένη κάλλιστον ἐπουλωτικὸν γίνεται φάρμακον.

9.3.24 [24. On potsherd.] Potsherd is of a drying and cleansing power, and most of all the potsherd from ovens, because of its having been thoroughly baked. For this reason too, among the plasters, the one which they call Hephaestias, being prepared with this, becomes an excellent scarring drug.

9.3.24 [24. On potsherd.] Potsherd is of a drying and cleansing power, especially that from the ovens (klibanoi), because it has been thoroughly baked. For this reason, among the plasters, the one they call hephaestias, when prepared with this, becomes a most excellent scarring drug.

Greek (Kühn)
9.3.25 [κε΄. Περὶ πομφόλυγος καὶ σποδίου.] Πομφόλυξ γίνεται μὲν καὶ κατὰ τὴν τοῦ χαλκοῦ καμινείαν, ὥσπερ καὶ ἡ καδμεία. γίνεται δὲ καὶ αὐτῆς τῆς καδμείας καμινευομένης καὶ κατά τε τὴν Κύπρον, ἐπειδὴ τὴν παρασκευὴν οὐκ εἶχεν εἰς τὴν τοῦ χαλκοῦ καμινείαν ὁ ἐπίτροπος, ἐκέλευσεν ἐξ αὐτῆς τῆς καδμείας σκευασθῆναι παρόντι καὶ θεωμένῳ πομφόλυγα, κατὰ σμικρὰ θραύματα τῆς καδμείας ἐπιβαλλομένης τῷ πυρὶ, προσκειμένῳ αὐτῷ δηλονότι χαλκευτικῆς φύσεως. ὄροφος δ’ ἦν τις στεγανὸς ὑποδεχόμενος τὸν ἀναφερόμενον αἴθαλον, καμινευομένης τῆς καδμείας, ὃν ἀθροίσας ἔσχον πομφόλυγα. τὸ δ’ ἀντικαταφερόμενον κάτω καὶ πῖπτον ἐπὶ τοὔδαφος ἡ καλουμένη σποδός ἐστι, πλείων ἀθροιζομένη κατὰ τὰς τοῦ χαλκοῦ καμινείας. ἔνιοι δὲ σπόδιον οὐδετέρως αὐτὴν ὀνομάζουσιν, ᾧ δοκεῖ παραπλησίαν δύναμιν ἔχειν τὸ καλούμενον ἀντισπόδιον. ἐγὼ δ’ οὐδέποτε ἐχρησάμην αὐτῷ διὰ τὸ δαψιλῆ ἔχειν ἀεί τις πομφόλυγα. ταύτην γὰρ ἔχων τις οὐδ’ ἂν τῷ σποδίῳ χρήσαιτο, μήτοι γε τῷ ἀντισποδίῳ. φάρμακον δ’ ἔστιν ὁ πομφόλυξ, εἰ πλυθείη, σχεδὸν ἁπάντων ὅσα ξηραίνειν ἀδήκτως πέφυκε προφερέστατον, ὅθεν εἴς τε τὰ καρκινώδη τῶν ἑλκῶν ἐστιν ἐπιτήδειος καὶ τἄλλα τὰ κακοήθη πάντα. μίγνυται τοιγαροῦν εἴς τε τὰ κατὰ τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς ῥεύματα συντιθέμενα κολλύρια καὶ ὅσα φλυκταίνας τὰς ἐν αὐτοῖς ἢ ἕλκη θεραπεύει, καὶ πρὸς τὰ κατὰ τὴν ἕδραν τε καὶ αἰδοῖον ἕλκη. καὶ κάλλιστόν ἐστι φάρμακον ἀδήκτως, ὡς ἔφην, ξηραῖνον. ἀλλ’ οὐ τῇς προκειμένης πραγματείας τὸ χρονίζειν ἐν τούτοις.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προσκειμένῳ αὐτῷ δηλονότι χαλκευτικῆς φύσεως, παρασκευὴν, ὄροφος στεγανὸς
9.3.25 [25. On pompholyx and spodion.] Pompholyx is produced also in the smelting of copper, just as cadmia is too. But it is produced also from cadmia itself when it is smelted; and in Cyprus, since the procurator did not have the equipment for the smelting of copper, he ordered pompholyx to be prepared from cadmia itself, while I was present and watching, the cadmia being thrown onto the fire in small fragments, with, evidently, something of the smith's kind (χαλκευτικῆς φύσεως, perhaps a smith's bellows) applied to it. And there was a kind of covered roof which received the soot that was carried upward as the cadmia was smelted; this I collected and had as pompholyx. But what is carried down in the opposite direction and falls onto the floor is what is called spodos, which is collected in greater quantity in the smeltings of copper. Some people name it in the neuter, spodion, and the so-called antispodion seems to have a power similar to it. I myself have never used the latter, because one always has pompholyx in abundance. For anyone who has this would not even use spodion, let alone antispodion. Pompholyx is a drug which, if it has been washed, is nearly the most excellent of all those which are by nature such as to dry without biting; hence it is suitable for the cancerous kinds of ulcers and all the other malignant ones. It is accordingly mixed into the collyria compounded for the fluxes of the eyes and into all those that treat the phlyctaenae in them or ulcers, and for the ulcers at the seat and the genitals. And it is the finest drug for drying without biting, as I said. But it is not the business of the present treatise to linger on these matters.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: προσκειμένῳ αὐτῷ δηλονότι χαλκευτικῆς φύσεως, ἔσχον, ἀεί τις, παρόντι καὶ θεωμένῳ, ὁ πομφόλυξ
9.3.25 [25. On pompholyx and spodium.] Pompholyx is produced in the smelting of copper, as cadmia also is. But it is also produced when cadmia itself is smelted. In Cyprus, for instance, since the overseer did not have the equipment for smelting copper, he ordered pompholyx to be prepared from the cadmia itself, in my presence and while I watched. The cadmia was thrown onto the fire in small fragments, and the fire evidently had something of a smithing nature applied to it. There was a closed roof that received the soot carried upward as the cadmia was smelted, and by gathering this I obtained pompholyx. What is carried back down and falls onto the floor is what is called spodos; a greater amount of it is collected in the smelting of copper. Some people call it spodium, in the neuter, and the so-called antispodium seems to have a power similar to it. I myself have never used it, because one always has pompholyx in abundance. For anyone who has this would not use spodium, still less antispodium. Pompholyx, if it is washed, is almost the most excellent drug of all those whose nature is to dry without biting. Hence it is suitable for cancerous ulcers and for all the other malignant ones. Accordingly it is mixed into the collyria compounded for fluxes in the eyes and for those that treat the phlyctaenae or ulcers in them, and into remedies for ulcers at the seat and the genitals. And it is a most excellent drug, drying, as I said, without biting. But to dwell on these matters does not belong to the present treatise.

9.3.28 [κη΄. Περὶ σκωρίας.] Σκωρία πᾶσα ξηραντικὸν ἱκανῶς ἐστι φάρμακον, ἡ δὲ τοῦ σιδήρου μάλιστα. καὶ λειοτάτην γε ποιῶν αὐτὴν ἐν ὄξει δριμυτάτῳ, καὶ μετὰ ταῦθ’ ἑψῶν εἴς τε τὰ πυοῤῥοῦντα τῶν ὤτων χρονίως χρῶμαι φαρμάκῳ ξηραντικωτάτῳ, ὥστε θαυμάζειν τοὺς ἰδόντας σκευαζόμενον αὐτὸ καὶ ἀπιστεῖν αὐτῷ πρὸ τῆς πείρας, εἰ ἀναδέχεσθαι δύναται τὰ ὦτα τοιούτου φαρμάκου. τὴν δὲ τοῦ ἀργυρίου σκωρίαν ἰδίως προσαγορεύουσιν ἕλκυσμα, καὶ μίγνυται δὲ καὶ ἐμπλάστροις τισὶ ξηραντικῆς δηλονότι δυνάμεως οὖσιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἕλκυσμα
9.3.28 [28. On slag.] All slag is a drug that is sufficiently drying, but that of iron most of all. And indeed, having made it very fine in the most pungent vinegar, and after this boiling it, I use it for ears that have been discharging pus for a long time as a most drying drug, so that those who see it being prepared marvel and disbelieve it before the trial, whether the ears can tolerate such a drug. The slag of silver they specifically call helkysma, and it is also mixed into certain plasters which are evidently of drying power.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἕλκυσμα
9.3.28 [28. On slag.] All slag is a considerably drying drug, but that of iron most of all. And indeed, grinding it very fine in the most pungent vinegar and after this boiling it, I use it for ears that discharge pus chronically as a most drying drug, so that those who saw it being prepared were amazed and distrusted it before the trial, as to whether the ears could tolerate such a drug. The slag of silver they call by the special name helkysma, and it is also mixed into certain plasters which are, evidently, of drying power.

9.3.30 [λ΄. Περὶ στυπτηρίας σχιστῆς καὶ στρογγύλης καὶ ὑγρᾶς.] Στυπτηρία. τούτου τοῦ φαρμάκου καὶ τοὔνομα παρωνόμασται τῇ στύψει, σφοδροτάτην γὰρ αὐτὴν ἔχει. παχυμεροῦς δ’ ὄντος αὐτοῦ, λεπτομερεστέρα πώς ἐστιν τῶν ἄλλων στυπτηριῶν ἡ σχιστὴ καλουμένη. μετ’ αὐτὴν δ’ ἡ στρογγύλη καὶ ἀστραγαλωτή. παχυμερὴς δ’ ἱκανῶς ἥ θ’ ὑγρὰ καὶ ἡ πλακῖτίς τε καὶ ἡ πλινθῖτις ὀνομαζομένη.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀστραγαλωτή, πλακῖτις, πλινθῖτις
9.3.30 [30. On alum, the fissile, the round and the liquid.] Alum. The very name of this drug is derived from astringency, for it possesses this in the most vehement degree. Though it is coarse-particled, the kind called fissile is somewhat more fine-particled than the other alums; after it comes the round and the knuckle-shaped (astragalus-like). Quite coarse-particled are the liquid kind and those named plate-alum (plakitis) and brick-alum (plinthitis).
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀστραγαλωτή, πλακῖτις, πλινθῖτις
9.3.30 [30. On split, round and liquid alum.] Alum (styptēria). The very name of this drug is derived from astringency (stypsis), for it has astringency in the highest degree. But although the drug is coarse-particled, the alum called 'split' is somewhat more fine-particled than the other alums. After it comes the round, knucklebone-shaped alum. The liquid alum, and those named plate alum and brick alum, are quite coarse-particled.

9.3.31 [λα΄. Περὶ τιτάνου.] Τίτανος, ἡ μὲν ἄσβεστος καίει σφοδρῶς, ὡς ἐσχάραν ποιεῖν, ἡ σβεσθεῖσα δὲ παραχρῆμα μὲν ἐσχαροῖ καὶ αὐτὴ, μεθ’ ἡμέραν δὲ μίαν ἢ δευτέραν ἧττον καίει καὶ ἧττον ἐσχαροῖ, μετὰ δὲ χρόνον οὐδ’ ὅλως ἐσχάρας ἐργάζεται. θερμαίνει δ’ ἔτι καὶ διατήκει τὰς σάρκας. εἰ δὲ πλυθείη, τὴν μὲν δῆξιν ἐναποτίθεται τῷ ὕδατι καὶ ποιεῖ τὴν καλουμένην κονίαν, αὐτὴ δ’ ἀδήκτως ξηραίνει. καὶ εἰ δίς γε ἢ τρὶς ἢ πλεονάκις πλυθείη, τελέως ἄδηκτος γίγνεται καὶ ξηραίνει γενναίως ἄνευ δήξεως.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
9.3.31 [31. On lime.] Lime: the unslaked kind burns violently, so as to produce an eschar; the slaked kind also produces an eschar itself at once, but after one or two days it burns less and produces an eschar less, and after a time it does not produce eschars at all. It still heats, however, and melts away the flesh. If it is washed, it deposits its biting in the water and makes what is called lye, while it itself dries without biting. And if it is washed two or three times or more often, it becomes completely free of biting and dries nobly without biting.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: γενναίως
9.3.31 [31. On lime (titanos).] Lime: unslaked lime burns violently, so as to produce an eschar; slaked lime likewise forms an eschar immediately, but after one or two days it burns less and forms less of an eschar, and after a time it does not produce eschars at all. It still heats, however, and melts down the flesh. If it is washed, it deposits its biting in the water and makes what is called lye, while it itself dries without biting. And if it is washed twice or three times or more often, it becomes completely non-biting and dries vigorously without biting.
Flags (places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors): none raised.

9.3.32 [32. On mercury.] Mercury is not one of the naturally occurring drugs but one of those that are prepared, like white lead and verdigris and psoricon and litharge. I have no experience of it, either as destroying life if swallowed, or when applied externally.

9.3.33 [λγ΄. Περὶ φύκους.] Φῦκος ὁποίας ἐστὶ δυνάμεως ἐν τῷ περὶ ψιμμυθίου λόγῳ ῥηθήσεται. 9.3.34 [λδ΄. Περὶ χαλκάνθου.] Χάλκανθος. ἱστόρηταί μοι καὶ περὶ χαλκάνθου κατὰ τύχην εἰς χαλκῖτιν μεταβάλλοντι. ἐκόμισα γὰρ καὶ τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον ἐκ τῆς Κύπρου πάμπολυ. τὸ δ’ ἔξωθεν αὐτοῦ πᾶν, ὡς μετὰ ἔτη σχεδὸν κ΄ χαλκῖτις ἐγένετο, τοῦ ἔνδον ὄντος ἔτι χαλκάνθου. διὸ καὶ φυλάττω μέχρι νῦν ἔτι τὸ μεταβάλλον οὕτως ἐπιτηρῶν, ὅπως ἄχρι τοῦ βάθους ἡ μεταβολὴ προς έρχηται καθ’ ἕκαστον ἔτος, ὥσπερ τῆς χαλκίτεως εἰς τὸ μίσυ, ὡς προείρηται. θαυμάσαι δ’ ἔστιν ἐπὶ τοῦ φαρμάκου τούτου πῶς ἰσχυροτάτῃ στύψει μέμικται θερμότης οὐκ ἀγεννής. δῆλον οὖν ὅτι ταριχεῦσαι μάλιστα πάντων δύναται τὰς ὑγρὰς σάρκας ἐκδαπανῶν μὲν τῇ θερμότητι τὴν ὑγρότητα, τῇ στύψει δὲ συνάγον καὶ πιλοῦν τὴν οὐσίαν. ἐν τούτῳ γάρ τοι τῷ ἔργῳ προσεκθλίβει μέν τι καὶ αὐτῆς τῆς ὑγρότητος, σφίγγει δὲ καὶ ξηραίνει καὶ συνάγει πρὸς ἑαυτὴν ὅλης τῆς σαρκὸς τὴν ﻿ οὐσίαν. ἐν δ’ οὖν τῇ Κύπρῳ, καθ’ ὃν ἐγὼ καιρὸν ἐγενόμην ἐν αὐτῇ, τὸ φάρμακον τοῦτο τόνδε τὸν τρόπον ἐθεασάμην ἀθροιζόμενον. οἶκος ἦν μέγας μὲν, οὐ μὴν ὑψηλός γε προκείμενος τῆς εἰς τὸ μέταλλον εἰσόδου. καὶ τούτου τοῦ οἴκου κατὰ τὸν ἀριστερὸν τοῖχον, ὅστις ἦν τοῖς εἰσιοῦσιν κατὰ χεῖρα δεξιὰν, ἐξεκεκόλαπτο διῶρυξ εἰς τὸν συνεχῆ λόφον, εὖρος μὲν ὡς ψαύειν ἀλλήλων τρεῖς ἄνδρας, ὕψος δὲ ὡς τὸν μακρότατον ἄνθρωπον ὀρθὸν δύνασθαι βαδίζειν. ἡ δὲ διῶρυξ αὕτη κατάντης μὲν, οὐ μὴν ὀξεῖά γε καὶ κριμνώδης. ἐπὶ τῷ τέλει δ’ αὐτῆς οὔσης ὡς σταδιαίας λάκκος ὕδατος χλωροῦ τε καὶ παχέος χλιαροῦ μεστὸς ἦν. ἐν ἁπάσῃ δὲ τῇ καταβάσει θερμασία παραπλησία τῇ κατὰ τοὺς πρώτους οἴκους τῶν βαλανείων, οὓς εἰώθασιν προμαλακτήρια καλεῖν. τὸ δὲ καθ’ ἑκάστην ἡμέραν ἀθροιζόμενον ὕδωρ πλῆθος ἦν ἀμφορέων Ῥωμαϊκῶν ὡς ὀκτὼ, κατὰ σταγόνας μικρὰς, ἐν ταῖς τέτταρσιν καὶ εἴκοσιν ὥραις ὅλης τῆς ἡμέρας καὶ νυκτὸς ἐκ τοῦ διατιτραμένου λόφου καταφερόμενον. ἀναφέροντες οὖν τὸ ὕδωρ τοῦτο πεδῆταί τινες ἐνέβαλον πυέλοις τετραγώνοις κατὰ τὸν προκείμενον οἶκον, ἐκ κεραμίων γεγονυίαις, ἐν αἷς πηγνύμενος ἡμέραις ὀλίγαις ἐγίνετο χάλκανθος. ἐμοὶ δ’, ὁπότε κατέβην ἐπὶ τὸ πέρας τοῦ ὀρύγματος, ἔνθα τὸ χλιαρόν τε καὶ χλωρὸν ὕδωρ ἠθροίζετο, καὶ ἡ ὀδμὴ τοῦ ἀέρος ἐφαίνετο πνιγώδης καὶ δύσφορος, ὄζουσα χαλκίτεώς τε καὶ ἰοῦ. τοιαύτην δ’ εἶχε ποιότητα καὶ αὐτὸ τὸ ὕδωρ γευόμενον. ταῦτ’ ἄρα καὶ γυμνοὶ καὶ μετὰ σπουδῆς τοὺς ἀμφορέας ἀνεκόμιζον οἱ πεδῆται καὶ πλείω χρόνον οὐχ ὑπέμενον ἐνδιατρίβειν, ἀλλὰ ταχέως ἀνέτρεχον, ἑκατέρωθεν ἐν τοῖς τοίχοις τοῦ ὀρύγματος, λύχνων καιομένων ἐκ συμμέτρου διαστήματος, οἵτινες οὐδ’ αὐτοὶ χρόνον ἐξήρκουν μακρὸν, ἀλλ’ ἐσβέννυντο τάχιστα. τὸ δ’ οὖν ὄρυγμα τοῦτο κατὰ βραχὺ διωρύχθαι παρ’ αὐτῶν ἤκουον σὺν ἔτεσι πολλοῖς. τουτὶ γὰρ ἔφασαν, ὃ νῦν ὁρᾷς, ἀπὸ τοῦ λόφου καταφερόμενον εἰς τὸν λάκκον ὕδωρ χλωρὸν ἔλαττον εἴωθεν αὑτοῦ γίγνεσθαι κατὰ βραχὺ, κᾀπειδὰν ἐγγὺς ᾖ τοῦ παύσασθαι, πάλιν ὑπορύττουσιν οἱ πεδῆται τὸ συνεχὲς τοῦ λόφου. καί ποτε πρόσθεν ἔπεσεν ἀθρόως τὸ διορυττόμενον, ὡς ἀποκτεῖναι μὲν πάντας αὐτοὺς, ὅλην δὲ διαφθεῖραι τὴν ὁδόν. ὅταν οὖν τοῦτο γένηται, πάλιν ἄλλην ὀπὴν ποιησάμενοι διορύττουσιν, ἄχρις ἂν ὑπακούσῃ τὸ ὕδωρ. ταῦτά σοι περὶ χαλκάνθου λέγειν εἶχον, ἴσως 9.3.33 [33. On phykos.] Of what sort of power phykos is will be stated in the account concerning psimythion. 9.3.34 [34. On chalkanthos.] Chalkanthos. I have also made observation concerning chalkanthos, which by chance changes into chalkitis. For I brought this drug too from Cyprus in very great quantity. And the whole of its outer part, after nearly twenty years, became chalkitis, while the inner part was still chalkanthos. For this reason I still keep up to now the piece that is changing in this way, watching how far the change advances toward the depth in each year, just as that of chalkitis into misy, as has been said before. One may marvel, in the case of this drug, how a not ignoble heat is mixed with the strongest astringency. It is clear, then, that it is able most of all things to cure moist flesh as by salting, consuming the moisture by its heat, and by its astringency drawing together and compacting the substance. For in this very action it also squeezes out something of the moisture itself, and it binds and dries and draws together toward itself the substance of the whole flesh. In Cyprus, then, at the time when I was there, I saw this drug being collected in the following manner. There was a house, large but not high, lying before the entrance to the mine. And along the left wall of this house, which was on the right hand for those entering, a tunnel had been hollowed out into the adjoining hill, in breadth such that three men could touch one another, in height such that the tallest man could walk upright. This tunnel sloped downward, but was not steep or precipitous. At its end, it being about a stade long, there was a pit full of water, greenish and thick and lukewarm. Throughout the whole descent there was a warmth similar to that in the first rooms of the baths, which they are accustomed to call promalakteria. The water collected each day was a quantity of about eight Roman amphorae, falling in small drops, in the twenty-four hours of the whole day and night, out of the hill which had been bored through. Certain fettered men, then, carrying up this water, threw it into square basins in the house lying in front, made of pottery, in which, congealing in a few days, it became chalkanthos. To me, when I went down to the end of the excavation, where the lukewarm and greenish water was collected, the smell of the air appeared stifling and hard to bear, smelling of chalkitis and ios. And the water itself, when tasted, had the same quality. For these reasons the fettered men carried up the amphorae both naked and with haste, and did not endure to remain for a longer time, but quickly ran back up, lamps burning on either side in the walls of the excavation at a measured interval, which did not themselves last for a long time either, but were extinguished very quickly. This excavation, then, I heard from them had been dug little by little over many years. For this, they said, which you now see carried down from the hill into the pit, the greenish water, is accustomed to become less than itself little by little, and when it is near to ceasing, the fettered men again dig under the adjoining part of the hill. And once before, what was being dug through collapsed all at once, so as to kill all of them and to destroy the whole passage. Whenever, then, this happens, they again make another opening and dig through, until the water answers. These things I was able to tell you about chalkanthos, perhaps indeed not necessary, yet better to be known than to be unknown. And remember that I said the mine of sory and chalkitis and misy had been seen by me on the left hand of those entering, so that from these things I was able to conceive that the rainwater percolating in the hill washes around and rinses all that earth, from which sory and misy and chalkitis came into being spontaneously, and, by smelting, copper and kadmeia and pompholyx and spodion and diphryges.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φῦκος, ὅπως, προς έρχηται, προμαλακτήρια, πεδῆται, ὑπακούσῃ, καμινευόμενος
9.3.33 [33. On phykos.] What power phykos has will be stated in the discussion of white lead. 9.3.34 [34. On chalcanthum.] Chalcanthum. I have also made an inquiry concerning chalcanthum, which by chance was changing into chalcitis. For I brought this drug too from Cyprus in great quantity. All of its outer part, after about twenty years, became chalcitis, while what was inside was still chalcanthum. For this reason I still keep, even now, what is changing in this way, watching whether the change advances into the depth each year, as with the change of chalcitis into misy, as has been said before. One may wonder at how, in this drug, a not ignoble heat is mixed with a most powerful astringency. It is clear, then, that it is able beyond all others to pickle moist flesh, consuming the moisture by its heat and, by its astringency, drawing together and compacting the substance. For in this action it squeezes out besides some of the moisture itself, and it binds, dries and draws together towards itself the substance of the whole flesh. Now in Cyprus, at the time when I was there, I saw this drug being collected in the following manner. There was a building, large but not high, lying in front of the entrance to the mine. And in the left wall of this building, which was on the right hand for those entering, a passage had been hewn out into the adjoining hill, in breadth such that three men would touch one another, in height such that the tallest man could walk upright. This passage sloped downward, though not steeply or precipitously. At its end, the passage being about a stade long, there was a pit full of green, thick, lukewarm water. Throughout the whole descent there was a warmth similar to that in the first rooms of the baths, which they are accustomed to call promalaktēria. The water collected each day amounted to about eight Roman amphorae, coming down in small drops over the twenty-four hours of the whole day and night from the bored-through hill. Certain fettered convicts carried this water up and poured it into square tubs in the building in front, made of earthenware, in which, solidifying in a few days, it became chalcanthum. When I went down to the end of the excavation, where the lukewarm and green water collected, the odour of the air appeared to me stifling and hard to bear, smelling of chalcitis and of verdigris. The water itself, when tasted, also had such a quality. For this reason the fettered convicts carried up the amphorae naked and in haste, and did not endure to linger longer, but ran quickly back up, lamps burning on either side in the walls of the excavation at moderate intervals; and these themselves did not last a long time either, but went out very quickly. I heard from them that this excavation had been dug little by little over many years. For they said: this green water which you now see coming down from the hill into the pit is accustomed to become less than it was, little by little, and when it is near to stopping, the fettered convicts again dig under the adjoining part of the hill. And once before, the part being dug through fell in all at once, so as to kill all of them and to destroy the whole way. So whenever this happens, they make another opening and dig through, until the water responds. These things I had to tell you about chalcanthum; perhaps they are not necessary, yet it is better that they be known than unknown. Remember that I said the mine of sory, chalcitis and misy was seen by me on the left hand of those entering, so that from these facts I was able to conceive that the rain water percolating in the hill washes around and rinses all that earth from which there came into being naturally sory, misy and chalcitis, and, when smelted, copper, cadmia, pompholyx, and spodium and diphryges.

9.3.35 [λε΄. Περὶ χαλκίτεως.] Χαλκῖτις, εἴρηται περὶ αὐτῆς ἐν τῷ περὶ μίσυος λόγῳ, νῦν οὖν ἀρκέσει τοσοῦτον εἰπεῖν, ὡς μεμιγμένας ἔχει τήν τε στυπτικὴν καὶ δριμεῖαν δύναμιν. ἐπικρατεῖ δ’ ἡ δριμεῖα σφοδρά τις οὖσα μέχρι τοῦ καίειν τε τὴν σάρκα καὶ τὴν καλουμένην ἐσχάραν ἐργάζεσθαι. καυθὲν δὲ τὸ φάρμακον τοῦτο δάκνει μὲν ἧττον, ξηραίνει δ’ οὐχ ἧττον, ἀποτίθεται δὲ καὶ τῆς στύψεως οὐκ ὀλίγον, ὥστε πάντῃ κρείττων ἐστὶν ἡ κεκαυμένη χαλκῖτις τῆς ἀκαύστου, λεπτομερεστέρα μὲν, ὥσπερ καὶ τἄλλα τὰ κεκαυμένα, γιγνομένη, δριμύτητα δ’ οὐ προσλαμβάνουσα, καθάπερ ἄλλα πολλά. μεμάθηκας δὲ καὶ ὅτι πάντα τὰ κεκαυμένα πλυνόμενα μετριώτερά τε καὶ ἀδηκτότερα γίγνεται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
9.3.35 [35. On chalcitis.] Chalcitis: it has been spoken of in the account of misy, so for now it will suffice to say this much, that it possesses the astringent and the pungent power mixed together. The pungent one predominates, being so vehement as to go as far as burning the flesh and producing what is called an eschar. When this drug has been burnt it bites less, but dries no less, and it also lays aside no small part of its astringency, so that burnt chalcitis is in every way better than the unburnt, becoming more fine-particled, like the other burnt substances too, but not taking on pungency in addition, as many others do. And you have learned also that all burnt substances, when washed, become more moderate and less biting.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
9.3.35 [35. On chalcitis.] Chalcitis: it has been discussed in the account of misy, so for now it will suffice to say this much, that it has the astringent and the pungent power mixed together. The pungent prevails, being so vehement as to burn the flesh and produce what is called an eschar. When burnt, this drug bites less but dries no less, and it also lays aside not a little of its astringency, so that burnt chalcitis is in every respect better than unburnt: it becomes more fine-particled, as other burnt things also do, but it does not take on pungency, as many others do. You have also learned that all burnt things, when washed, become more moderate and less biting.

9.3.36 [36. On burnt copper.] Burnt copper has something pungent as well, and it also partakes of astringency, and for this reason, if it is washed, it is an excellent drug for the cicatrization of ulcers; it is able to effect this even before being washed, and especially in the case of harder flesh. 9.3.36 [36. On burnt copper.] Burnt copper has something pungent as well, and it also partakes of astringency; and for this reason, if it is washed, it is a most excellent drug for bringing wounds to close with a scar. It is able to accomplish this even before it has been washed, and especially in the case of harder flesh; but for tender flesh the washed copper is more useful.

Greek (Kühn)
9.3.38 [λη΄. Περὶ χρυσοκόλλης.] Χρυσοκόλλα. καὶ τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον ἐκ τῶν τηκόντων σάρκας ἐστὶν, οὐ μὴν ἰσχυρῶς γε δάκνει, διαφορητική γε μὴν ἱκανῶς ἐστιν καὶ ξηραντική. ἔνιοι μὲν οὖν τὸ μεταλλευόμενον μόνον οὕτως ὀνομάζουσιν, ἔνιοι δὲ τὸ σκευαζόμενον ἐν θυίᾳ χαλκῇ καὶ δοίδυκι χαλκῷ δι’ οὔρου παιδὸς, ὅπερ ἔνιοι κατὰ τὰς διαφορὰς τῶν ἰῶν ἀριθμοῦσιν. ἄμεινον δὲ θέρους αὐτὸ σκευάζειν, ἢ πάντως γε ἐν ἀέρι θερμῷ, τρίβοντας τὸ οὖρον ἐν τῇ θυίᾳ. κάλλιον δὲ ἐρυθρὸν εἶναι τὸ χαλκὸν, ἐξ οὗ κατασκευάζεις αὐτήν τε τὴν θυίαν καὶ τὸν δοίδυκα. πλέον γὰρ ἀποτρίβεται περιαγομένου τοῦ δοίδυκος, ὅταν ἁπαλώτερος ὁ χαλκὸς ᾖ. τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον ἐπιτηδειότατόν ἐστι κακοήθεσιν ἕλκεσιν, αὐτό τε καθ’ ἑαυτὸ καὶ μιγνύμενον ἑτέροις, ὡς ἐν τοῖς περὶ συνθέσεως φαρμάκων εἰρήσεται. νυνὶ δὲ ἀρκεῖ τό γε γινώσκειν ὡς τῆς μεταλλευτῆς χρυσοκόλλης ὅσον τοι μᾶλλον ξηραίνει ἧττον δάκνει, τοσοῦτον αὐτῆς ὑπερέχει τῇ λεπτομερείᾳ. καὶ αὐτὴν μέντοι αὐτὴν χρυσοκόλλην ἐκείνην καύσας πολὺ λεπτομερεστέραν ἐργάσῃ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἰῶν, ὅσον τοι μᾶλλον ξηραίνει ἧττον δάκνει, αὐτὴν μέντοι αὐτὴν
9.3.38 [38. On chrysocolla.] Chrysocolla. This drug too belongs to those that melt flesh, yet it does not bite strongly; it is, however, sufficiently dispersing and drying. Some people, then, give this name only to the mined kind, while others give it to the kind prepared in a copper mortar with a copper pestle by means of a boy's urine, which some people count among the different kinds of ios (verdigris). It is better to prepare it in summer, or at any rate in warm air, grinding the urine in the mortar. It is better, too, that the copper from which you make both the mortar itself and the pestle be red; for more is rubbed off as the pestle is turned round when the copper is softer. This drug is most suitable for malignant ulcers, both by itself and mixed with others, as will be stated in the books on the composition of drugs. For now it suffices to know this much: by as much as it dries more and bites less than the mined chrysocolla, by so much does it surpass it in fineness of particles. And indeed, if you burn that mined chrysocolla itself, you will make it much more fine-particled.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τηκόντων σάρκας, ἰῶν, τὸ χαλκὸν, ὅσον τοι μᾶλλον ξηραίνει ἧττον δάκνει, αὐτὴν μέντοι αὐτὴν
9.3.38 [38. On chrysocolla.] Chrysocolla. This drug too is among those that melt down flesh; it does not, however, bite strongly, but it is sufficiently dispersing and drying. Now some people give this name only to the mined kind, while others give it to the kind prepared in a copper mortar with a copper pestle by means of a boy's urine, which some count among the varieties of verdigris. It is better to prepare it in summer, or at any rate in hot air, grinding the urine in the mortar. It is better too that the copper from which you make both the mortar itself and the pestle be red. For more is rubbed off as the pestle is turned round when the copper is softer. This drug is most suitable for malignant ulcers, both on its own and mixed with others, as will be said in the works on the composition of drugs. For now it is enough to know that, by as much as it dries more and bites less than the mined chrysocolla, by so much does it surpass it in fineness of particles. And indeed, if you burn that chrysocolla itself, you will make it much more fine-particled.

9.3.39 [λθ΄. Περὶ ψιμμυθίου.] Ψιμμύθιον. καὶ τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον μαρτυρεῖ τῇ προειρημένῃ πρὸ τούτου δυνάμει. ὑπὸ γοῦν ὄξους δριμέος αὐτὸ διαλυόμενον γευόμενον ὅμως οὔτε δριμὺ οὔτε δακνῶδές ἐστιν οὔτε διαφορητικὸν, ἀλλ’ ἐμπλαστικόν τε καὶ ἐμψυκτικὸν, ἐναντιωτάτης ὂν ἰῷ δυνάμεως. καίτοι κᾀκεῖνος ὄξους διαλύοντος τὸν χαλκὸν γίγνεται. καιόμενον δὲ τὸ ψιμμύθιον εἰς τὴν καλουμένην σάνδικα μεταβαλὸν λεπτομερέστερόν γε αὐτοῦ φάρμακον, οὐ μὴν ἤδη γέ πω θερμαῖνον. τὸ δέ γε φῦκος, τὴν ψύξιν τοῦ ψιμμυθίου φυλάττον, λεπτομέρειαν προσείληφε, ὡς δύνασθαι δι’ αὐτὴν εἰς τὸ βάθος καταδύεσθαι τῶν ὁμιλούντων σωμάτων τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φῦκος, σάνδικα
9.3.39 [39. On psimythion.] Psimythion (white lead). This drug too bears witness to the power described before it. At any rate, although it is itself dissolved by pungent vinegar, when tasted it is nevertheless neither pungent nor biting nor dispersing, but clogging and cooling, being of a power most opposed to that of verdigris; and yet that too comes into being when vinegar dissolves the copper. When psimythion is burnt and has changed into what is called sandyx, it becomes a drug more fine-particled than itself, though not yet, however, a heating one. The phykos, for its part, while preserving the coldness of psimythion, has acquired fineness of particles, so that by means of it its power is able to sink down into the depth of the bodies it comes into contact with.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φῦκος, σάνδικα, μεταβαλὸν, τῇ προειρημένῃ πρὸ τούτου δυνάμει
9.3.39 [39. On white lead.] White lead (psimmythion). This drug too bears witness to the power stated just before this one. At any rate, although it comes from being dissolved by pungent vinegar, when tasted it is nevertheless neither pungent nor biting nor dispersing, but clogging and cooling, being of a power most contrary to verdigris (ios). And yet verdigris too comes about when vinegar dissolves copper. White lead, when burnt, changing into what is called sandyx, becomes a drug more fine-particled than itself, but not yet, at any rate, heating. Phykos, however, preserving the cooling quality of white lead, has taken on fineness of particles, so that through it its power is able to sink down into the depth of the bodies with which it comes into contact.

Greek (Kühn)
9.3.40 [μ΄. Περὶ ψωρικοῦ.] Ψωρικόν. λιθαργύρῳ μιγνυμένης διπλασίας χαλκίτεως γίνεται τὸ φάρμακον, ὄξει μὲν πρῶτα δριμυτάτῳ λειωθέντων ἀμφοῖν, εἶτ’ εἰς καινὴν χύτραν ἐμβληθέντων, ἣν καταχώσεις ἡμέρας μ΄. μέσου θέρους ἐν κοπρίᾳ τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον ξηραντικώτερόν τε ἅμα καὶ ἀδηκτότερον γίνεται χαλκίτεως καὶ δηλονότι καὶ λεπτομερέστερον. ΓΑΛΗΝΟΥ ΠΕΡΙ ΤΗς ΤΩΝ ΑΠΛΩΝ ΦΑΡΜΑΚΩΝ ΚΡΑΣΕΩς ΚΑΙ ΔΥΝΑΜΕΩς ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ Κ. Οὐδὲν τῶν νῦν λεχθησομένων τοῖς ἀγνοοῦσι τὰ κατὰ τὴν ἀρχὴν τῆς πραγματείας ἐν τοῖς πρώτοις πέντε βιβλίοις εἰρημένα μεγάλην ὠφέλειαν οἴσει, τινὰ δ’ ἴσως καὶ βλάψει τὸν χρησόμενον τοῖς ἐν αὐτῷ γεγραμμένοις φαρμάκοις, οὐκ ἔχοντα μέθοδον. ὡς οὖν ἐκεῖνα μεμαθηκότι σοι διαλέξομαι, τὸ κεφάλαιον ἀναμνήσας ὅλης τῆς πραγματείας. ἐδείχθη τὰ φάρμακα κατὰ μὲν τὰς δραστικὰς ὀνομαζομένας ποιότητας ἐνεργοῦντα θερμότητα καὶ ψυχρότητα καὶ ξηρότητα καὶ ὑγρότητα, τῇ δὲ τούτων κράσει στρυφνὰ καὶ αὐστηρὰ καὶ ἁλμυρὰ καὶ ἁλυκὰ καὶ πικρὰ καὶ δριμέα καὶ γλυκέα γιγνόμενα, καὶ τὰ μὲν ῥυπτικὰ, τὰ δὲ ἀποκρουστικὰ, τὰ δὲ ἑλκτικὰ, τὰ δὲ μαλακτικὰ, τὰ δὲ καυστικὰ, τὰ δὲ σηπτικὰ, τὰ δὲ ἐσχαρωτικὰ, καὶ πρός γε τούτοις ἔτι κατ’ ἄλλας ἰδικωτέρας ἐνεργείας, σαρκωτικά τε καὶ συνουλωτικὰ καὶ κολλητικὰ συρίγγων ἢ ἑλκῶν, ἢ καθαιρετικὰ τῶν ὑπεραυξανόμενον σαρκῶν. ἐδείχθη δὲ καὶ ὡς ἡ καθόλου δύναμις ἐκ πείρας μιᾶς ἐνδεικτικῶς εὑρίσκοιτο, καὶ οὐ τῆς τυχούσης γε πείρας, ἀλλὰ μετὰ τῶν εἰρημένων διορισμῶν γιγνομένης· εὑρεθείσης δ’ ἅπαξ τῆς καθόλου δυνάμεως οὐδεμιᾶς ἔτι πείρας εἶναι χρείαν εἴς γε τὰς κατὰ μέρος ἐνεργείας, ὅτι μὴ πρὸς βεβαίωσιν μόνην ὧν ὁ λόγος εὗρεν. οὕτως οὖν καὶ νῦν ποιησαίμεθα τὴν κρίσιν τῆς προκειμένης ὕλης, αὕτη δ’ ἐστὶν ἡ ἐκ τῶν ζώων. ἐν μὲν γὰρ τοῖς μετὰ τὸ πέμπτον ἐφεξῆς τρισὶ βιβλίοις, ἕκτω καὶ ἑβδόμῳ καὶ ὀγδόω, τὴν περὶ τὰ φυτὰ διήλθομεν ὕλην, οὐ πᾶσαν δηλονότι τὴν καθ’ ὅλην τὴν οἰκουμένην, ἀλλ’ ὅσης ἡμεῖς ἔχομεν πεῖραν. ἐν δὲ τῷ πρὸ τοῦδε, τῆς δ’ ὅλης πραγματείας ἐννάτῳ, τὴν περὶ τὰ γεώδη τε καὶ λιθώδη σώματα. λείπεται δ’ ἡμῖν ἔτι τὴν περὶ τὰ ζῶα διελεῖν ὕλην· εἶτ’ ἔτι τῶν ἐν θαλάττῃ καὶ λίμναις ἢ ὅλως ἐν ὕδατι γεννωμένων ἐστὶν ὑπόλοιπον, ἃ μήτε φυτὰ μήτε γῆ μήτε λίθος ἐστὶ μήτε ζῶον. ὀλίγιστα δὲ τὰ τοιαῦτα παντάπασίν ἐστι καὶ γεγράψεται τελευταῖα, μετὰ τὸ διελθεῖν ἡμᾶς τὰ κατὰ τὰ σώματα τῶν ζώων. ἔσται δὲ καὶ τούτων ἡ τάξις τῆς διδασκαλίας κατὰ τὴν τῶν πρώτων γραμμάτων τάξιν ἐν ταῖς προσηγορίαις αὐτῶν. ὥσπερ δ’ ἐν τῇ τῶν φυτῶν ὕλῃ καὶ περὶ τῶν ἐξ αὐτῶν γινομένων χυμῶν τὸν λόγον ἐποιησάμην, οὕτως καὶ νῦν οὐ μόνον τῶν στερεῶν μορίων ἐν τοῖς ζώοις ἡ διδασκαλία τῆς δυνάμεως, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν ἐν αὐτοῖς περιεχομένων ἔσται, φλέγματος, χολῆς, αἵματος, οὔρου, κόπρου καὶ τῶν ὁμοίων. ἐν μὲν οὖν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν εἰρημένοις οὐ πολλὰ τῶν κατὰ μέρος ἐν ταῖς ὑπὸ τῶν ἰατρῶν γεγραμμέναις ὕλαις ἄγνωστά μοι γέγονεν, ἀλλ’ αὐτὸς ἐσπούδασα διὰ τῆς πείρας γνῶναι τὰς δυνάμεις αὐτῶν, εἴ τινος δ’ οὐκ ἔγνων, οὐδ’ ἔγραψα περὶ τούτου τὴν ἀρχὴν, οὐκ ἀξιῶν ἄλλοις πιστεύειν οὐδὲ περὶ ἑνὸς τοιούτου, διὰ τὸ καταμαθεῖν ἐνίους πολλὰ ψευδομένους. ἐπὶ δὲ τῆς νῦν προκειμένης ὕλης οὐχ οὕτως ἔχει. πάμπολλα ὁμολογῶ μορίων τε καὶ ὑγρῶν ἐν τοῖς τῶν ζώων σώμασι περιεχομένων, ὧν οὐδεμίαν αὐτὸς ἔσχηκα τοιαύτην πεῖραν, ὁποίαν ἔγραψάν τινες· ἔνια μὲν γὰρ αὐτῶν ἀσελγῆ τέ ἐστι καὶ βδελυρὰ, τινὰ δὲ καὶ πρὸς τῶν νόμων ἀπηγορευμένα, περὶ ὧν οὐκ οἶδα πῶς ἔγραψεν ὁ Ξενοκράτης, ἄνθρωπος οὐ πάλαι γεγονὼς, ἀλλὰ κατὰ τοὺς πάππους ἡμῶν, τῆς Ῥωμαϊκῆς βασιλείας ἀπηγορευκυίας ἀνθρώπους ἐσθίειν, ἀλλ’ ἐκεῖνός γε ὡς αὐτὸς πεπειραμένος ἀξιοπίστως πάνυ γράφει τίνα πάθη θεραπεύειν πέφυκεν ἐγκέφαλος ἐσθιόμενος ἢ σάρκες ἢ ἧπαρ ἀνθρώπου, τίνα δὲ τὰ τῆς κεφαλῆς ἢ κνήμης ἢ δακτύλων ὀστᾶ τὰ μὲν καυθέντα, τὰ δ’ ἄκαυστα πινόμενα, τίνα δ’ αὐτὸ τὸ αἷμα. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν εἰ καὶ παρὰ τοὺς νόμους, ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἀσελγῆ γε. πόσις δ’ ἱδρῶτός τε καὶ οὔρου καὶ καταμηνίου γυναικὸς ἀσελγῆ καὶ βδελυρὰ, καὶ τούτων οὐδὲν ἧττον ἡ κόπρος, ἣν διαχριομένην τε τοῖς κατὰ τὸ στόμα καὶ τὴν φάρυγγα μορίοις εἴς τε τὴν γαστέρα καταπινομένην ἔγραψεν ὁ Ξενοκράτης ὅ τί ποτε ποιεῖν δύναται· γέγραφε δὲ καὶ περὶ τοῦ κατὰ τὰ ὦτα ῥύπου καταπινομένου. ἐγὼ μὲν οὖν οὐδὲ τοῦτον ἂν ὑπέμεινα καταπιεῖν, ἐφ’ ᾧ γε μηδέποτε νοσῆσαι. πολὺ δ’ αὐτοῦ βδελυρώτερον ἡγοῦμαι τὴν κόπρον εἶναι. καὶ μεῖζόν γε ὄνειδός ἐστιν ἀνθρώπῳ σωφρονοῦντι κοπροφάγον ἀκούειν ἢ αἰσχρουργὸν ἢ κίναιδον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν αἰσχρουργῶν μᾶλλον βδελυττόμεθα τοὺς φοινικίζοντας τῶν λεσβιαζόντων, ᾧ φαίνεταί μοι παραπλήσιόν τι πάσχειν ὁ καὶ καταμηνίου πίνων. οὔτ’ οὖν τούτων ὑπομείναι τις ἂν εἰς πεῖραν ἐλθεῖν ἄνθρωπος κατὰ φύσιν ἔχων οὔθ’ ὅσα μετριώτερα μὲν τούτων, ἔτι δ’ ἀσελγῆ, κόπρῳ καταχρίεσθαί τι τοῦ σώματος μέρος, ἕνεκα τοῦ κατ’ αὐτὸ πάθους, ἢ ἀνθρώπου σπέρματος. γόνον δὲ αὐτὸ καλεῖν ﻿ εἴωθεν ὁ Ξενοκράτης, καὶ διορίζεταί γε μετὰ πάσης ἐπιμελείας τίνα μὲν αὐτὸς ὁ γόνος μόνος ὠφελεῖν πέφυκε καταχριόμενος, τίνα δὲ μετὰ τὴν ὁμιλίαν ἀνδρὸς καὶ γυναικὸς, ὅταν ἐκπέσῃ τοῦ γυναικείου κόλπου. μεγάλην γάρ τινα δεῖ γενέσθαι βοηθημάτων πενίαν, ἵνα τις χίμεθλα θεραπεύσῃ ὑπερεχούσας ἀνδρὸς σπέρμα μὴ μεῖναν ἔνδον, ἀλλ’ ἐκρυὲν τῆς γυναικὸς ἐπὶ τῇ συνουσίᾳ. πολὺ μὲν δὴ καὶ τὸ τοιοῦτο τῆς ὕλης εἶδός ἐστιν ἐν τοῖς περὶ τῆς ἀπὸ τῶν ζώων ὠφελείας ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ γεγραμμένοις. οὐ γὰρ ἀνθρώπου δηλονότι, τίνα δύναμιν ἔχει πινόμενον οὖρον ἢ καταπινομένοις τε καὶ διαχριομένοις τοῖς ἐν τὸ στόματι μέρεσι κόπρος, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ζώων ἑκάστου διηγεῖται, πολὺ δ’ ἄλλο τῶν δυσπορίστων, οἷον ὅταν ἐλέφαντος ἢ ἵππου Νειλώου μνημονεύῃ. βασιλίσκον μὲν γὰρ τὸ θηρίον οὐδὲ εἶδον οὐδέποτε, καὶ εἰ ἀληθῆ τὰ λεγόμενα περὶ αὐτοῦ, κινδυνῶδές ἐστι καὶ τὸ πλησίον ἀφικέσθαι τῷ ζώῳ τούτῳ. παραπλήσια δὲ τῷ Ξενακράτει καὶ ἄλλοι τινὲς ἔγραψαν περὶ ζώων, ἐξ ὧν καὶ αὐ τὸς ὁ Ξενοκράτης ἐξεγράψατο τὰ πλεῖστα. πόθεν γὰρ ἂν ηὐπόρησε τοσούτων τε καὶ τοιούτων πραγμάτων αὐτὸς πειραθῆναι; ὁ γοῦν ἡμέτερος γενόμενός ποτε βασιλεὺς Ἄτταλος ἐλάττονα φαίνεται γράφων, καίτοι φιλοτιμότατα σχὼν περὶ τὴν τῶν τοιούτων πεῖραν. ἐπαινῶν δέ τις Ἀτευρίστου τὴν αὐτὴν πραγματείαν, ἔδωκέ μοι καὶ αὐτὴν διελθεῖν, ὥς γε ἐμοὶ δοκεῖ, χωρὶς αὐτοψίας ἰδίας τοῦ γράψαντος αὐτὴν γεγονυῖαν. ἐγὼ τοίνυν οὔτε βασιλίσκων οὔτε ἐλεφάντων οὔθ’ ἵππων Νειλώων οὔτ’ ἄλλου τινὸς οὗ μὴ πεῖραν αὐτὸς ἔχω μνημονεύσω, τῶν δὲ καλουμένων φίλτρων, ἀγωγίμων, ὀνειροπομπῶν τε καὶ μισήτρων, αὐτοῖς γὰρ τοῖς ἐκείνων ὀνόμασιν ἐξεπίτηδες χρῶμαι, τὴν ἀρχὴν ἂν, οὐδ’ εἰ πεῖραν ἱκανὴν εἶχον, ἐμνημόνευσα διὰ γραμμάτων, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ τῶν θανασίμων φαρμάκων ἢ τῶν ὡς αὐτοὶ καλοῦσιν παθοποιῶν. ἐκεῖνα μὲν γὰρ αὐτῶν καὶ γελοῖα, καταδῆσαι τοὺς ἀντιδίκους, ὡς μηδὲν ἐπὶ τοῦ δικανικοῦ δυνηθῆναι φθέγξασθαι, ἢ ἐκτρῶσαι ποιῆσαι τὴν κύουσαν, ἢ μηδέποτε συλλαβεῖν, ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τοιαῦτα. τὰ μέν γε πλεῖστα εἶναι τούτων ἐστὶ καὶ πρὸς τῆς πείρας ἀδύνατα ὑπάρχειν, ἔνια δὲ εἰ καὶ δυνατὰ, βλαβερὰ γοῦν γ’ ἐστὶ τῷ βίῳ τῶν ἀνθρώπων, ὥστ’ ἐγὼ νὴ τοὺς θεοὺς θαυμάζω κατὰ τίνα τὴν ἔννοιαν ἧκον ἐπὶ τὸ γράφειν αὐτά τινες. ἃ γὰρ καὶ τοῖς ζῶσιν ἀδοξίαν φέρει γνωσθέντα, πῶς ταῦτα μετὰ θάνατον εὐδοξίαν οἴσειν αὑτοῖς ἤλπισαν; εἰ μὲν οὖν βασιλεῖς ὄντες ἐν ἀνθρώποις ἐπὶ θανάτῳ κατακεκριμένοις ἐποιήσαντο τὴν πεῖραν αὐτῶν, οὐδὲν ἔπραξαν δεινόν. ἐπεὶ δ’ ἰδιῶται τοιαύτης ἐξουσίας ἐν ὅλῳ τῷ βίῳ γεγονότες ἐπὶ τὸ γράφειν ἧκον αὐτὰ, δυοῖν θάτερον, ἢ μὴ πειραθέντες αὐτοὶ γράφουσιν ὑπὲρ ὧν οὐκ ἴσασιν, ἢ εἴπερ ἐπειράθησαν, ἀσεβέστατοι πάντων ἀνθρώπων εἰσὶν, ἕνεκα πείρας ὀλέθρια δόντες φάρμακα τοῖς οὐδὲν ἠδικηκόσιν, ἐνίοτε δὲ καὶ καλοῖς τε καὶ ἀγαθοῖς ἀνδράσιν. ἰατροὺς γοῦν τις ἑστῶτας ἐπὶ ῥωποπώλαις θεασάμενος δύο, προσεκόμισεν αὐτοῖς μέλι πιπράσκων δῆθεν. οἱ δὲ ἐγεύσαντό τε καὶ περὶ τῆς τιμῆς διελέγοντο καὶ ὡς ὀλίγον αὐτῶν διδόντων, ὁ μὲν σπεύσας ἐχωρήθη, τῶν δ’ ἰατρῶν οὐδέτερος ἐσώθη. τὰ τοιαῦτ’ οὖν ἅπαντα τῶν πραξάντων τοὺς γράψαντας οὐχ ἧττον, ἀλλὰ καὶ μᾶλλον ἄξιον μισεῖν, ὅσῳ καὶ μεῖόν ἐστιν ἀδίκημα μόνον τι ποιῆσαι κακὸν ἢ μετὰ πολλῶν. καὶ τῷ μὲν πράξαντι συναπέθανεν ἡ τῶν κακῶν θεωρημάτων ἐμπειρία, τῶν δὲ γραψάντων πάντων ἀθάνατός ἐστιν τοῖς πονηροῖς ὅπλα τῆς κακουργίας παρασκευάζουσα. λέγωμεν οὖν ἡμεῖς ἤδη περὶ τῶν χρησίμων ἀνθρώποις πραγμάτων, ὅσων πεῖραν ἔχομεν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ Κ, ὑπερεχούσας, Ἀτευρίστου, ἐχωρήθη, φοινικίζοντας, λεσβιαζόντων, ῥωποπώλαις, παθοποιῶν
9.3.40 [40. On psoricon.] Psoricon. The drug is produced when a double quantity of chalcitis is mixed with litharge, both being first ground fine in the most pungent vinegar, then put into a new pot, which you will bury for forty days in midsummer in a dung-heap. This drug becomes at once more drying and less biting than chalcitis, and evidently also more fine-particled. GALEN, ON THE MIXTURE AND POWER OF SIMPLE DRUGS, BOOK 10. None of what is now to be said will bring great benefit to those ignorant of what was said at the beginning of the treatise in the first five books, and some of it will perhaps even harm the man who uses the drugs written in it without possessing a method. I shall therefore address you as one who has learned those things, having first recalled the chief point of the whole treatise. It was shown that drugs act, in respect of the qualities called active, by heat and cold and dryness and moistness, and that by the mixture of these they become acerbic and harsh and saline and salty and bitter and pungent and sweet, and some cleansing, some repelling, some drawing, some softening, some caustic, some putrefying, some escharotic, and further, in addition to these, in respect of other more specific activities, flesh-forming and cicatrizing and agglutinating of fistulas or ulcers, or reducing of overgrowing flesh. It was also shown that the universal power may be discovered by indication from a single trial—and not any chance trial, but one made together with the distinctions stated; and that once the universal power has been discovered there is no longer any need of trial as regards the particular activities, except solely for the confirmation of what reason has discovered. In this way, then, let us now too make our judgment of the material before us, and this is the material derived from animals. For in the three books following in order after the fifth, the sixth, seventh and eighth, we went through the material concerning plants—not, evidently, all that exists throughout the whole inhabited world, but as much as we ourselves have experience of. In the book before this one, the ninth of the whole treatise, the material concerning earthy and stony bodies. It remains for us still to go through the material concerning animals; then there is still left over that of the things generated in the sea and in lakes, or in general in water, which are neither plants nor earth nor stone nor animal. Such things are altogether very few and will be written last, after we have gone through what pertains to the bodies of animals. The order of teaching for these too will follow the order of the first letters in their names. And just as in the material of plants I also gave an account of the juices produced from them, so too now the teaching of the power will be not only of the solid parts in animals but also of what is contained in them: phlegm, bile, blood, urine, dung and the like. Now in what was said before, not many of the particular items in the materia written by physicians were unknown to me, but I myself took pains to know their powers through experience; and if there was any whose power I did not know, I did not write about it at all, not thinking it right to trust others even about a single thing of this kind, because I had found that some of them lie about many things. But with the material now before us it is not so. I confess that there are very many parts and fluids contained in the bodies of animals of which I myself have had no such experience as some have written of; for some of them are lewd and disgusting, and some are even forbidden by the laws—about which I do not know how Xenocrates came to write, a man who lived not long ago but in the time of our grandfathers, when the Roman empire had forbidden the eating of human beings; yet he, as though having made trial himself, writes with full confidence what affections human brain, when eaten, or flesh or liver is naturally fitted to cure, and what the bones of the head or shin or fingers cure, some burnt, some unburnt, when drunk, and what blood itself cures. These things, then, even if contrary to the laws, are at least not lewd. But the drinking of sweat and urine and a woman's menstrual blood is lewd and disgusting, and no less than these dung, of which Xenocrates wrote what it is able to do when smeared on the parts about the mouth and the throat and swallowed down into the belly; he has written also about the filth in the ears being swallowed. I for my part would not have endured to swallow even this, on condition of never falling ill; and I consider dung far more disgusting than it. And it is a greater reproach for a man of sound mind to be called a dung-eater than a performer of shameful acts or a catamite; indeed even among performers of shameful acts we loathe those who phoenicize more than those who lesbianize, and the man who drinks menstrual blood seems to me to suffer something similar to the former. Neither, then, would any human being in his natural condition endure to come to the trial of these, nor of those things which are more moderate than these yet still lewd—to smear some part of the body with dung on account of the affection in it, or with human semen. Xenocrates is accustomed to call it gonos, and he distinguishes with all care what the gonos by itself alone is naturally fitted to help when smeared on, and what it helps after the intercourse of man and woman, when it has fallen out of the woman's vagina. For there must have arisen some great poverty of remedies, for someone to treat chilblains with a man's semen that did not remain inside but flowed out of the woman upon intercourse. Indeed much of this kind of material is in what he has written about the benefit derived from animals. For it is not only of man, evidently, that he recounts what power urine has when drunk, or dung when swallowed and smeared on the parts in the mouth, but of each of the other animals as well, and much else besides of things hard to procure, as when he mentions the elephant or the Nile horse. As for the basilisk, I have never even seen the beast, and if what is said about it is true, it is dangerous even to come near this animal. Others too wrote things similar to Xenocrates about animals, from whom Xenocrates himself also copied out the most part. For whence could he have had the means himself to make trial of so many things and of such a kind? At any rate our own Attalus, who was once king, appears to write less, although he was most ambitious concerning the trial of such things. And someone, praising the same kind of treatise by Ateuristus, gave me that too to read through—a work produced, as it seems to me at least, without the personal observation of its author. I therefore shall mention neither basilisks nor elephants nor Nile horses nor anything else of which I have no experience myself; and as for the so-called philtres, attracting-charms, dream-senders and hate-charms—for I deliberately use their own names for them—I would not have mentioned them in writing at all, even if I had sufficient experience, just as I would not deadly drugs or those which, as they themselves call them, are affection-producing. For some of those are even ridiculous: to bind one's opponents at law so that they can utter nothing in the court, or to make the pregnant woman miscarry, or never conceive, and all other such things. Most of these are indeed also impossible by the test of experience, and some, even if possible, are at any rate harmful to the life of men; so that I, by the gods, wonder with what thought some men came to write them. For things which bring disrepute to the living when known—how did they hope that these would bring them good repute after death? If, being kings among men, they made trial of them on those condemned to death, they did nothing terrible. But since private persons, having had no such authority in their whole life, came to write them, one of two things follows: either, not having made trial themselves, they write about what they do not know, or, if they did make trial, they are the most impious of all men, having given destructive drugs for the sake of trial to those who had done no wrong, and sometimes even to fine and good men. For instance, someone, seeing two physicians standing by the dealers in petty wares, brought them honey, pretending to sell it. They tasted it and were discussing the price, and as they offered little, he hastened off and withdrew, and neither of the physicians survived. For all such things, then, it is right to hate those who wrote them no less than those who did them, but even more, in proportion as it is a lesser wrong to do some evil alone than together with many. And with the doer his acquaintance with evil theorems died too, but that of all who wrote is immortal, furnishing weapons of wrongdoing to the wicked. Let us then now speak about the things useful to men, as many as we have experience of.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑπερεχούσας, ἐχωρήθη, Ἀτευρίστου, ὅσῳ καὶ μεῖόν ἐστιν ἀδίκημα μόνον τι ποιῆσαι κακὸν ἢ μετὰ πολλῶν, θεωρημάτων, ῥωποπώλαις, κόλπου, φοινικίζοντας, λεσβιαζόντων, παθοποιῶν
9.3.40 [40. On psoricum.] Psoricum. The drug is made from litharge mixed with twice as much chalcitis. Both are first ground in the most pungent vinegar and then put into a new pot, which you will bury in dung for forty days in midsummer. This drug becomes both more drying and at the same time less biting than chalcitis, and evidently also more fine-particled. GALEN, ON THE MIXTURE AND POWER OF SIMPLE DRUGS, BOOK 10. None of the things now about to be said will bring great benefit to those who are ignorant of what was said at the beginning of the treatise in the first five books. Perhaps it will even harm a person who uses the drugs written about in it without having a method. I shall therefore address you as one who has learned those things, after recalling the main point of the whole treatise. It was shown that drugs act according to the so-called active qualities, heat and coldness and dryness and moisture. Through the mixture of these they become acerbic and harsh and briny and salty and bitter and pungent and sweet. Some become cleansing, others repelling, others drawing, others softening, others caustic, others putrefying, others eschar-forming. In addition to these, according to other more specific actions, they become flesh-forming and cicatrizing and agglutinating of fistulas or ulcers, or reducing of overgrowing flesh. It was also shown that the universal power would be discovered indicatively from a single trial. This is not just any trial, but one carried out with the distinctions that were stated. Once the universal power has been discovered, there is no longer any need of trial for the particular actions, except solely for confirmation of what reason has discovered. In this way, then, let us now too make our judgement of the material before us, and this is the material from animals. For in the three books that follow the fifth in sequence, the sixth, seventh and eighth, we went through the material concerning plants. We did not, evidently, go through all of it throughout the whole inhabited world, but only as much as we have experience of. In the book before this one, the ninth of the whole treatise, we went through the material concerning earthy and stony bodies. It remains for us still to go through the material concerning animals. After that there still remains what is generated in the sea and lakes, or generally in water, things which are neither plants nor earth nor stone nor animal. Such things are altogether very few, and they will be written of last, after we have gone through what concerns the bodies of animals. The order of instruction for these too will follow the order of the first letters in their names. In the material of plants I also gave an account of the juices that come from them. So now too the instruction about power will concern not only the solid parts in animals but also what is contained in them: phlegm, bile, blood, urine, dung and the like. In what was said earlier, not many of the particular items in the materia medica written by the physicians remained unknown to me. I myself took pains to know their powers through experience. If there was something I did not know, I did not write about it at all, since I did not think it right to trust others about even one such thing, because I had learned that some people tell many lies. With the material now before us, however, it is not so. I confess that there are very many of the parts and fluids contained in the bodies of animals of which I myself have had no such experience as some have written about. For some of them are licentious and disgusting, and some are even forbidden by the laws. About these I do not know how Xenocrates came to write. He was a man who lived not long ago but in the time of our grandfathers, when the Roman empire had forbidden men to eat human beings. Yet he, at any rate, writes quite credibly, as one who had himself made trial of them. He records which affections the brain or flesh or liver of a human being naturally cures when eaten, and which the bones of the head or shin or fingers cure when drunk, some burnt and others unburnt, and which blood itself cures. These things, then, even if contrary to the laws, are at least not licentious. But the drinking of sweat and urine and a woman's menses is licentious and disgusting. No less so than these is dung. Xenocrates wrote what it can do when smeared on the parts in the mouth and pharynx and when swallowed into the stomach. He has also written about ear-wax when swallowed. I, for my part, would not have consented to swallow even this, even on condition of never falling ill. And I consider dung far more disgusting than it. Indeed, it is a greater reproach for a temperate man to be called a dung-eater than a doer of obscene acts or a catamite. But even among those who do obscene acts we loathe the 'phoenicizers' more than the 'lesbianizers', and the one who drinks menses seems to me to suffer something similar to the former. No man in his natural condition, then, would consent to make trial of these things. Nor would he consent to make trial of those that are more moderate than these but still licentious, such as smearing some part of the body with dung for the sake of the affection in it, or with human semen. Xenocrates is accustomed to call it 'gonos'. He distinguishes with all care which conditions the gonos itself, by itself, naturally benefits when smeared on, and which it benefits after the intercourse of man and woman, when it falls out of the female bosom. For there must be some great poverty of remedies for anyone to treat chilblains [ὑπερεχούσας?] with a man's semen that has not stayed inside but has flowed out of the woman after intercourse. This kind of material too is plentiful in what he has written about the benefit from animals. For he recounts not only, evidently, for man, what power urine has when drunk, or dung when swallowed and when smeared on the parts in the mouth. He recounts this also for each of the other animals, and much else that is hard to procure, as when he mentions the elephant or the Nile horse. The beast called the basilisk I have never even seen, and if what is said about it is true, it is dangerous even to come near this animal. Certain others too wrote things similar to Xenocrates about animals, and from them Xenocrates himself copied out most of what he wrote. For whence would he have had the means to make trial himself of so many and such things? Our own former king Attalus, at any rate, appears to write of fewer, although he was most ambitious in the testing of such things. Someone who praised the treatise of Ateuristus on the same subject also gave it to me to go through. As it seems to me, it had been composed without any personal autopsy on the part of the man who wrote it. I, then, shall mention neither basilisks nor elephants nor Nile horses, nor anything else of which I myself have no experience. As for the so-called philtres, attraction-charms, dream-senders and hate-charms, I deliberately use their own very names for them. I would not have mentioned these in writing at all, not even if I had sufficient experience of them. In the same way I do not mention the deadly drugs, or those which they themselves call 'affection-producing'. Some of these are even ridiculous, such as binding one's legal opponents so that they cannot utter anything in court, or causing a pregnant woman to miscarry, or making her never conceive, and other such things. Most of these are, even by the test of experience, impossible. Some, even if possible, are at any rate harmful to the life of men, so that I, by the gods, wonder with what intention certain people came to write them. Things that bring disrepute even to the living once they are known, how did they hope these would bring them good repute after death? Now if they had been kings and had made trial of these things on men condemned to death, they would have done nothing terrible. But they were private persons who had no such authority in their whole life, and yet they came to write these things. So it is one of two things. Either they write, without having made trial themselves, about what they do not know. Or, if they did make trial, they are the most impious of all men, having given deadly drugs for the sake of trial to those who had done no wrong, and sometimes even to fine and good men. Someone, at any rate, saw two physicians standing by the dealers in petty wares and brought them honey, supposedly to sell it. They tasted it and discussed the price, and as they offered little, he hurriedly went off [ἐχωρήθη], and neither of the physicians survived. All such things, then, deserve to be hated in those who wrote them no less than in those who did them, indeed even more. For it is a lesser wrong to do some evil alone than to do it along with many [ὅσῳ ... μετὰ πολλῶν]. The experience of evil precepts died together with the one who did the deed. But the experience of all those who wrote is immortal, furnishing the wicked with weapons of malefaction. Let us, then, now speak about the things useful to men, of which we have experience.

    But since private persons, having had no such authority in their whole life, came to write them, one of two things follows: either, not having made trial themselves, they write about what they do not know, or, if they did make trial, they are the most impious of all men, having given destructive drugs for the sake of trial to those who had done no wrong, and sometimes even to fine and good men.

    ἐδείχθη δὲ καὶ ὡς ἡ καθόλου δύναμις ἐκ πείρας μιᾶς ἐνδεικτικῶς εὑρίσκοιτο, καὶ οὐ τῆς τυχούσης γε πείρας, ἀλλὰ μετὰ τῶν εἰρημένων διορισμῶν γιγνομένης· εὑρεθείσης δ’ ἅπαξ τῆς καθόλου δυνάμεως οὐδεμιᾶς ἔτι πείρας εἶναι χρείαν εἴς γε τὰς κατὰ μέρος ἐνεργείας, ὅτι μὴ πρὸς βεβαίωσιν μόνην ὧν ὁ λόγος εὗρεν.
    Some become cleansing, others repelling, others drawing, others softening, others caustic, others putrefying, others eschar-forming.     It was also shown that the universal power would be discovered indicatively from a single trial. This is not just any trial, but one carried out with the distinctions that were stated.

    In the book before this one, the ninth of the whole treatise, we went through the material concerning earthy and stony bodies. It remains for us still to go through the material concerning animals.

    After that there still remains what is generated in the sea and lakes, or generally in water, things which are neither plants nor earth nor stone nor animal. Such things are altogether very few, and they will be written of last, after we have gone through what concerns the bodies of animals.

    So now too the instruction about power will concern not only the solid parts in animals but also what is contained in them: phlegm, bile, blood, urine, dung and the like. In what was said earlier, not many of the particular items in the materia medica written by the physicians remained unknown to me.
